Equations in Burnside groups
Rémi Coulon, Zlil Sela

Abstract
This article is a first step in the study of equations in periodic groups. As an application, we study the structure of periodic quotients of hyperbolic groups. We investigate for instance the Hopf and co-Hopf properties, the isomorphism problem, the existence of free splittings, etc. We also consider the automorphism group of such periodic groups.



Keywords.
equations in groups; Burnside groups and periodic groups; geometric group theory; small cancellation theory; hyperbolic geometry.

MSC.

20F70, 20F50, 20F65, 20F67, 20F06, 20F10

1
Introduction

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
A group
\Gamma
is
periodic with exponent
n
(or simply
n
-periodic
) if it satisfies the law
x^{n}=1
, i.e.
\gamma^{n}=1
, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
The
Burnside variety of exponent
n
, denoted by
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, is the class of all
n
-periodic groups.
Its study was initiated by a question of Burnside who asked whether every finitely generated group in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is finite or not.
The first breakthrough was achieved by Novikov and Adian
[
41
]
.
They prove that if
n
is a sufficiently large odd exponent, then the free Burnside group of rank
r\geqslant 2
, defined by

\mathbf{B}_{r}(n)=\mathchoice{\left\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\mid x^{n}=1,\ \forall x\right\rangle}{\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\mid x^{n}=1,\ \forall x\rangle}{\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\mid x^{n}=1,\ \forall x\rangle}{\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\mid x^{n}=1,\ \forall x\rangle},

is infinite.
See also Ol’shanskiĭ
[
42
]
or Delzant-Gromov
[
19
]
for an alternative proof.
Free Burnside groups of sufficiently large even exponents are also infinite
[
29
,
37
,
15
]
.
Nevertheless the structure of
\mathbf{B}_{r}(n)
in this case is much more intricate.
In this article we restrict our study to
odd
exponents.
It turns out that
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is a very rich class of groups
[
2
,
55
,
38
,
16
]
.
In particular, every non-elementary, hyperbolic group admits infinite periodic quotients
[
30
,
15
]
.

This article is an initial step in the study of the first order logic of periodic groups.
We focus here on equations in periodic groups.
Let
F
be the free group generated by
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
.
An
equation
is an element of
w\in F
.
Given a tuple
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{r})
of elements in a group
\Gamma
, we write
w(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{r})
for the element of
\Gamma
obtained by replacing in
w
each
x_{i}
by
\gamma_{i}
.
Such a tuple is the
solution
to a set of equations
W\subset F
, if
w(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{r})=1
, for every
w\in W
.
The set of solutions to
W
in
\Gamma
is in bijection with the set
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)
of all homomorphisms
G\to\Gamma
, where
G
is the group defined by the presentation

G=\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\mid W\right\rangle}{\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\mid W\rangle}{\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\mid W\rangle}{\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\mid W\rangle}.

For every integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by
\Gamma^{n}
the (normal) subgroup of
\Gamma
generated by the
n
-th power of all its elements, so that the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
belongs to
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
We call it the
n
-periodic quotient
of
\Gamma
.
If
W\subset F
is a set of equations, then every solution of
W
in
\Gamma
gives rise to a solution in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Said differently, the projection
\pi\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
induces a map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)&\to&{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})\\
\varphi&\mapsto&\pi\circ\varphi\end{array}.

(1)

We would like to understand under which conditions the solutions in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
of a set of equations
W
all come from solutions in
\Gamma
.
Reformulated in terms of homomorphisms, our problem takes the following form.

Question 1.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Does there exist a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}

lifts
?
That is, can we find a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma
, such that
\varphi=\pi\circ\tilde{\varphi}
, where
\pi\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
stands for the canonical projection?

Remark
.

Our approach is asymptotic in the exponent
n
.
Indeed, we could have fixed
n
and study all possible equations in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Instead, we fix once and for all a set of equations, and investigate its solutions in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
for all sufficiently large (odd) exponents
n
.
In particular, the critical exponent
N
in
Question 1.1
does depend on the group
G
.

Question 1.1
has various answers depending on the group
G
.
Let us describe first some obstructions to lifting morphisms.

Example 1.1

(Torsion abelian groups)
.

Consider the group

G=\mathchoice{\left\langle x\mid x^{3}=1\right\rangle}{\langle x\mid x^{3}=1\rangle}{\langle x\mid x^{3}=1\rangle}{\langle x\mid x^{3}=1\rangle}.

Let
r,n\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Fix an element
\gamma\in\mathbf{B}_{r}(3n)
of order exactly
3n
.
The map
\varphi\colon G\to\mathbf{B}_{r}(3n)
sending
x
to
\gamma^{n}
does not lift to a morphism
G\to\mathbf{F}_{r}
.
This obstruction can be eventually fixed by restricting ourselves to periodic groups with (sufficiently large) prime exponents.

Example 1.2

(Roots)
.

Consider the group

G=\left<x_{1},x_{2},y\mid x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}=y^{2}\right>.

Let
n=2p-1
be an odd integer.
We denote by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
the standard generators of
\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
and define an epimorphism
\varphi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
by

\varphi(x_{1})=a_{1},\quad\varphi(x_{2})=a_{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi(y)=\left(a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}\right)^{p}.

It follows from a result of Lyndon that the image of any morphism
G\to\mathbf{F}_{2}
is abelian
[
36
]
.
Hence, no matter how large
n
is, the morphism
\varphi
does not lift to a morphism
G\to\mathbf{F}_{2}
.

The problem in the latter example comes from the fact that it is possible to extract roots in a periodic group.
This can be formalized as follows.
Let
p\in\mathbf{N}
.
Let
H
be a finitely generated group and
h\in H
.
Consider the group

G=\mathchoice{\left\langle H,y\mid y^{p}=h\right\rangle}{\langle H,y\mid y^{p}=h\rangle}{\langle H,y\mid y^{p}=h\rangle}{\langle H,y\mid y^{p}=h\rangle}.

Assume now that
n
is an integer co-prime with
p
and
\Gamma
an
n
-periodic group.
Then every morphism
H\to\Gamma
uniquely extends to a morphism
G\to\Gamma
(see
Proposition 5.3
).

The previous example motivates the following definition.
A
root splitting
of a group
G
is a decomposition as a non-trivial amalgamated product
G=A\ast_{C}B
, where
B
is an abelian group and
C
a finite index subgroup of
B
.
Such a splitting is
non-essential
if
C
is a free factor of
A
and abstractly isomorphic to
B
.
The general philosophy of our work is that every time we stay away from abelian groups with torsion as well as root splittings then
Question 1.1
is likely to have a positive answer.
For instance we have the following statement.

Theorem 1.3

(see
Corollary 8.16
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Let
G
be a finitely generated group, none of whose quotients admit an essential root splitting.
There exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, for every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
whose image is not abelian, there is a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi
.

Remark
.

Examples of groups
G
satisfying the assumptions of
Theorem 1.3
are groups with Serre’s property (FA)
[
53
]
or free products of perfect groups with Serre’s property (FA).

Recall that
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)
can be entirely parametrized in terms of Makanin-Razborov diagrams
[
52
]
.
Hence in this case, we get for free a description of
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
.

Remark 1.4
.

In general, the map
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)\to{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
given in (
1
) is not one-to-one.
The reason is the following.
The subgroup
\Gamma^{n}
is characteristic in
\Gamma
.
Hence the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
induces a morphism
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
.
The latter may not be one-to-one (for instance if
\Gamma
is a surface group, then any Dehn twist induces a finite order automorphism of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
).
Consequently if
\varphi
belongs to
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)
and
\theta
is an element in the kernel of
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
, then
\varphi
and
\theta\circ\varphi
have the same image in
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
.
It follows that the lift
\tilde{\varphi}
provided by
Theorem 1.3
is not necessarily unique.

Observe also that
Theorem 1.3
does not quite say that the map
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)\to{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
is onto (provided
G
satisfies the required hypothesis).
Indeed we excluded morphisms with an abelian image to avoid the pathology such as the one described in
Example 1.1
.

Using variations of
Theorem 1.3
, we also explore topics such as free splittings in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, the Hopf and co-Hopf properties, the isomorphism problem, automorphism groups, etc.
Before detailing our general strategy let us survey these applications.

Free splitting in the Burnside variety.

The Burnside variety
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
comes with a notion of free product.
It is characterized by the universal property below.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
There exist a group
G\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and two monomorphisms
\iota_{1}\colon A_{1}\to G
and
\iota_{2}\colon A_{2}\to G
with the following property.
For every group
H\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, for every morphisms
\varphi_{1}\colon A_{1}\to H
and
\varphi_{2}\colon A_{2}\to H
, there is a unique morphism
\varphi\colon G\to H
such that the following diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\iota_{1}
\iota_{2}
\varphi_{1}
\varphi_{2}
\varphi

Proof.

It suffices to take for
G
the
n
-periodic quotient of the (regular) free product
A_{1}\freep A_{2}
.
Note that
G
maps onto
A_{1}\times A_{2}
.
Hence the morphism
\iota_{i}\colon A_{i}\to G
is one-to-one.
∎

It follows from
Proposition 1.5
that
G
is unique up to isomorphisms.
We call
G
the
n
-periodic free product
(or simply the
free product
if there is no ambiguity) of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
and denote it by
A_{1}\ast^{n}A_{2}
.
It turns out that the free product in the Burnside variety is also exact, associative and commutative, provided
n
is a sufficiently large odd exponent
[
1
]
.
We say that a group
G\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is
freely decomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, if it is isomorphic to
A\ast^{n}B
for some non trivial groups
A,B\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Theorem 1.6

(see
Theorem 9.2
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
If
\Gamma
has no essential root splitting, then the following are equivalent.

1.

The group
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable.

2.

For every
N\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists an odd integer
n\geqslant N
, such that the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

3.

There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd exponent
n\geqslant N
, the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Example 1.7
.

The previous statement fails if
\Gamma
admits a root-splitting.
Indeed let
p\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
and define a group
\Gamma
by the following presentation:

\Gamma=\left<a,b,c\middle|c^{p}=[a,b]\right>.

It is a freely indecomposable, torsion-free, hyperbolic group. We claim that for every odd integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
which is co-prime with
p
, the
n
-periodic quotient of
\Gamma
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
.
According to Bezout’s identity, we can find
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
\alpha p-\beta n=1
.
In
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
the relation
c^{p}=[a,b]
is equivalent to

c=cc^{\beta n}=c^{\alpha p}=[a,b]^{\alpha}.

It follows that the generator
c
and the relation
c^{p}=[a,b]
can be removed from the presentation of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, whence our claim.
Consequently
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
splits as a free product in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Isomorphism problem between periodic groups.

Theorem 1.6
states that the free splittings of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
can be read from the free splittings of
\Gamma
.
The next result has a similar flavor.
Roughly speaking, it says that if
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable, then the isomorphism type of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
can be read from the one of
\Gamma
.

Theorem 1.8

(see
Corollary 9.7
)
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two torsion-free, hyperbolic groups with no essential root splitting.
There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that the following are equivalent.

1.

\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are isomorphic.

2.

There exists an odd integer
n\geqslant N
, such that
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic.

3.

For every integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, the groups
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic.

Example 1.9
.

One more time, the assumption regarding the root splittings is crucial.
Consider indeed the groups

\Gamma_{2}=\left<a,b,c\middle|c^{2}=[a,b]\right>\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma_{3}=\left<a,b,c\middle|c^{3}=[a,b]\right>

Both are torsion-free, freely indecomposable, non-elementary hyperbolic groups.
The groups
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{3}
are not isomorphic (the torsion parts of their abelianizations are distinct).
However, we explained in
Example 1.7
that for every exponent
n
that is coprime with
6
, the groups
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{3}/\Gamma_{3}^{n}
are both isomorphic to the free Burnside group
\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
.

Recall that the isomorphism problem is solvable among hyperbolic groups, see Sela
[
48
]
, Dahmani-Groves
[
17
]
and Dahmani-Guirardel
[
18
]
.
It follows from the previous statement, that given two torsion-free, freely indecomposable, hyperbolic groups with no essential root splitting, there is an algorithm that can decide whether their periodic quotients (of sufficiently large exponent) are isomorphic.

Automorphism groups of periodic groups.

Let
\Gamma
be a group and
n
an integer.
The subgroup
\Gamma^{n}\subset\Gamma
is characteristic.
Hence the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
induces a homomorphism
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
at the level of automorphisms.
In general the latter map is neither one-to-one nor onto
[
10
]
.
If
\Gamma
is a free group, it is even not known whether the automorphism group
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
or the outer automorphism group
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Out}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
are finitely generated.
Nevertheless these problems can be solved when
\Gamma
is a suitable freely indecomposable, hyperbolic group.

Theorem 1.10

(see
Corollary 9.5
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, freely indecomposable, hyperbolic group with no essential root splitting.
There exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
is onto.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
and
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Out}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
are finitely generated.

Remark
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed compact hyperbolic surface and
\Gamma
its fundamental group.
We denote by
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma)
its mapping class group, that is the set of homeomorphisms of
\Sigma
up to isotopy (we do not require the homeomorphisms to be orientation-preserving).
The previous result has the following consequence.
If
n
is a sufficiently large odd exponent, then the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
induces an
epimorphism

\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma)\twoheadrightarrow\mathchoice{\operatorname{Out}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
.
We suspect that the kernel of this map is exactly the normal subgroup generated by the
n
-th power of every Dehn twist.

Example 1.11
.

Example 1.7
is again an example where
Theorem 1.10
fails without the root splitting assumption.
Indeed, if
n
is an odd exponent, then
n
-periodic quotient of

\Gamma=\left<a,b,c\middle|c^{2}=[a,b]\right>.

is the free Burnside group
\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
generated by (the images of)
a
and
b
.
Consider now the automorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbf{B}_{2}(n)
given by
a\mapsto a^{2}
and
b\mapsto b
.
Assume that
\varphi
lifts to an automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\Gamma
.
The abelianization of
\Gamma
is
A=B\times T
where
B=\mathbf{Z}^{2}
( respectively
T=\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
) is generated by (the images) of
a
and
b
( respectively
c
).
Moreover
A
maps onto its torsion-free part
B\cong A/T
.
Note that
\tilde{\varphi}
induces an automorphism of
B
, that we denote
\psi
.
By looking at the
n
-periodic quotient of
B
, which is also the abelianization of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, i.e.
(\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z})^{2}
, we observe that
\psi
can be represented by a matrix of the form

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}2&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right]\mod\lx@nobreakspace n.

However such a matrix is not invertible provided
n>3
, which contradicts the fact that
\psi
is an automorphism.

Hopf/co-Hopf property.

A group
G
is
Hopfian
if every surjective homomorphism from
G
to itself is actually an isomorphism.
Hyperbolic groups are Hopfian, see Sela
[
50
]
and Weidmann-Reinfeldt
[
58
]
.
We prove that certain periodic quotients of hyperbolic groups are also Hopfian.

Theorem 1.12

(see
Theorem 9.1
)
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is a torsion-free, hyperbolic group, none of whose quotient admits an abelian splitting.
Then there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the periodic quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is Hopfian.

To the best of our knowledge it is still an open question whether
free
Burnside groups of sufficiently large exponents are Hopfian.
It was proved by Ivanov and Storozhev that there exists a group variety whose free elements are not necessarily Hopfian
[
31
]
.

Dually, a group
G
is
co-Hopfian
if every injective homomorphism from
G
to itself is an isomorphism.
A hyperbolic group is co-Hopfian as soon as it does not split as an amalgamated product or an HNN-extension over a finite group, see Sela
[
50
]
and Moioli
[
39
]
.
We obtain here an analogue statement for periodic quotients of torsion-free, hyperbolic groups.

Theorem 1.13

(see
Corollary 9.5
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group with no essential root splitting.
If
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable, then there exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is co-Hopfian.

Remark
.

The converse statement certainly holds true, i.e. if
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is co-Hopfian (for a sufficiently large odd exponent
n
) then
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable.
Since the article is already long, we did not include a proof of this fact.
The strategy would be the following.
Assume that
\Gamma
splits as a free product
\Gamma=A\ast B
.
Let
a\in A\setminus\{1\}
and
b\in B\setminus\{1\}
.
It is known that the morphism
\varphi\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma
fixing
A
and conjugating
B
by
\gamma=ba
is one-to-one by not onto.
Using a variation of
[
14
]
in the context of free products, one can certainly prove that for sufficiently large odd exponents
n
, the map
\varphi
induces a one-to-one endomorphism of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
that is not onto.

In particular if
\Gamma
is one of the group with an essential root splitting studied in
Example 1.7
, then
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
may not be co-Hopfian.

Toward equational noetherianity.

A group
H
is
equationally noetherian
if for every finitely generated free group
F
, for every epimorphism
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
, there is a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
with the following property: every morphism
\varphi\colon F\to H
whose kernel contains
W
factors through
\pi
.
Hyperbolic groups are equationally noetherian, see Sela
[
52
]
and Weidmann-Reinfeldt
[
58
]
.
The definition of equational noetherianity can be extended to a class of groups as in Groves-Hull
[
24
]
.
As before we adopt an asymptotic point of view.

Definition 1.14
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group.
We say that the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
are
asymptotically equationally noetherian
if for every finitely generated free group
F
, for every epimorphism
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
, there exist a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
and a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
with the following property.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an (odd) integer and
\varphi\colon F\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
be a morphism.
If
W\subset\ker\varphi
, then
\varphi
factors through
\pi
.

We are not able yet to prove that periodic quotients of torsion-free, hyperbolic groups are asymptotically equationally noetherian.
Nevertheless we made a first step in this direction.

Theorem 1.15

(see
Corollary 8.17
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Let
F
be a finitely generated free group and
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
an epimorphism where no quotient of
G
admits an essential root splitting.
There exist a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
and a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
with the following property.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer and
\varphi\colon F\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
be a morphism.
If
W\subset\ker\varphi
, then
\varphi
factors through
\pi
.

Remark
.

For some of the above statements (Theorems
1.6
,
1.8
,
1.10
, and
1.13
) we illustrated with examples that it is crucial to assume that the group
\Gamma
has no essential root splitting.
We refer the reader to
Section 9.2
where we address this issue and provide more general results.
The underlying idea is that the hypothesis that
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable without root splitting can be replaced by asking that the periodic quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, see
Theorem 9.11
.
This only works for suitable exponents though.

Strategy.

If
\Gamma
is a torsion-free hyperbolic group, the set of homomorphisms
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)
has been described by Sela in terms of Makanin-Razborov diagram
[
52
]
.
In our study of
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
we borrow Sela’s tools such as limit groups, actions on
\mathbf{R}
-trees, shortening arguments, etc.
This may sound odd at first.
Periodic groups are indeed very far from being hyperbolic: any action by isometries of a periodic group on a Gromov hyperbolic space is either elliptic or parabolic.
The “trick” is to replace
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
by some hyperbolic group approximating it.
Let us briefly detail this idea.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}
be a large odd exponent.
All known strategies for studying
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
start in the same way: one produces by induction an
approximation sequence
of non-elementary, hyperbolic groups

\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{2}\dots\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}\twoheadrightarrow\dots

(2)

whose direct limit is exactly
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
At each step
\Gamma_{j+1}
is obtained from
\Gamma_{j}
by adding new relations of the form
\gamma^{n}=1
, where
\gamma
runs over a set of “small” loxodromic elements of
\Gamma_{j}
.

Fix now a finitely presented group
G=\mathchoice{\left\langle U|R\right\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}
.
Any morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
factors through some
\Gamma_{j}
, that is there is morphism
\psi\colon G\to\Gamma_{j}
such that
\varphi=\pi_{j}\circ\psi
where
\pi_{j}\colon\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
stands for the natural projection.
Hence it suffices to understand the morphisms from
G
to
\Gamma_{j}
.
However the works of Champetier
[
7
]
and Osin
[
44
]
show that it is not possible to give a useful “uniform” description for the set of morphisms from
G
to any hyperbolic group.
Therefore we need to use the fact that the groups
\Gamma_{j}
belong to a very specific subclass of hyperbolic groups, which we describe now.

Following Delzant-Gromov
[
19
]
we attach to each group
\Gamma_{j}
a hyperbolic space
X_{j}
on which it acts by isometries.
This space is obtained by means of geometric small cancellation theory (see
Section A.1
).
Its geometry is “finer” than the one of the Cayley graph of
\Gamma_{j}
.
In particular, we have a uniform control (i.e. independent of the exponent
n
and the approximation step
j
) on its hyperbolicity constant, as well as on a weak form of acylindricity.
In addition, the projection
\Gamma_{j}
is one-to-one when restricted to balls of radius
r(n)
(for the metric on
\Gamma_{j}
induced by the one on
X_{j}
) where
r(n)
only depends on
n
and diverges to infinity when
n
tends to infinity.
All the needed properties of the spaces
X_{j}
are wrapped up in a preferred class of group actions defined in
Section 3.1
.
See in particular
Theorem 3.11
.
We now use
X_{j}
to measure the energy of the lift
\psi\colon G\to\Gamma_{j}
as follows (see
Definition 3.6
)

\lambda_{\infty}(\psi,U)=\inf_{x\in X_{j}}\max_{u\in U}\mathchoice{\left|\psi(u)x-x\right|}{|\psi(u)x-x|}{|\psi(u)x-x|}{|\psi(u)x-x|}.

This is the place where our asymptotic approach jumps in.
Note indeed that all of our statements involve a critical exponent
N
that depends on the group
G
.
Their proofs are usually by contradiction (see for instance Propositions
8.3
and
8.5
).
Assuming that a statement is false, we get a sequence of counter-examples, i.e. morphisms
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
that fail our theorem, where
n_{k}
is a sequence of odd integers diverging to infinity.
For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we fix a lift
\psi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
of
\varphi_{k}
, where
\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
is an approximation of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
as above.
We choose it so that the approximation step
j_{k}
is minimal.
We distinguish then two cases.

—

Suppose first that the energy
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
is bounded.
The projection between the approximation groups of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
are injective on larger and larger balls when
k
tends to infinity.
We use this fact to prove that the minimal lift
\psi_{k}
is actually a lift from
G
to
\Gamma_{0,k}=\Gamma
(see
Proposition 3.14
).
Hence we are back to the standard situation of understanding
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma)
for the
fixed
hyperbolic group
\Gamma
.

—

Suppose now that (up to passing to a subsequence)
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
diverges to infinity.
Since the hyperbolicity constant of the spaces
X_{j_{k},k}
(on which
\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
acts) is uniformly bounded, the sequence of rescaled spaces

\frac{1}{\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)}X_{j_{k},k}

converges to an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T
endowed with an action without global fixed point of the limit group
L
associated the sequence of morphisms
(\psi_{k})
(
Section 7.1
).
Our control of the action of
\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
on
X_{j_{k},k}
is enough to control the one of
L
on
T
(Sections
7.2
and
7.3
).
In particular, if
L
is freely indecomposable, we are back on track to exploit all the existing tools to analyze the action of a group on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree.
We first prove that the action of
L
on
T
decomposes as a graph of actions whose components are either simplicial, axial or of Seifert type (
Section 7.4
).
It provides in particular a graph of groups decomposition of
L
whose edge groups are abelian (see
Theorem 7.44
).
Then, we shorten (when possible) the morphisms
\psi_{k}
using modular automorphisms of this graph of groups decomposition (
Section 7.5
).
This part of the argument involves a new kind of component in the decomposition of
T
that is handled in
Section 7.5.2
.

In the latter case, we may face an obstruction to perform the shortening argument.
As we sketched, the action of
L
on the
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T
provides a graph of groups decomposition of
L
whose modular group can be used to shorten the morphism
\psi_{k}
.
If this decomposition corresponds to a root splitting, then there may be no non-inner modular automorphism to perform the shortening.

For the moment we solve this problem, by imposing conditions on the group
G
that will ensure that the limit group
L
obtained via this process has no root splitting.
The existence of root splittings is a major difficulty to completely describe the solutions of an arbitrary system of equations.

Remark
.

In
[
24
]
Groves and Hull study equational noetherianity for a
family
of groups
\mathcal{G}
.
This is not unrelated to our approach as they need to understand morphisms
G\to\Gamma
where
\Gamma
can vary in
\mathcal{G}
.
Nevertheless their work does not apply in our context.
Indeed Groves and Hull focus on a class of
uniformly
acylindrically hyperbolic groups.
As the exponent
n
varies, it turns out that the action of the group
\Gamma_{j}
(approximating
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
) on the space
X_{j}
is not uniformly acylindrical.
Indeed its injectivity radius converges to zero as
n
tends to infinity.

Outline of the article.

The first five sections review various tools needed for our study.

Section 2
deals with hyperbolic geometry.

Section 3
recollects and formalizes all the properties of the hyperbolic groups approximating the periodic quotients
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
(see in particular
Theorem 3.11
whose proof is given in
Appendix A
).

Section 4
focuses on limit groups seen as limits in the compact space of marked groups.

Section 5
reviews the vocabulary associated to splittings and graph of actions.

Section 6
recalls the construction of the JSJ decomposition of freely indecomposable, CSA groups.

The core of the article lies in
Section 7
.
We study there the action of a limit group (over the periodic quotients of a hyperbolic group) on a limit
\mathbf{R}
-tree, and explain under which assumptions the shortening argument can be adapted to this context.
In
Section 8
, we use this analysis to prove several lifting theorems.

Section 9
is devoted to the applications of this work.

Geometric Group Theory
in Fall 2016 and the HIM Program
Logic and Algorithms in Group Theory
in Fall 2018.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Hyperbolic geometry

In this section, we briefly recall the definitions and notations we use to deal with hyperbolic spaces in the sense of Gromov.
Unless mentioned otherwise all the paths we consider are parametrized by arc length.
For brevity, we did not elaborate on the proofs for statements which are standard exercises of hyperbolic geometry.
For more details, we refer the reader to Gromov’s original article
[
22
]
or the numerous literature on the subject, e.g.
[
9
,
21
,
5
,
6
]
.

Four point inequality.

Let
X
be a length space.
For every
x,y\in X
, we write
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}
, or simply
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}
, for the distance between
x
and
y
.
If it exists, we write
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
for a geodesic joining
x
to
y
.
Given
x\in X
and
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we denote by
B(x,r)
the open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
, i.e.

B(x,r)=\mathchoice{\left\{y\in X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}<r\right\}}{\{y\in X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}<r\}}{\{y\in X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}<r\}}{\{y\in X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}<r\}}.

The
punctured ball
of radius
r
centered at
x
is
\mathring{B}(x,r)=B(x,r)\setminus\{x\}
.
The Gromov product of three points
x,y,z\in X
is

\mathchoice{\left\langle x,y\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathchoice{\left|x-z\right|}{|x-z|}{|x-z|}{|x-z|}+\mathchoice{\left|y-z\right|}{|y-z|}{|y-z|}{|y-z|}-\mathchoice{\left|y-z\right|}{|y-z|}{|y-z|}{|y-z|}\right).

Let
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
For the remainder of
Section 2
, we always assume that
X
is
\delta
-hyperbolic, i.e. for every
x,y,z,t\in X
,

\min\left\{\mathchoice{\left\langle x,y\right\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{t}},\mathchoice{\left\langle y,z\right\rangle_{t}}{\langle y,z\rangle_{t}}{\langle y,z\rangle_{t}}{\langle y,z\rangle_{t}}\right\}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle x,z\right\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,z\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,z\rangle_{t}}{\langle x,z\rangle_{t}}+\delta.

(3)

We denote by
\partial X
its boundary at infinity.
The definition of the Gromov product
\mathchoice{\left\langle x,y\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,y\rangle_{z}}
extends to the triple of points where
x,y\in X\cup\partial X
and
z\in X
.

Quasi-geodesics.

Let
\kappa\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
\ell\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
A
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-isometric embedding
is a map
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
between two metric spaces such that for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{1}
,

\kappa^{-1}\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}-\ell\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})\right|}{|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|}{|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|}{|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|}\leqslant\kappa\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}+\ell.

A
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-geodesic
is a
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-isometric embedding
c\colon I\to X
of an interval
I\subset\mathbf{R}
into
X
.
A path is an
L
-local
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-geodesic
if its restriction to any subinterval of length
L
is a
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-geodesic.
If
c\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
is
(\kappa,\ell)
-quasi-geodesic, then there exists a unique point
\xi\in\partial X
such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}c(t)=\xi
.
We view
\xi
as the endpoint at infinity of
c
and write
\xi=c(\infty)
.

Quasi-convex subsets.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
A subset
Y
of
X
is
\alpha
-quasi-convex
if
d(x,Y)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle y,y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}+\alpha
, for every
x\in X
, and
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
.
We denote by
|\ .\ |_{Y}
the length metric on
Y
induced by the restriction of
|\ .\ |_{X}
to
Y
.
We say that
Y
is
strongly quasi-convex
if it is
2\delta
-quasi-convex and for every
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
,

\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|_{Y}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{Y}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{Y}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{Y}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}+8\delta,

(4)

(in particular, it forces
Y
to be connected by rectifiable paths).

We adopt the convention that the diameter of the empty set is zero, whereas the distance from a point to the empty set is infinite.
Let
x
be a point of
X
.
A point
y\in Y
is an
\eta
-projection
of
x
on
Y
if
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}\leqslant d(x,Y)+\eta
.
A
0
-projection is simply called a
projection
.

Isometries.

An isometry
\gamma
of
X
is either
elliptic
(its orbits are bounded)
parabolic
(its orbits admit exactly one accumulation point in
\partial X
) or
loxodromic
(its orbits admit exactly two accumulation points in
\partial X
).
In order to measure the action of
\gamma
on
X
we use the
translation length
and the
stable translation length
respectively defined by

\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}}=\inf_{x\in X}\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathchoice{\left|\gamma^{n}x-x\right|}{|\gamma^{n}x-x|}{|\gamma^{n}x-x|}{|\gamma^{n}x-x|}.

If there is no ambiguity, we will omit the space
X
from the notations.
These lengths are related by

\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}+8\delta.

(5)

In addition,
\gamma
is loxodromic if and only if
\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}>0
.
In such a case, the accumulation points of
\gamma
in
\partial X
are

\gamma^{-}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma^{-n}x\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma^{+}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma^{n}x.

They are the only points of
X\cup\partial X
, fixed by
\gamma
.
We write
U_{\gamma}
for the union of all
L
-local
(1,\delta)
-quasi-geodesic joining
\gamma^{-}
to
\gamma^{+}
with
L>12\delta
.
The
cylinder
of
\gamma
is the set

Y_{\gamma}=\mathchoice{\left\{x\in X\;:\ d(x,U_{\gamma})<20\delta\right\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ d(x,U_{\gamma})<20\delta\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ d(x,U_{\gamma})<20\delta\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ d(x,U_{\gamma})<20\delta\}}.

It is a strongly quasi-convex subset of
X
which can be thought of as the “smallest”
\gamma
-invariant quasi-convex subset
[
15
, Section 2.3]
.
See also
[
12
, Lemma 2.32]
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
U
be a set of isometries of
X
.
The
energy
of
U
is

\lambda(U)=\inf_{x\in X}\max_{\gamma\in U}\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}.

Given
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we define
\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)
as

\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)=\mathchoice{\left\{x\in X\;:\ \forall\gamma\in U,\ \mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant d\right\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ \forall\gamma\in U,\ \mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant d\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ \forall\gamma\in U,\ \mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant d\}}{\{x\in X\;:\ \forall\gamma\in U,\ \mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant d\}}.

(6)

If
d>\max\{\lambda(U),5\delta\}
, then
\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)
is
8\delta
-quasi-convex.
Moreover for every
x\in X\setminus\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)
, we have

\sup_{\gamma\in U}\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\geqslant 2d\left(x,\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)\right)+d-10\delta,

(7)

see
[
15
, Lemma 2.8]
.
Compare also with
[
12
, Proposition 2.24]
.
For simplicity, we write
\operatorname{Fix}(U)
for
\operatorname{Fix}(U,0)
.
If the set
U=\{\gamma\}
is reduced to a single isometry, then
\lambda(U)=\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}
and we simply write
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,d)
for
\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.

—

An elliptic isometry
\gamma
of
X
is
\alpha
-thin at
x\in X
, if for every
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
the set
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,d)
is contained in
B(x,d/2+\alpha)
.
It is
\alpha
-thin
, if it is
\alpha
-thin at some point of
X
.

—

A loxodromic isometry
\gamma
of
X
is
\alpha
-thin
if for every
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, for every
y\in\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,d)
, we have

\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(d-\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|})+\alpha.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Examples of isometries of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbf{H}^{2}
which are not
\alpha
-thin are rotations with a sufficiently small angle and loxodromic isometries with a very sufficiently translation length.

Remark 2.3
.

It follows from the stability of quasi-geodesics that any loxodromic isometry
\gamma
is
\alpha
-thin where

\alpha\leqslant 100\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}}+1\right)\delta.

In our context the thinness provides a way to control the action of an isometry
\gamma
, when we do not have any lower bound for its stable norm
\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}
(see
Section A.2.3
).

The local-to-global statement below is a direct consequence of (
7
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\gamma
be an isometry of
X
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.

1.

Assume that
\gamma
is elliptic.
If
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,6\delta)
is contained in the ball
B(x,\alpha)
for some
x\in X
, then
\gamma
is
\beta
-thin at
x
, where
\beta=\alpha+2\delta
.

2.

Assume that
\gamma
is loxodromic.
If the set
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}{\|\gamma\|}+5\delta)
is contained in the
\alpha
-neighborhood of some
L
-local
(1,\delta)
-quasi-geodesic from
\gamma^{-}
to
\gamma^{+}
with
L>12\delta
, then
\gamma
is
\beta
-thin with
\beta=\alpha+6\delta
.

2.2
Group actions

Let
\Gamma
be a group acting by isometries on a
\delta
-hyperbolic length space
X
.
We denote by
\Lambda(\Gamma)
its
limit set
, i.e. the set of accumulation points in
\partial X
of the
\Gamma
-orbit of a point
x\in X
.
We say that
\Gamma
is
non-elementary
(
for its action on
X
) if
\Lambda(\Gamma)
contains at least three points.

Observe that we do not assume that the action of
\Gamma
on
X
is either proper or co-bounded.
In order to control this action, we use instead three invariants: the injectivity radius, the acylindricity parameter and the
\nu
-invariant.

Definition 2.5

(Injectivity radius)
.

The
injectivity radius
of
\Gamma
on
X
is the quantity

\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma,X\right)=\inf\mathchoice{\left\{\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}\;:\ \gamma\in\Gamma\ \text{loxodromic}\right\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}\;:\ \gamma\in\Gamma\ \text{loxodromic}\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}\;:\ \gamma\in\Gamma\ \text{loxodromic}\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|_{X}^{\infty}}\;:\ \gamma\in\Gamma\ \text{loxodromic}\}}

Definition 2.6
.

Let
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}

The
acylindricity parameter at scale
d
, denoted by
A(\Gamma,X,d)
is defined as

A(\Gamma,X,d)=\sup_{U\subset\Gamma}\operatorname{diam}\left(\operatorname{Fix}(U,d)\right),

where
U
runs over all subsets of
\Gamma
generating a non-elementary subgroup.

Definition 2.7

(
\nu
-invariant)
.

The
\nu
-invariant
\nu(\Gamma,X)
is the smallest integer
m
with the following property.
For every
\gamma,\tau\in\Gamma
with
\tau
loxodromic, if
\gamma,\tau\gamma\tau^{-1},\dots\tau^{m}\gamma\tau^{-m}
generate an elementary subgroup, then so do
\gamma
and
\tau
.

The latter two quantities provide the following analogue of the Margulis lemma for manifolds with pinched negative curvature,
[
15
, Proposition 3.5]
.
See also
[
13
, Proposition 3.44]
.

Proposition 2.8
.

For every
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we have

A(\Gamma,X,d)\leqslant\left[\nu(\Gamma,X)+3\right]d+A(\Gamma,X,400\delta)+24\delta.

This motivates the next definition

Definition 2.9

(Acylindricity)
.

The
(global) acylindricity parameter
, denoted by
A(\Gamma,X)
, is the smallest non-negative number such that for every
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
,

A(\Gamma,X,d)\leqslant\left[\nu(\Gamma,X)+3\right]d+A(\Gamma,X).

Remark
.

The definition of
A(\Gamma,X)
slightly differs from Delzant-Gromov
[
19
]
and Coulon
[
12
,
15
]
.
The advantage of this new definition is that it does not involve the hyperbolicity constant
\delta
.
Nevertheless it plays the exact same role.
In particular,
A(\Gamma,X)
satisfies the following homogeneity: if
\varepsilon X
stands for the space
X
rescaled by a factor
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
, then
A(\Gamma,\varepsilon X)=\varepsilon A(\Gamma,X)
.
Moreover
A(\Gamma,X)\leqslant A(\Gamma,X,400\delta)+24\delta
.

3
Approximation of periodic groups

Our goal is to study morphisms of the form
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, where
\Gamma
is a torsion-free hyperbolic group and
n
a sufficiently large odd exponent.
As we explained in the introduction, one can produce a directed sequence of hyperbolic groups
(\Gamma_{j})
converging to
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Our strategy is to take advantage of this feature.
Indeed if
G
is a finitely presented group, then every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
lifts to a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j}
, provided
j
is large enough.
In this way we reduce our problem to the study of morphisms from
G
to a class of suitable hyperbolic groups.
The purpose of this section is to make precise the kind of negatively curved groups that appear in this context.

3.1
A preferred class of groups.

Definition 3.1
.

A group
G
is
Conjugately Separated Abelian
(
CSA
) if every maximal abelian subgroup of
G
is malnormal.

General settings.

Let
\delta,\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
In this section we consider triples
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
such that

—

X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic length space,

—

\Gamma
is a CSA group acting by isometries on
X
,

—

\mathcal{C}
is
\Gamma
-invariant,
2\rho
-separated subset of
X
(that is
\mathchoice{\left|c-c^{\prime}\right|}{|c-c^{\prime}|}{|c-c^{\prime}|}{|c-c^{\prime}|}\geqslant 2\rho
for every distinct
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
).

Vocabulary 3.1
.

The elements of
\mathcal{C}
are called
apices
or
cone points
.
An element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is
conical
if it fixes a unique cone point.
It is
visible
if it is loxodromic or conical and
elusive
otherwise.
A subgroup
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
is
elusive
if all its elements are elusive. We chose the word “elusive” to stress that fact that, in practice, the action of
\Gamma_{0}
on
X
will not provide much information on the structure of
\Gamma_{0}
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is
\Gamma
-invariant, the collection of visible elements is invariant under conjugation.
Hence so it the collection of elusive subgroups.
The latter is also stable under taking subgroups.

We think of
\mathcal{C}
as a thin-thick decomposition of
X
:
the
thin parts
are the balls
B(c,\rho)
, where
c
runs over
\mathcal{C}
;
the
thick part
, that we denote by
X^{+}
, is

X^{+}=X\setminus\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}B(c,\rho).

Definition 3.2
.

We write
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
for the set of all triples
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
as above which satisfy the following additional properties.

(H1)

\nu(\Gamma,X)=1
and
A(\Gamma,X)\leqslant\delta
.

(H2)

Every elementary subgroup of
\Gamma
is either elusive or cyclic (finite or infinite).

(H3)

Every non-trivial elusive element of
\Gamma
is elliptic and
\delta
-thin.

(H4)

Every element
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
fixing a cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}
is
(\rho+\delta)
-thin at
c
.

(H5)

For every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists
\Theta\in[2\pi,\infty)
and a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
-equivariant cocycle

\theta\colon\mathring{B}(c,\rho)\times\mathring{B}(c,\rho)\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta\mathbf{Z}

such that for every
x,y\in\mathring{B}(c,\rho)
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}-\ell\right|}{|\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}-\ell|}{|\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}-\ell|}{|\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}-\ell|}\leqslant\delta
, where

\operatorname{ch}\ell=\operatorname{ch}\mathchoice{\left|c-x\right|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}\operatorname{ch}\mathchoice{\left|c-y\right|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}-\operatorname{sh}\mathchoice{\left|c-x\right|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}\operatorname{sh}\mathchoice{\left|c-y\right|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}\cos\left(\min\left\{\pi,\tilde{\theta}(x,y)\right\}\right)

(8)

and
\tilde{\theta}(x,y)
is the unique representative of
\theta(x,y)
in
(-\Theta/2,\Theta/2]
.
Moreover,
\theta(\gamma x,x)\lx@nobreakspace\neq 0
, for every
\gamma\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}\setminus\{1\}
, and
x\in\mathring{B}(c,\rho)
,

(H6)

Every loxodromic element of
\Gamma
is
\delta
-thin.

(H7)

For every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, for every
x\in B(c,\rho)
, there exists
y\in X^{+}
such that
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}=\rho-\mathchoice{\left|x-c\right|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}
.

Notations
.

For simplicity, we write
\mathfrak{H}
for the union of all
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
where
\delta
and
\rho
run over
\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
In practice we will consider situations where
\rho
is very large compare to
\delta
.

Remark 3.3
.

Let us comment upon this set of axioms.
Note that we do not require
\Gamma
to act properly on
X
.
All we will need is a weak form of acylindricity captured by
(H1)
.
This assumption is weaker than the usual acylindricity.
Indeed it does not provide any control on the injectivity radius for the action of
\Gamma
on
X
.
Axiom
(H2)
refers to the algebraic structure of elementary subgroups of
\Gamma
.
Axioms
(H3)
-
(H6)
give geometric informations on the action of
\Gamma
.
In particular, it follows from
(H3)
and
(H4)
, that
\Gamma
has no parabolic subgroup.
In Axiom
(H5)
, by cocycle we mean that for every
x,y,z\in\mathring{B}(c,\rho)
we have
\theta(x,z)=\theta(x,y)+\theta(y,z)
.
The exact formula in (
8
) does not really matter.
It provides a uniform control on the action of
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
, i.e. which does not depend on the triple
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
.
It has the following interpretation:
the ball
B(c,\rho)
is seen approximately as a cone of radius
\rho
endowed with a hyperbolic metric whose total angle at the apex is
\Theta
.
The quantity
\theta(x,y)
represents the angle at
c
between
x
and
y
.
With this idea in mind, one recognizes in (
8
) the law of cosines in
\mathbf{H}^{2}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
with
\rho>\delta
.
Let
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
in
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Every non-trivial element in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
is conical.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}\setminus\{1\}
.
Assume that contrary to our claim
\gamma
is elusive.
By
(H3)
, it is
\delta
-thin.
Hence there exists
x\in X
such that
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma)
is contained in the ball
B(x,\delta)
.
Since
\gamma
fixes
c
, we have in particular
\mathchoice{\left|x-c\right|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}\leqslant\delta
.
Let
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{c\}
.
It follows from the triangle inequality that

\mathchoice{\left|c^{\prime}-x\right|}{|c^{\prime}-x|}{|c^{\prime}-x|}{|c^{\prime}-x|}\geqslant\mathchoice{\left|c^{\prime}-c\right|}{|c^{\prime}-c|}{|c^{\prime}-c|}{|c^{\prime}-c|}-\mathchoice{\left|x-c\right|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}\geqslant 2\rho-\delta.

However by assumption,
\rho>\delta
.
Consequently
c^{\prime}
does not belong to
B(x,\delta)
.
Hence
\gamma
cannot fix a cone point distinct from
c
, and therefore is visible, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
with
\rho>\delta
.

Let
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
in
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
.
Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
such that
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]=1
.
If
\gamma_{1}
is visible, then so is
\gamma_{2}
.

Proof.

By assumption
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
generate an elementary subgroup
\Gamma_{0}
of
\Gamma
.
Assume first that
\gamma_{1}
is loxodromic.
According to
(H2)
,
\Gamma_{0}
is cyclic, hence
\gamma_{2}
is loxodromic as well.
Suppose now that
\gamma_{1}
is conical, fixing a cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}
say.
Since
\gamma_{1}
commutes with
\gamma_{2}
, the point
\gamma_{2}c
is also fixed by
\gamma_{1}
.
Hence
\gamma_{2}c=c
.
It follows from
Lemma 3.4
that
\gamma_{2}
is conical.
∎

We sometimes make an abuse of notation and write
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
to designate the set of groups
\Gamma
instead of the set of triples
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
.
One should keep in mind that such a group always comes with a preferred action on a hyperbolic space.
This is useful for defining energies (see below).
The class
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
is stable under taking subgroup in the following sense: if
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
is an element of
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
and
\Gamma_{0}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, then
(\Gamma_{0},X,\mathcal{C})
also belongs to
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
.

Energies.

Let
G
be a group and
U
a finite subset of
G
.
We write
\left|U\right|
for its cardinality.
We are going to investigate morphisms of the form
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma
, for some triple
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}
.
If we need to emphasize the space
\Gamma
is acting on, we write
\varphi\colon G\to(\Gamma,X)
or
\varphi\colon G\to(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})
.
Given
x\in X
, the
L^{1}
- and
L^{\infty}
-
energies of
\varphi
at the point
x
(
with respect to
U
) are defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in U}\mathchoice{\left|\varphi(u)x-x\right|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in U}\mathchoice{\left|\varphi(u)x-x\right|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}.

Definition 3.6

(Energy)
.

Let
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}
.
Let
p\in\{1,\infty\}
.
Given a homomorphism
\varphi\colon G\to(\Gamma,X)
, the
L^{p}
-
energy of
\varphi
(
with respect to
U
) is

\lambda_{p}(\varphi,U)=\inf_{x\in X}\lambda_{p}(\varphi,U,x).

These energies are related as follows:

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U).

(9)

Later in the article, we will chose a point
o\in X
minimizing one of these energies and treat is as a basepoint.
Nevertheless it will be convenient to keep the basepoint in the thick part
X^{+}
of the space
X
.
This motivates the following variation of energy.

Definition 3.7

(Restricted energy)
.

Let
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}
.
Given a homomorphism
\varphi\colon G\to(\Gamma,X)
, the
restricted energy of
\varphi
(
with respect to
U
) is defined by

\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi,U)=\inf_{x\in X^{+}}\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,x).

(Note that
x
runs over the thick part
X^{+}
instead of
X
.)

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
with
\rho>\delta
.
Let
(\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)
.
Let
\varphi\colon G\to(\Gamma,X)
be a homomorphism and
U
a finite subset of
G
.
Assume that the image of
\varphi
is not abelian, then

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)\leqslant 2\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U).

Moreover, for every
\varepsilon>12\delta
, there exists
x\in X
and
x^{+}\in X^{+}
such that

1.

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x)\leqslant\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon
and
\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,x^{+})\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon
,

2.

\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{+}\right|}{|x-x^{+}|}{|x-x^{+}|}{|x-x^{+}|}\leqslant\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The first inequalities
\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)
follow from the definition of the various energies.
Let
x\in X
.
We claim that

2d(x,\mathcal{C})\geqslant 2\rho-\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x).

Indeed for every
u\in U
we have
\mathchoice{\left|\varphi(u)x-x\right|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}{|\varphi(u)x-x|}\leqslant\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x)
.
Hence, if our claim fails, it follows from the triangle inequality that there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}
such that for every
u\in U
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\varphi(u)c-c\right|}{|\varphi(u)c-c|}{|\varphi(u)c-c|}{|\varphi(u)c-c|}<2\rho
.
Consequently the image of
\varphi
is contained in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
.
By
Lemma 3.4
, if
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
is not trivial, then it cannot be elusive.
Axiom
(H2)
forces
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
, and thus the image of
\varphi
, to be abelian.
This contradicts our assumption and completes the proof of our claim.
By
(H7)
, there exists
y\in X^{+}
such that
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}\leqslant\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x)/2
.
It follows then from the triangle inequality that

\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,y)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,x)+2\left|U\right|\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}\leqslant 2\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x).

This inequality holds for every
x\in X
, therefore
\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)\leqslant 2\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)
.

Let us focus now on the second part of the statement.
Let
\varepsilon>12\delta
and
x^{+}\in X^{+}
be a point in the thick part such that
\lambda_{1}(\varphi,U,x^{+})\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon
.
For simplicity we let

Y=\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi(U),\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon).

If
x^{+}
already belongs to
Y
, then we simply take
x=x^{+}
.
Otherwise, we denote by
x
a
\delta
-projection of
x^{+}
on
Y
.
Inequality (
7
) yields

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x^{+})\geqslant 2\mathchoice{\left|x^{+}-x\right|}{|x^{+}-x|}{|x^{+}-x|}{|x^{+}-x|}+\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U).

In particular,

\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{+}\right|}{|x-x^{+}|}{|x-x^{+}|}{|x-x^{+}|}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U,x^{+})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi,U)+\varepsilon.\qed

3.2
Lifting morphisms

Let us now explain how the class of groups
\mathfrak{H}
introduced in the previous section naturally appears in the study of periodic groups.

Bootstrap and approximations.

Recall that the acylindricity parameter, the injectivity radius and the
\nu
-invariant have been defined in
Section 2.2
.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\tau\in(0,1)
.
A
\tau
-bootstrap
is a pair
(\Gamma,X)
where
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic length space for some
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
, endowed with a non-elementary action by isometries of a CSA group
\Gamma
with the following properties.

—

\nu(\Gamma,X)=1
,
A(\Gamma,X)\leqslant\delta
, and
\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma,X\right)\geqslant\tau\delta
.

—

Every elementary subgroup of
\Gamma
which is not elliptic is loxodromic and cyclic.

—

Every element of
\Gamma
(elliptic or loxodromic) is
\delta
-thin.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
If in addition every elliptic element in
\Gamma
has finite order dividing
n
, we say that
(\Gamma,X)
is a
\tau
-bootstrap for the exponent
n
.

Example 3.10
.

For our applications, we have two main examples of bootstraps in mind.

—

\Gamma
is a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group acting on its Cayley graph
X
.

—

\Gamma
is a non-trivial free product of CSA groups with no even torsion, acting on the corresponding Bass-Serre tree
X
.

Let
(\Gamma_{1},X_{1})
and
(\Gamma_{2},X_{2})
be two pairs where
X_{i}
is a metric space and
\Gamma_{i}
a group acting by isometries on
X_{i}
.
A
morphism

(\Gamma_{1},X_{1})\to(\Gamma_{2},X_{2})
is a pair
(\pi,f)
where
\pi
is a homomorphism from
\Gamma_{1}
to
\Gamma_{2}
and
f
a
\pi
-equivariant map from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
(we do not require
f
to be an isometry).
Such a morphism is called an
epimorphism
if
\pi
is onto (we do not require
f
to be onto).
The statement below summarizes all the properties satisfied by our approximation groups.

Theorem 3.11
.

There exist
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and a non-decreasing function
\rho\colon\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
diverging to infinity with the following properties.
For every
\tau\in(0,1)
, there is a critical exponent
N_{\tau}\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N_{\tau}
, the following holds.

Let
(\Gamma,X)
be a
\tau
-bootstrap for the exponent
n
.
The quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is infinite.
Moreover, there exists a sequence of epimorphisms

(\Gamma_{0},X_{0})\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{1},X_{1})\twoheadrightarrow\dots\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})\twoheadrightarrow\dots

with the following properties.

1.

\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
while
X_{0}
is a rescaled version of
X
, that does not depend on
n
.

2.

The direct limit of the sequence
(\Gamma_{j})
is isomorphic to
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Moreover, if every finite subgroup of
\Gamma
is cyclic, then the same holds for
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

3.

For every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, there is a subset
\mathcal{C}_{j}\subset X_{j}
such that
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
belongs to the class
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho(n))
.

4.

Let
j\in\mathbf{N}
.
On the one hand, the map
X_{j}\to X_{j+1}
is
\lambda(n)
-Lipschitz, where
\lambda(n)<1
only depends on
n
, and converges to zero as
n
tends to infinity.
On the other hand, for every
x\in X_{j}
, the projection
\pi_{j}\colon\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}
is one-to-one when restricted to the set

\mathchoice{\left\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \lambda(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\right\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \lambda(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \lambda(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \lambda(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}}.

5.

Let
j\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
F
be the free group generated by a finite set
U
.
Let
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
and
V
be the set of elements of
F
whose length (seen as words over
U
) is at most
\ell
.
Let
\varphi\colon F\to\Gamma_{j}
be a homomorphism whose image does not fix a cone point in
\mathcal{C}_{j}
.
Assume that

\lambda_{\infty}\left(\varphi,U\right)<\frac{\rho(n)}{100\ell}

Then there exists a map
\tilde{\varphi}\colon F\to\Gamma_{j-1}
such that
\varphi=\pi_{j-1}\circ\tilde{\varphi}
and
V\cap\ker{\varphi}=V\cap\ker{\tilde{\varphi}}
.

The proof of
Theorem 3.11
relies on iterated geometric small cancellation theory.
It is postponed in
Appendix A
.

Vocabulary
.

We call any sequence of triples
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
satisfying the conclusion of
Theorem 3.11
an
approximation sequence of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

Remark 3.12
.

—

Being CSA is stable under direct limit, see for instance
[
40
]
.
Hence
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is CSA as well.

—

Assume that
\Gamma
is a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Using an induction on
j
based on
Theorem 3.11

4
one proves the following fact:
for every finite subset
U\subset\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
, there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, we have
U\cap\Gamma^{n}=\emptyset
.
Although not directly stated in this form, this fact was already observed by Ol’shanskiĭ
[
43
]
.

Lifting morphisms.

Let
\tau\in(0,1)
.
We choose an odd integer
n\geqslant N_{\tau}
, where
N_{\tau}
is the critical exponent given by
Theorem 3.11
.
Fix a
\tau
-bootstrap
(X,\Gamma)
for the exponent
n
.
Let
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
be a sequence of approximations of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
For every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
\sigma_{j}
for the natural epimorphism
\sigma_{j}\colon\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Similarly we write
\sigma
for the projection
\sigma\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
G
be a discrete group,
V
a subset of
G
, and
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
a morphism.
An
approximation of
\varphi
relative to
V
is a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j}
for some
j\in\mathbf{N}
such that
\varphi=\sigma_{j}\circ\tilde{\varphi}
and
V\cap\ker\tilde{\varphi}=V\cap\ker\varphi
.
Alternatively, we say that
\tilde{\varphi}

lifts
\varphi
relative to
V
.
Such an approximation is
minimal
if
j
is minimal for the above properties.

Recall that
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is the direct limit of
\Gamma_{j}
.
Hence if
G
is finitely presented and
V
is finite, then any morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
admits a minimal approximation relative to
V
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
G=\mathchoice{\left\langle U|R\right\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}{\langle U|R\rangle}
be finitely presented group.
Let
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
bounded from below by the length of any relation in
R
(seen as words over
U
).
Let
V
be the set of all elements of
G
of length at most
\ell
(for the word metric with respect to
U
).
Let
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
be a morphism whose image is not abelian.
If
\varphi_{j}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j}
is a minimal approximation of
\varphi
relative to
V
, then either
j=0
or

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{j},U)\geqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100\ell}

Proof.

The proof is by contradiction.
Assume indeed that
j\geqslant 1
and

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{j},U)<\frac{\rho(n)}{100\ell}

Note that the image of
\varphi_{j}
does not fix a cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
.
Indeed, otherwise, it would be abelian by Axiom
(H2)
and thus the image of
\varphi
as well.
The presentation of
G
defines an epimorphism
\chi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
, where
F
is the free group generated by
U
.
We denote by
\tilde{V}
the set of all elements of
F
of length at most
\ell
so that
\chi
maps
\tilde{V}
onto
V
.
It follows from our choice of
\ell
, that
R
is contained in
\tilde{V}
.
We apply
Theorem 3.11

5
, to the morphism
\psi_{j}=\varphi_{j}\circ\chi
.
There exists a morphism
\psi_{j-1}\colon F\to\Gamma_{j-1}
such that

1.

\varphi\circ\chi=\sigma_{j-1}\circ\psi_{j-1}
, and

2.

\tilde{V}\cap\ker\psi_{j-1}=\tilde{V}\cap\ker\psi_{j}=\tilde{V}\cap\ker\left(\varphi\circ\chi\right)
.

The last item tells us that
R
lies in the kernel of
\psi_{j-1}
.
Hence
\psi_{j-1}
factors through
\chi
.
The resulting morphism
\varphi_{j-1}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j-1}
is an approximation of
\varphi
relative to
V
, which contradicts the minimality of
j
.
∎

4
The space of marked groups

Our study of
{\rm Hom}(G,\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})
make use of
limit groups
(see
Definition 4.2
).
They can be seen as limit of periodic groups in a compact space of marked groups.
In this section we recall some features of this topological approach.
We follow
[
7
,
8
]
.

4.1
Generalities

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
A
group marked by
G
is a pair
(H,\varphi)
where
H
is a group and
\varphi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H
an epimorphism from
G
onto
H
.
If there is no ambiguity we omit the group
G
and simply say that
(H,\varphi)
is a
marked group
.
Given two groups
(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})
marked by
G
, we say that
(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
is a
cover
of
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})
or
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})
is
quotient
of
(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
and write
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\prec(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
if there exists an epimorphism
\theta\colon H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
such that
\theta\circ\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}
.
Equivalently it means that
\ker\varphi_{1}\subset\ker\varphi_{2}
.
The relation
\prec
defines a pre-order on the set of marked groups.
Two marked groups
(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})
are equivalent, if
(H_{1},\varphi_{1})
is a cover of
(H_{2},\varphi_{2})
and conversely.
Equivalently it means that there exists an isomorphism
\theta\colon H_{1}\to H_{2}
such that
\theta\circ\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}
.

Definition 4.1
.

The
space of groups marked by
G
(or simply the
space of marked groups
) is the set of equivalence classes of groups marked by
G
.
We denote it by
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.

Notations
.

In the remainder of the article we make the following abuse of notation: given a group
(H,\varphi)
marked by
G
, we still denote by
(H,\varphi)
its equivalence class in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.

We endow the space
\mathfrak{G}(G)
with a topology (called the
topology of marked groups
) defined as follows.
Let
(H,\varphi)
be a group marked by
G
.
Let
U
be a finite set of
G
.
We let

\mathfrak{V}_{U}(H,\varphi)=\mathchoice{\left\{(H^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ U\cap\ker\varphi=U\cap\ker\varphi^{\prime}\right\}}{\{(H^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ U\cap\ker\varphi=U\cap\ker\varphi^{\prime}\}}{\{(H^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ U\cap\ker\varphi=U\cap\ker\varphi^{\prime}\}}{\{(H^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ U\cap\ker\varphi=U\cap\ker\varphi^{\prime}\}}.

When
U
runs over all finite subsets of
G
, the collection
\{\mathfrak{V}_{U}(H,\varphi)\}_{U\subset G}
describes a basis of open neighborhoods of
(H,\varphi)
.
The space
\mathfrak{G}(G)
endowed with this topology is metrizable and compact.

Remark
.

Sometimes it is convenient to consider pairs
(H,\varphi)
, where the morphism
\varphi\colon G\to H
is not necessarily onto.
In this situation we make an abuse of notation and write
(H,\varphi)
to mean
(\varphi(G),\varphi)
.
In particular, we say that a sequence
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
of such pairs converges to
(L,\eta)
and write

(L,\eta)=\lim_{k\to\infty}(H_{k},\varphi_{k}),

if
(\varphi_{k}(G),\varphi_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta)
in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
In such a situation
L
is the quotient of
G
by the
stable kernel
of
(\varphi_{k})
, i.e. the normal subgroup

K=\mathchoice{\left\{\gamma\in G\;:\ \varphi_{k}(\gamma)=1,\ \text{for all but finitely many}\ k\in\mathbf{N}\right\}}{\{\gamma\in G\;:\ \varphi_{k}(\gamma)=1,\ \text{for all but finitely many}\ k\in\mathbf{N}\}}{\{\gamma\in G\;:\ \varphi_{k}(\gamma)=1,\ \text{for all but finitely many}\ k\in\mathbf{N}\}}{\{\gamma\in G\;:\ \varphi_{k}(\gamma)=1,\ \text{for all but finitely many}\ k\in\mathbf{N}\}}.

(10)

By construction, the pre-order
\prec
induces an order on
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
It is compatible with the topology in the sense that the set

\mathchoice{\left\{\left((H_{1},\varphi_{1}),(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\right)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\times\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ (H_{1},\varphi_{1})\lx@nobreakspace\prec(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\right\}}{\{\left((H_{1},\varphi_{1}),(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\right)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\times\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ (H_{1},\varphi_{1})\lx@nobreakspace\prec(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\}}{\{\left((H_{1},\varphi_{1}),(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\right)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\times\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ (H_{1},\varphi_{1})\lx@nobreakspace\prec(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\}}{\{\left((H_{1},\varphi_{1}),(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\right)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)\times\mathfrak{G}(G)\;:\ (H_{1},\varphi_{1})\lx@nobreakspace\prec(H_{2},\varphi_{2})\}}

is closed for the product topology.

Changing the marker.

Given an epimorphism
\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
, we define a map
\pi_{\ast}\colon\mathfrak{G}(G^{\prime})\to\mathfrak{G}(G)
by sending
(H,\varphi)
to
(H,\varphi\circ\pi)
.
This is an order-preserving homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{G}(G^{\prime})
onto its image.
In addition, if the kernel of
\pi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
is the normal closure of a
finite
subset of
G
, then the image of
\pi_{\ast}
is an open subset of
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.

4.2
Limit groups.

A
filtration
of groups is a sequence
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
of collections of groups that is non-increasing for the inclusion (i.e.
\mathfrak{F}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{k^{\prime}}
for every
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
).
Given such a filtration we write
\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
for the groups
(H,\varphi)
marked by
G
such that
H
belongs to
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
be a filtration of groups. A
limit group over
\mathfrak{F}
is a marked group in the set

\bigcap_{k\in\mathbf{N}}\overline{\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)}.

where
\overline{\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)}
stands for the closure of
\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.

Vocabulary
.

A sequence
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
of marked groups is an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence
if
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
belongs to
\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Since
(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
is non-increasing for the inclusion, any subsequence of an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence is still an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence.
Concretely,
(L,\eta)
is a limit group over
\mathfrak{F}
, if and only if it is the limit of an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence.

We will be interested in the following particular situations.

—

A
limit group over a group
\Gamma
is a limit group over the constant filtration that consists of all subgroups of
\Gamma
.

—

Let
\Gamma
be a group.
Given
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
for the union over all
n\geqslant k
of all subgroups of the periodic quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
A
limit group over the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
is a limit group over the filtration
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
.

—

Let
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Given
k\in\mathbf{N}
, denote by
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
, the collection

\mathfrak{F}_{k}=\bigcup_{\rho\geqslant k}\mathchoice{\left\{\Gamma\;:\ (\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)\right\}}{\{\Gamma\;:\ (\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)\}}{\{\Gamma\;:\ (\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)\}}{\{\Gamma\;:\ (\Gamma,X,\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho)\}}.

A
limit group over
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
is a limit group over the filtration
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
.

In the latter case, we borrow the following terminology from Groves and Hull
[
24
]
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
U
be a finite generating set of
G
.
A limit group
(L,\eta)
over
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
is
divergent
if there exist a sequence
(\rho_{k})
diverging to infinity, a sequence of marked groups
(\Gamma_{k},\varphi_{k})
, where
(\Gamma_{k},X_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho_{k})
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, which converges to
(L,\eta)
and such that the energy
\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)
measured in
X_{k}
diverges to infinity.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that the definition does not depend on the generating set
U
.
Because of (
9
), we could also have replaced the
L^{\infty}
-energy, by the
L^{1}
-energy.
If
L
is not abelian, it is also equivalent to the fact that the restricted energy
\lambda_{1}^{+}(\varphi_{k},U)
diverges to infinity (
Lemma 3.8
).

We now prove a few statements which are not necessarily needed for our study, but should help to understand the relations between these various types of limit groups.

Proposition 4.6
can also be seen as a first warm up for the lifting statements proved in
Section 8
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
If
(L,\eta)
is a limit group over
\Gamma
then it is also a limit group over the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma
be a sequence of morphisms so that
(\Gamma,\varphi_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta)
.
Let
(U_{k})
be a non-decreasing exhaustion of
G
by finite subsets.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists an odd integer
n_{k}\geqslant k
such that the projection
\pi_{k}\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
is one-to-one when restricted to
\varphi_{k}(U_{k})
(
Remark 3.12
).
It follows directly that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\pi_{k}\circ\varphi_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta)
as well.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
There exists
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
with the following property.
Let
G
be a finitely presented group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group over the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
.
Then one of the following holds

—

L
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
,

—

L
is an abelian group.

—

(L,\eta)
is a divergent limit group over
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
,

Proof.

Let
\delta\in\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+}
and
\rho\colon\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
be the data given by
Theorem 3.11
.
We assume that
L
is not abelian.
There exists a sequence of morphisms
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
where
(n_{k})
is a sequence of odd integers diverging to infinity and such that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta)
.
In particular,
\rho(n_{k})
diverges to infinity.
We choose a non-decreasing sequence of integers
(\ell_{k})
diverging to infinity and such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\rho(n_{k})}{\ell_{k}}=\infty.

Let
U
be a finite generating set of
G
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by
U_{k}
the ball of radius
\ell_{k}
in
G
(for the word metric with respect to
U
).
Let
X
be a Cayley graph of
\Gamma
so that
(\Gamma,X)
is a
\tau
-bootstrap for some
\tau\in(0,1)
.
For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
,
Theorem 3.11
, provides an approximating sequence

(\Gamma_{j,k},X_{j,k},\mathcal{C}_{j,k})_{j\in\mathbf{N}}

of
\Gamma_{k}/\Gamma_{k}^{n_{k}}
whose elements belong to
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho(n_{k}))
.
We denote by
\psi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
a minimal approximation of
\varphi_{k}
relative to
U_{k}
.
Since

U_{k}\cap\ker\psi_{k}=U_{k}\cap\ker\varphi_{k},\quad\forall k\in\mathbf{N},

the sequence
(\Gamma_{j_{k},k},\psi_{k})
also converges to
(L,\eta)
.
We now distinguish two cases.
Suppose first that the energy
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
is not bounded.
Up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
diverges to infinity.
Hence
(L,\eta)
is a diverging limit group over
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
.

Suppose now that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
is bounded.
We have chosen
\ell_{k}
such that
\rho(n_{k})/\ell_{k}
diverges to infinity.
By construction
\psi_{k}
is a minimal approximation of
\varphi_{k}
relative to
U_{k}
.
Its image is not abelian (provided
k
is large enough) otherwise
L
would be abelian as well.
Applying
Proposition 3.14
with the minimal approximation
\psi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
, we obtain that
j_{k}=0
for all but finitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
In other words,
\psi_{k}
is a morphism from
G
to
\Gamma
.
Recall that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
is bounded.
Up to passing to a subsequence there exists
\psi\colon G\to\Gamma
such that
\psi_{k}
is conjugated to
\psi
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
In particular,
L\cong G/\ker\psi
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
.
∎

4.3
Infinite descending sequence

For the remainder of
Section 4.3
, we fix a filtration of groups
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{F}_{k})
.
Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
As before we write
\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
for the groups
(H,\varphi)
marked by
G
