such that
H
belongs to
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
.
In the remainder of this section, unless mentioned otherwise, all limit groups will be limit groups over
\mathfrak{F}
.
To lighten the statements, we simply refer to them as
limit groups
.

One of the central tools in the study of limit groups is the
shortening argument
of Rips and Sela
[
47
]
.
Given an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
converging to a limit group
(L,\eta)
it provides (under certain circumstances) a new
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence
(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
converging to a proper quotient
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
of
(L,\eta)
.
This construction has numerous applications which are now standard in the area.
We postpone the proof of the shortening argument in
Section 8.3
.
Instead we provide in this section a general abstract framework to investigate its consequences.
We revisit some material from
[
51
,
52
,
23
,
58
,
24
]
.
In particular, we show that if every limit group is shortenable (see
Definition 4.11
) then there is no infinite descending sequence of limit groups.

Factorization property.

If
(L,\eta)
is a limit group over the free group, it is known that
L
is finitely presented
[
33
,
51
,
25
]
.
In particular, the image of
\mathfrak{G}(L)
in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
is open.
This is no more the case a priori if
(L,\eta)
is a limit group over
\mathfrak{F}
.
This motivates the next definition.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
We say that
(Q,\pi)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
has the
factorization property
if there exist a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
, with the following property:
for every marked group
(H,\varphi)\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
, if
W\subset\ker\varphi
, then
\varphi
factors through
\pi
.

Note that if
(Q,\pi)
is a finitely presented group, then it has the factorization property.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(Q,\pi)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
with the factorization property.
There exists a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
such that for every limit group
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
, if
W\subset\ker\eta
, then
(L,\eta)\prec(Q,\pi)
.

Proof.

Let
W\subset\ker\pi
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
be the data provided by the definition of the factorization property.
Let
(L,\eta)
be a limit group such that
W\subset\ker\eta
.
Let
g\in\ker\pi
and set
U=W\cup\{g\}
.
Since
(L,\eta)
is a limit group, there exists
(H,\varphi)\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
such that
U\cap\ker\varphi=U\cap\ker\eta
.
In particular,
W
is contained in
\ker\varphi
.
Thus
\varphi
factors through
\pi
and
\varphi(g)=1
.
Consequently
\eta(g)=1
as well.
We proved that
\ker\pi\subset\ker\eta
, whence the result.
∎

Definition 4.9
.

A finitely generated group
L
is fully residually
\mathfrak{F}
if for every finite subset
U\subset L\setminus\{1\}
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there is a morphism
\varphi\colon L\to H
where
H\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}
and
U\cap\ker\varphi=\emptyset
.

Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
If
L
is fully residually
\mathfrak{F}
, then
(L,\eta)
is a limit group.
The converse requires the factorization property.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
If
(L,\eta)
has the factorization property, then
L
is fully residually
\mathfrak{F}
.

Proof.

Let
W\subset\ker\eta
and
k
be the data provided by the factorization property.
Let
U\subset L\setminus\{1\}
be a finite subset.
We fix a finite subset
V\subset G
containing
W
such that
\eta(V)=U
.
Since
(L,\eta)
is a limit group, there exists
(H,\varphi)\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
such that
V\cap\ker\varphi=V\cap\ker\eta
.
In particular,
W
is contained in
\ker\varphi
, thus
\varphi
factors through
\eta
.
The resulting morphism
\psi\colon L\to H
is such that
U\cap\ker\psi=\emptyset
.
Hence
(L,\eta)
is fully residually
\mathfrak{F}
.
∎

Shortenable limit groups.

For the remainder of this section, we fix a property
\mathcal{P}
of groups.

Example
.

In practice we shall use the following one: a group
H
has
\mathcal{P}
if every abelian subgroup of
H
is finitely generated.
Nevertheless in other contexts the required property can be slightly different.
For instance
[
58
]
uses the following property: group
H
has
\mathcal{P}
if every almost abelian subgroup of
H
is finitely generated.
Since this section aims to be as general as possible, we will not use a specific property
\mathcal{P}
for the moment.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to a limit group
(L,\eta)
.
We say that the sequence
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
is
shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
if there is an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence
(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
such that, up to passing to a subsequence, the following holds.

1.

H^{\prime}_{k}=H_{k}
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

2.

(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
converges to a limit group
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\prec(L,\eta)
.

3.

If
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is not a proper quotient of
(L,\eta)
, then
L^{\prime}
has
\mathcal{P}
.
Moreover, all but finitely many
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
.

4.

If
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
has
\mathcal{P}
, then so has
(L,\eta)
.
If in addition all but finitely many
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
, then all but finitely many
\varphi_{k}
factor through
\eta
.

In this context, we say that
(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
is a
shortening sequence
of
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
and
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
a
shortening quotient
of
(L,\eta)
.

A limit group
(L,\eta)
is
shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
if every
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to
(L,\eta)
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 4.12
.

—

Note that in the definition of shortenable sequences, we require that the target
H^{\prime}_{k}
of shortening morphism
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
is coincides with the one
H_{k}
of the original morphism
\varphi_{k}
.
This plays an important role later, see for instance the footnote in the proof of
Proposition 4.14
.

—

Note that if
(L,\eta)
is a limit group satisfying
\mathcal{P}
and the factorization property, then it is automatically shortenable.
Indeed if
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
is an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to
(L,\eta)
we can simply take as shortening sequence
(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})=(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
.
In particular, finitely presented limit groups satisfying
\mathcal{P}
are shortenable.

We start with some stabilities of the shortenable property.
If the context is clear, we may omit the property
\mathcal{P}
and simply write that a sequence / a limit group is shortenable.
The first statement directly follows from the definition.

Lemma 4.13

(Changing the marker)
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
W\subset G
be a finite subset and
\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow Q
the projection onto
Q=G/\langle\!\langle W\rangle\!\rangle
.
Let
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})\in\mathfrak{G}(Q)
be an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to a limit group
(L,\eta)
.
Then
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
, as a sequence in
\mathfrak{G}(Q)
, if and only if
(H_{k},\varphi_{k}\circ\pi)
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
as a sequence in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
.

In particular,
(L,\eta)
is shortenable if and only if so is
(L,\eta\circ\pi)
.

Proposition 4.14

(Free product stability)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{P}
is stable under free products and taking subgroups.
Let
G
be a finitely generated group which splits as a free product
G=G_{1}\ast G_{2}
.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group which decomposes as
L=L_{1}\ast L_{2}
so that
\eta
maps
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
onto
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
respectively.
We denote by
\eta_{i}\colon G_{i}\twoheadrightarrow L_{i}
the restriction of
\eta
to
G_{i}
.
If for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, the marked group
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
– viewed as an element of
\mathfrak{G}(G_{i})
– then so is
(L,\eta)
.

Proof.

Let
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
be an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to
(L,\eta)
.
Given
i\in\{1,2\}
, we denote by
\varphi_{k}^{i}
the restriction of
\varphi_{k}
to
G_{i}
and
H^{i}_{k}\subset H_{k}
its image.
Note that
H^{1}_{k}
and
H^{2}_{k}
generate
H_{k}
.
By assumption,
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
is shortenable, viewed as a limit group in
\mathfrak{G}(G_{i})
.
In particular,
any
subsequence of
(H^{i}_{k},\varphi_{k}^{i})
is shortenable.
We denote by
\psi_{k}^{1}\colon G_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}_{k}
and
\psi_{k}^{2}\colon G_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H^{2}_{k}
for the sequences obtained by shortening successively
(H^{1}_{k},\varphi_{k}^{1})
and
(H^{2}_{k},\varphi_{k}^{2})
.
We write
(L^{\prime}_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i})
for the corresponding shortening quotient of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
Let
\psi_{k}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H_{k}
be the morphism whose restriction to
G_{1}
( respectively
G_{2}
) is
\psi_{k}^{1}
( respectively
\psi_{k}^{2}
).
1
1

1

We have used here the fact that the morphism
\varphi^{i}_{k}
and its shortening
\psi^{i}_{k}
have the same image (which are also subgroups of
H_{k}
).
It allows us to build a map
\psi_{k}
whose target
H_{k}
belongs to the filtration
\mathfrak{F}
.
Without this property of shortenings an alternative strategy would have been to consider the map
G\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}_{k}\ast H^{2}_{k}
built out of
\psi^{1}_{k}
and
\psi^{2}_{k}
.
However there is a priori no reason that
H^{1}_{k}\ast H^{2}_{k}
is an element of the filtration
\mathfrak{F}
.

We define
\eta^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow L^{\prime}_{1}\ast L^{\prime}_{2}
in the same way.
Up to passing to a subsequence,
(H_{k},\psi_{k})
converges to a limit group
(Q,\nu)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
We are going to prove that
(H_{k},\psi_{k})
is a shortening sequence of
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
.

—

By construction, we have

(Q,\nu)\prec(L^{\prime}_{1}\ast L^{\prime}_{2},\eta^{\prime})\prec(L,\eta).

which proves Point
2
of
Definition 4.11
.

—

Let us focus on Point
3
of
Definition 4.11
.
Assume that
Q
is not proper quotient of
L
.
It follows that
(L^{\prime}_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i})=(L_{i},\eta_{i})
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Recall that
(L^{\prime}_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i})
has been obtained from
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
by the shortening procedure.
According to
Definition 4.11

3
both
(L_{1},\eta_{1})
and
(L_{2},\eta_{2})
satisfy
\mathcal{P}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is stable under taking free product,
(L,\eta)
has
\mathcal{P}
.
On the other hand, we get that all but finitely many
\psi_{k}^{i}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
.
Hence all but finitely many
\psi_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
.
This proves Point
3
of
Definition 4.11
.

—

We are left to prove Point
4
of
Definition 4.11
.
To that end we assume that
(Q,\nu)
has
\mathcal{P}
.
Note that the projection
L^{\prime}_{1}\ast L^{\prime}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow Q
induces an embedding from each
L^{\prime}_{i}
into
Q
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is stable under taking subgroups, each
L^{\prime}_{i}
has
\mathcal{P}
.
According to
Definition 4.11

4
, both
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
have
\mathcal{P}
, hence so has
L
(
\mathcal{P}
is stable under free products).
Suppose in addition that all but finitely many
\psi_{k}
factor through
\nu
.
It follows from the construction that all but finitely many
\psi_{k}^{i}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
.
By
Definition 4.11

4
, all but finitely many
\varphi_{k}^{1}
and
\varphi_{k}^{2}
factor through
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
.
Hence all but finitely many
\varphi_{k}
factor through
\eta
.
This proves Point
4
of
Definition 4.11
. ∎

Given
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
, an
infinite descending sequence starting at
(L,\eta)
is a sequence of marked groups

(L,\eta)=(L_{0},\eta_{0})\succ(L_{1},\eta_{1})\succ(L_{2},\eta_{2})\succ\dots

such that
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
is a proper quotient of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
, for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
Assume that every limit group
(Q,\nu)\prec(L,\eta)
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
.
Then one of the following holds.

1.

There is an infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L,\eta)
.

2.

(L,\eta)
satisfies
\mathcal{P}
and the factorization property.

Proof.

We assume that
2
does hot hold.
We are going to build by induction an infinite descending sequence of limit groups

(L,\eta)=(L_{0},\eta_{0})\succ(L_{1},\eta_{1})\succ(L_{2},\eta_{2})\succ\dots

such that for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, either
L_{i}
does not satisfy
\mathcal{P}
or
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
has not the factorization property.
We start with
(L_{0},\eta_{0})=(L,\eta)
.
Let
i\in\mathbf{N}
such that
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
has been built.
We are going to define an
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence converging to an auxiliary quotient
(Q,\nu)
of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
To that end, we distinguish two cases.

(C1)

Suppose that
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
does not satisfies
\mathcal{P}
.
We choose any
\mathfrak{F}
-sequence
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
converging to
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
In addition we let
(Q,\nu)=(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.

(C2)

Suppose that
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
does not have the factorization property.
We choose a non-decreasing exhaustion
(W_{k})
of
\ker\eta_{i}
by finite subsets.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}(G)
such that
W_{k}\subset\ker\varphi_{k}
, but
\varphi_{k}
does not factor through
\eta_{i}
.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
converges to a limit group
(Q,\nu)
.

In both cases,
(Q,\nu)
is a quotient of
(L,\eta)
hence is shortenable.
Let
(H^{\prime}_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
be a sequence obtained by shortening
(H_{k},\varphi_{k})
.
Up to passing to a subsequence, it converges to the shortening quotient
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
of
(Q,\nu)
.

Claim 4.16
.

Either
L_{i+1}
has not
\mathcal{P}
or
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
does not have the factorization property.

Suppose on the contrary that
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
had both
\mathcal{P}
and the factorization property.
In particular, all but finitely many
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta_{i+1}
.
On the one hand, by
Definition 4.11

4

(Q,\nu)
has
\mathcal{P}
.
Thus we cannot be in Case
(C1)
.
On the other hand,
\varphi_{k}
factors through
\nu
for all but finitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
However
(Q,\nu)\prec(L_{i},\eta_{i})
, hence
\varphi_{k}
factors through
\eta_{i}
, for all but finitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
This contradicts Case
(C2)
and completes the proof of our claim.

In view of our claim and
Definition 4.11

3
, the group
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
is a proper quotient of
(Q,\nu)
and thus of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
Hence
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
satisfies all the announced properties.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
Assume that every limit group
(Q,\nu)\prec(L,\eta)
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
.
Then there is no infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L,\eta)
.

Proof.

We fix a non-decreasing exhaustion
(U_{i})
of
G
by finite subsets.
Assume that the statement does not hold.
We build a preferred descending sequence of limit groups
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
such that for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, there is an infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
We proceed as follows.
We start with
(L_{0},\eta_{0})=(L,\eta)
.
Assume now that
(L_{i-1},\eta_{i-1})
has been defined for some
i\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Among all the limit groups
(Q,\nu)\prec(L_{i-1},\eta_{i-1})
we choose
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
such that

—

(L_{i},\eta_{i})
is a proper quotient of
(L_{i-1},\eta_{i-1})
;

—

there is an infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
;

—

the cardinality of
U_{i}\cap\ker\eta_{i}
is maximal for the above property.

By construction the sequence
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
converges in
\mathfrak{G}(G)
to a group
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
such that
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})\prec(L_{i},\eta_{i})
, for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
.
Since the set of limit groups is closed,
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
is also a limit group.

We claim that there is no infinite descending sequence starting at
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
.
Assume on the contrary that such a sequence exists

(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})\succ(R_{1},\mu_{1})\succ(R_{2},\mu_{2})\succ\dots

Hence, there is an element
g\in\ker\mu_{1}\setminus\ker\eta_{\infty}
which belongs to
U_{i}
for some
i\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
In particular,
g\in\ker\mu_{1}\setminus\ker\eta_{i}
.
By construction

(L_{i-1},\eta_{i-1})\succ(R_{1},\mu_{1})\succ(R_{2},\mu_{2})\succ\dots

is an infinite descending sequence.
Moreover
(R_{1},\mu_{1})
is a proper quotient of
(L_{i-1},\eta_{i-1})
and
U_{i}\cap\ker\eta_{i}
is strictly contained in
U_{i}\cap\ker\mu_{1}
.
This contradicts our definition of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
and completes the proof of our claim.

By construction
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
is a quotient of
(L,\eta)
.
It follows from our assumption that every limit group
(Q,\nu)\prec(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
is shortenable.
Consequently
(L_{\infty},\eta_{\infty})
has the factorization property (
Proposition 4.15
).
Hence by
Lemma 4.8
the sequence
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
eventually stabilizes, a contradiction.
∎

Combined with
Proposition 4.15
we get the following corollary

Corollary 4.18
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
If every limit group
(Q,\nu)\prec(L,\eta)
is shortenable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
, then
(L,\eta)
has the factorization property.

5
Splittings and graphs of actions

In this section we mostly set up notation and vocabulary around graphs of groups and graphs of actions.

5.1
Splittings

A
graph
is a pair
(V,E)
of two sets: the
vertex set

V
and the
edge set

E
together with a fixed point free involution
E\to E
that we denote by
e\to\bar{e}
, and two attachment maps
\iota\colon E\to V
and
t\colon E\to V
such that
\iota(\bar{e})=t(e)
, for every
e\in E
.
The vertices
\iota(e)
and
t(e)
are respectively called the
start
and the
end
of
e
.
Given a vertex
v
, its
link
, denoted by
\operatorname{lk}(v)
, is the set of all edges starting at
v
.
A
path
in this graph is a sequence
\nu=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{m})
of edges such that
\iota(e_{i+1})=t(e_{i})
, for every
i\in\llbracket 1,m-1\rrbracket
.
We usually denote it by
\nu=e_{1}e_{2}\cdots e_{m}
.
Given
v\in V
, a
circuit based at
v
is a path which starts and ends at
v
.
A
simplicial tree
is a connected graph without non-backtracking circuits.

G
-trees.

Let
G
be a group.
A
G
-tree
is a simplicial tree
S
endowed with a simplicial action of
G
without inversion.
This action is
trivial
if
G
fixes a point.
It is
minimal
if
S
does not contain any proper
G
-invariant subtree.
Given a
G
-tree, one of the following holds: the action of
G
is trivial,
G
fixes an end, or
G
contains loxodromic elements and there exists a minimal
G
-invariant subtree.
A
splitting
of
G
is a non-trivial minimal
G
-tree.
A
one-edge splitting
of
G
is a splitting with a single edge orbit.
Bass-Serre theory builds a correspondance between minimal
G
-trees and
graph of groups decompositions of
G

[
54
]
.
In this article we favor the point of view of
G
-trees.

If
v
( respectively
e
) is a vertex ( respectively edge) of
S
, we denote by
G_{v}
( respectively
G_{e}
) its stabilizer in
G
.

Maps between trees.

Let
S,S^{\prime}
be two
G
-trees.

—

A
morphism
is a
G
-equivariant graph morphism
f\colon S\to S^{\prime}
.
In particular, no edge of
S
is collapsed to a point.

—

A
collapse map
is a
G
-equivariant map
f\colon S\to S^{\prime}
obtained by collapsing certain edges to points, followed by an isomorphism.
In particular, it sends vertices to vertices ( respectively edges to edges or vertices).
In this setting we say that
S^{\prime}
is a
collapse
of
S
and
S

refines

S^{\prime}
.

If the action of
G
on
S^{\prime}
is minimal, then such maps are always onto.

Normal forms.

Let
S
be a minimal
G
-tree.
Our goal is to define a normal form to represent each element of
G
, which reflects the action on
S
.
The construction goes as follows.

We choose a subtree
D_{0}\subset S
lifting a maximal subtree in the quotient graph
S/G
.
In addition, we choose a subtree
D_{1}\subset S
containing
D_{0}
such that every edge orbit in
S
has exactly one representative in
D_{1}
.
We denote by
V
the vertex set of
D_{0}
and by
E
the edge set of
D_{1}
.
Let
\mathbf{F}(E)
be the free group

\mathbf{F}(E)=\mathchoice{\left\langle E\mid e\bar{e}=1,\ \forall e\in E\right\rangle}{\langle E\mid e\bar{e}=1,\ \forall e\in E\rangle}{\langle E\mid e\bar{e}=1,\ \forall e\in E\rangle}{\langle E\mid e\bar{e}=1,\ \forall e\in E\rangle},

and

\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{F}(E)\freep\left(\freep_{v\in V}G_{v}\right),

Notations 5.1
.

In order to avoid any confusion, we always write in bold type the elements of
E
and
G_{v}
(for
v\in V
) when primarily seen as elements of
\mathbf{G}
.

We now define a map
\sigma\colon\mathbf{G}\to G
which extends the embeddings
G_{v}\hookrightarrow G
, for every
v\in V
.
The construction goes as follows.
For every edge
\mathbf{e}\in E
we choose an element
s(\mathbf{e})\in G
so that
s(\mathbf{e})t(\mathbf{e})
belongs to
V
.
Then we let

\sigma(\mathbf{e})=s(\bar{\mathbf{e}})s(\mathbf{e})^{-1}.

Note that
\sigma(\bar{\mathbf{e}})=\sigma(\mathbf{e})^{-1}
, for every
\mathbf{e}\in E
, hence
\sigma
is well-defined.
It is known that
\sigma
is onto.
Let us make this idea precise.
A
decorated path
is an element
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbf{G}
, which can be written as follows

\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbf{g}_{1}\mathbf{e}_{2}\mathbf{g}_{2}\dots\mathbf{g}_{m-1}\mathbf{e}_{m}\mathbf{g}_{m},

where

(a)

for every
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, the element
\mathbf{e}_{i}
belongs to
E
; moreover the sequence
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{m})
maps to a path in the quotient graph
S/G
;

(b)

for every
i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, the element
\mathbf{g}_{i}
fixes the (unique) vertex of
v_{i}
of
D_{0}
lying in the
G
-orbit of
t(\mathbf{e}_{i})
if
i>0
( respectively
\iota(\mathbf{e}_{i+1})
if
i<m
)

Such a decorated path is
reduced
if

(a)

for every
i\in\llbracket 1,m-1\rrbracket
, either
\mathbf{e}_{i+1}\neq\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
, or
\mathbf{g}_{i}
does not fix
s(\mathbf{e}_{i})\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

A
decorated circuit based at a vertex
v\in V
is a decorated path

\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbf{g}_{1}\mathbf{e}_{2}\mathbf{g}_{2}\dots\mathbf{g}_{m-1}\mathbf{e}_{m}\mathbf{g}_{m},

such that
\iota(\mathbf{e}_{1})
and
t(\mathbf{e}_{m})
lie in the
G
-orbit of
v
.
It is known that every element
g\in G
has a pre-image under
\sigma
which is a reduced decorated circuit based at
v

This is essentially a direct consequence of Serre’s combinatorial definition of the fundamental group of a graph of groups.
See for instance Serre
[
53
, Chapitre 5.2, Exercice (c)]
.
We call this pre-image the
normal form of
g
based at
v
.
Note that this normal form is not necessarily unique though.

A decorated path
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbf{g}_{1}\dots\mathbf{e}_{m}\mathbf{g}_{m}
also gives rise to a (regular) path in
S
.
For every
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
define the edge
f_{i}
of
S
as
f_{i}=u_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
where

u_{i}=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\dots\mathbf{e}_{i-1}\mathbf{g}_{i-1})s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i}).

Then
\nu=f_{1}f_{2}\dots f_{m}
is a path in
S
from
v
to
gv^{\prime}
where
g=\sigma(\mathbf{g})
while
v
and
v^{\prime}
are the vertices of
D_{0}
in the
G
-orbits of
\iota(\mathbf{e}_{1})
and
t(\mathbf{e}_{m})
respectively.
It follows from the construction that
\nu
has no backtracking if and only if
\mathbf{g}
is reduced.

5.2
Graph of actions

Graph of actions were formalized by Levitt to decompose the action of a given group on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
[
34
]
.
We follow here
[
25
, Definition 4.3]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
graph of actions on
\mathbf{R}
-trees

\Lambda
consists of the following data.

(a)

A group
G
and a
G
-tree
S
(called the
skeleton
of the graph of actions).

(b)

An
\mathbf{R}
-tree
Y_{v}
(called
vertex tree
) for each vertex
v
of
S
.

(c)

An
attaching point

p_{e}\in Y_{t(e)}
for each oriented edge
e
of
S
.

Moreover all these data should be invariant under
G
, i.e.

—

G
acts on the disjoint union of the vertex trees so that the projection
Y_{v}\mapsto v
is equivariant,

—

for every edge
e
of
S
, for every
g\in G
, we have
p_{ge}=gp_{e}
.

Remark
.

If no confusion can arise, we write
\Lambda
for both the graph of actions and its underlying skeleton.
Similarly we speak of
\Lambda
as a splitting of
G
.

Given a vertex
v
of
S
, the action of
G_{v}
on
Y_{v}
is called a
vertex action
.
To such a graph of actions
\Lambda
one associates an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T_{\Lambda}
endowed with an action of
G
by isometries.
Roughly speaking,
T_{\Lambda}
is obtained from the disjoint union of the vertex trees by identifying the attaching points
p_{e}
and
p_{\bar{e}}
for each edge
e
of
S
, see
[
25
, Section 4.3]
for the precise construction.
We say that the action of
G
on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T

decomposes as a graph of actions
, if there exists a
G
-equivariant isometry from
T
to
T_{\Lambda}
.

Let us now relate, for later use, the geometry of
T_{\Lambda}
with the normal form introduced in the previous section.
The map
\sigma\colon\mathbf{G}\to G
, and all related objects are chosen as in
Section 5.1
.
Let
v\in V
and
x
be a point in the vertex tree
Y_{v}
.
Let
g\in G
and

\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbf{g}_{1}\dots\mathbf{g}_{m-1}\mathbf{e}_{m}\mathbf{g}_{m}

a decorated circuit based at
v
such that
\sigma(\mathbf{g})=g
.
For every
i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
we let

u_{i}=\sigma\left(\mathbf{g}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{1}\dots\mathbf{g}_{i-1}\right)s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad f_{i}=u_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}

According to our previous discussion
\nu=f_{1}f_{2}\dots f_{m}
is a path in
S
from
v
to
gv
.
By construction the following concatenation is a path in
T_{\Lambda}
between
x
and
gx
:

\mathchoice{\left[x,p_{f_{1}}\right]}{[x,p_{f_{1}}]}{[x,p_{f_{1}}]}{[x,p_{f_{1}}]}\cup\mathchoice{\left[p_{f_{1}},p_{f_{2}}\right]}{[p_{f_{1}},p_{f_{2}}]}{[p_{f_{1}},p_{f_{2}}]}{[p_{f_{1}},p_{f_{2}}]}\cup\mathchoice{\left[p_{f_{2}},p_{f_{3}}\right]}{[p_{f_{2}},p_{f_{3}}]}{[p_{f_{2}},p_{f_{3}}]}{[p_{f_{2}},p_{f_{3}}]}\cup\dots\cup\mathchoice{\left[p_{f_{m}},gx\right]}{[p_{f_{m}},gx]}{[p_{f_{m}},gx]}{[p_{f_{m}},gx]}.

This path is a geodesic of
T
whenever
\mathbf{g}
is reduced.
In particular, we get

\mathchoice{\left|gx-x\right|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-\mathbf{g}_{0}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}\right|}{|x-\mathbf{g}_{0}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}|}{|x-\mathbf{g}_{0}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}|}{|x-\mathbf{g}_{0}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}|}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathchoice{\left|s(\mathbf{e}_{i})p_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}-\mathbf{g}_{i}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1}}\right|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{i})p_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}-\mathbf{g}_{i}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1}}|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{i})p_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}-\mathbf{g}_{i}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1}}|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{i})p_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}-\mathbf{g}_{i}s(\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1})p_{\bar{\mathbf{e}}_{i+1}}|}+\mathchoice{\left|s(\mathbf{e}_{m})p_{\mathbf{e}_{m}}-\mathbf{g}_{m}x\right|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{m})p_{\mathbf{e}_{m}}-\mathbf{g}_{m}x|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{m})p_{\mathbf{e}_{m}}-\mathbf{g}_{m}x|}{|s(\mathbf{e}_{m})p_{\mathbf{e}_{m}}-\mathbf{g}_{m}x|}

(11)

with equality whenever
\mathbf{g}
is reduced.
This decomposition of
\mathchoice{\left[x,gx\right]}{[x,gx]}{[x,gx]}{[x,gx]}
will be useful when it comes to the shortening argument, see
Section 7.5

5.3
Root splittings

Let us recall the definition of a root splitting, whose relevance in our context was highlighted in the introduction (see for instance Examples
1.2
and
1.7
).

Definition 5.2

(Root splitting)
.

Let
G
be a group.
A
root splitting
of
G
is a decomposition of
G
as a non-trivial amalgamated product
G=A\freep_{C}B
, where
B
is an abelian group and
C
is a proper, finite index subgroup of
B
.
We call this index the
order
of the splitting.
Alternatively we say that
G
is
obtained from
A
by adjoining roots
and
B
is the
adjoint root factor
of the splitting.
We say that the root splitting is
non-essential
if
C
is a proper, free factor of
A
and (abstractly) isomorphic to
B
.

Consider the free group
\mathbf{F}_{2}
generated by two elements, say
a
and
b
.
A typical example of a non-essential root splitting is

\mathbf{F}_{2}=\mathchoice{\left\langle a,b^{2}\right\rangle}{\langle a,b^{2}\rangle}{\langle a,b^{2}\rangle}{\langle a,b^{2}\rangle}*_{\mathchoice{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle}{\langle b^{2}\rangle}{\langle b^{2}\rangle}{\langle b^{2}\rangle}}\mathchoice{\left\langle b\right\rangle}{\langle b\rangle}{\langle b\rangle}{\langle b\rangle}.

Note that if
G
is freely indecomposable, then every root splitting of
G
is essential.
The next statements stresses the importance of root splittings in our study of periodic groups.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
G
be a group which admits a root splitting
G=A\freep_{C}B
of order
p
where
B
is the adjoint root factor of the splitting.
Let
n\in\mathbf{N}
be an integer co-prime with
p
.
Let
\Gamma
be a periodic group of exponent
n
.
Every morphism
\varphi\colon A\to\Gamma
uniquely extends to a morphism
\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\to\Gamma
.
Moreover
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
have the same image.

Proof.

Since
n
is co-prime with
p
, one checks that
B=CB^{n}
and
C\cap B^{n}=C^{n}
.
It means that the embedding
C\hookrightarrow B
induces an isomorphism
\alpha\colon C/C^{n}\to B/B^{n}
.
Let
\varphi\colon A\to\Gamma
be a morphism.
Since
\Gamma
has exponent
n
, its restriction to
C
factors through the projection
C\twoheadrightarrow C/C^{n}
.
We write
\psi\colon C/C^{n}\to\Gamma
for the resulting morphism.
Extending
\varphi
to a morphism
\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\to\Gamma
is equivalent to finding a morphism
\psi^{\prime}\colon B/B^{n}\to\Gamma
such that
\psi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\psi
.
Since
\alpha
is an isomorphism, there is exactly one way to do so.
Moreover the extension
\psi^{\prime}
has the same image as
\psi
.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
G
be a group which admits a root splitting
G=A\freep_{C}B
of order
p
where
B
is the adjoint root factor of the splitting.
Then for every integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, which is co-prime with
p
, the embedding
A\hookrightarrow G
induces an isomorphism
A/A^{n}\to G/G^{n}
.

Proof.

We denote by
\pi_{A}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow A/A^{n}
and
\pi_{G}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G/G^{n}
the canonical projections.
The embedding
\iota\colon A\hookrightarrow G
induces a morphism
\iota_{n}\colon A/A^{n}\to G/G^{n}
such that
\pi_{G}\circ\iota=\iota_{n}\circ\pi_{A}
.
According to
Proposition 5.3
there is an epimorphism
\varphi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow A/A^{n}
extending
\pi_{A}
, i.e.
\varphi\circ\iota=\pi_{A}
.
Since
A/A^{n}
has exponent
n
, the morphism
\varphi
factors through
\pi_{G}
.
We denote by
\psi\colon G/G^{n}\twoheadrightarrow A/A^{n}
the resulting epimorphism, so that
\psi\circ\pi_{G}=\varphi
.
One checks that

\iota_{n}\circ\psi\circ\pi_{G}\circ\iota=\iota_{n}\circ\varphi\circ\iota=\iota_{n}\circ\pi_{A}=\pi_{G}\circ\iota.

In other words
\iota_{n}\circ\psi\circ\pi_{G}
extends
\pi_{G}\circ\iota
.
The uniqueness part of
Proposition 5.3
, tells us that
\iota_{n}\circ\psi\circ\pi_{G}=\pi_{G}
.
Recall that
\pi_{G}
is onto, hence
\iota_{n}\circ\psi=\operatorname{Id}
.
It follows that
\psi
, and thus
\iota_{n}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

Let
G
be a group.
A
generalized root splitting
of
G
is a graph of groups decomposition of
G
of the following form
A
B_{1}
B_{2}
B_{3}
\vdots
B_{m}
C_{1}
C_{2}
C_{3}
C_{m}

where for every
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, the group
B_{i}
is abelian and
C_{i}
is a proper finite index subgroup of
B_{i}
.

5.4
Covers of marked group

In general, limit groups need not be finitely presented.
In order to bypass this difficulty, it is common to replace them by finitely presented covers.
For our purpose we need these covers to reflect a given graph of groups decomposition.
This motivates the next definitions.

Definition 5.6

(Strong cover)
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(H,\varphi)
and
(\hat{H},\hat{\varphi})
be two groups marked by
G
.
Let
S
( respectively
\hat{S}
) be a splitting of
H
( respectively
\hat{H}
) over abelian groups.
We say that the triple
(\hat{H},\hat{\varphi},\hat{S})
is a
strong cover
of
(H,\varphi,S)
if there exist an epimorphism
\zeta\colon\hat{H}\twoheadrightarrow H
and a

\zeta
-equivariant map
f\colon\hat{S}\to S
such that the following holds.

(a)

\zeta\circ\hat{\varphi}=\varphi
. In particular,
(H,\varphi)\prec(\hat{H},\hat{\varphi})
.

(b)

The map
f
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{S}/\hat{H}
onto
S/H
.

(c)

The morphism
\zeta
is one-to-one when restricted to any edge group of
\hat{S}
.

(d)

Let
\hat{v}
be a vertex of
\hat{S}
and
v=f(\hat{v})
its image in
S
.
If
H_{v}
is finitely presented ( respectively abelian), then
\zeta
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{H}_{\hat{v}}
onto
H_{v}
( respectively an embedding from
\hat{H}_{\hat{v}}
into
H_{v}
).

Definition 5.7

(Convergence of strong covers)
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(H,\varphi)
be a group marked by
G
and
S
a splitting of
H
over abelian groups.
Let
(\hat{H}_{i},\hat{\varphi}_{i},\hat{S}_{i})
be a sequence of strong covers of
(H,\varphi,S)
.
We write
\zeta_{i}\colon\hat{H}_{i}\to H
and
f_{i}\colon\hat{S}_{i}\to S
for the corresponding underlying maps.
The sequence
(\hat{H}_{i},\hat{\varphi}_{i},\hat{S}_{i})

converges to

(H,\varphi,S)
if the following holds.

—

(H_{i},\varphi_{i})
converges to
(H,\varphi)
in the space of marked groups.

—

Let
e
be an edge of
S
.
For every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists a pre-image
\hat{e}_{i}
of
e
in
\hat{S}_{i}
such that

H_{e}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbf{N}}\zeta_{i}\left(\hat{H}_{i,\hat{e}_{i}}\right).

—

Let
v
be a vertex of
S
.
For every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists a pre-image
\hat{v}_{i}
of
v
in
\hat{S}_{i}
such that

H_{v}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbf{N}}\zeta_{i}\left(\hat{H}_{i,\hat{v}_{i}}\right).

The next lemma ensures the existence of converging sequences of strong covers.
The proof works verbatim as in
[
58
, Lemma 7.1]
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
G
be a finitely presented group.
Let
(H,\varphi)
be a group marked by
G
and
S
a splitting of
H
over abelian groups.
There exists a sequence of strong covers
(\hat{H}_{i},\hat{\varphi}_{i},\hat{S}_{i})
which converges to
(H,\varphi,S)
such that for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, the group
\hat{H}_{i}
is finitely presented.

6
JSJ decompositions

Let
G
be a (finitely generated) group.
Given a class
\mathcal{A}
of subgroups of
G
, the JSJ decomposition of
G
relative to
\mathcal{A}
is a graph of groups decomposition which (when it exists) encodes all the possible splittings of
G
over subgroups in
\mathcal{A}
.
For our purpose we need to understand the abelian splittings of
G
, provided
G
is CSA (see
Definition 3.1
).
In this setting, the existence of a JSJ decomposition is often stated in the literature under the assumption that
G
is one-ended (which is equivalent to asking that
G
is freely indecomposable, when
G
is torsion-free), see for instance
[
28
, Theorem 9.5 and 9.14]
.
However in this article we will consider CSA groups which are freely indecomposable, but not necessarily torsion-free (and thus maybe not one-ended).
It turns out that the JSJ decomposition does exist in this case as well.
In this section we briefly recall the main arguments of its construction.
We use the language of Guirardel and Levitt
[
28
]
.

6.1
Generalities

Let
G
be a group.
Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{H}
be two classes of subgroups of
G
.
We assume that
\mathcal{A}
is closed under conjugation and taking subgroups.
In addition we suppose that
G
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
An
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree is a
G
-tree such that each edge stabilizer belongs to
\mathcal{A}
while the subgroups of
\mathcal{H}
are elliptic.

A subgroup
E
of
G
is
universally elliptic
if it is elliptic in any
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
An
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree is
universally elliptic
if its edge stabilizers are universally elliptic.
Given two
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, we say that
T_{1}

dominates

T_{2}
if there is a
G
-equivariant map
T_{1}\to T_{2}
or equivalently, if every vertex stabilizer of
T_{1}
is elliptic in
T_{2}
.

Deformation spaces
are defined by saying that two
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees belong to the same deformation space if they dominate each other.

Definition 6.1
.

The
JSJ deformation space of
G
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
is the collection of universally elliptic
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees which dominate every other universally elliptic
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
A point of the JSJ deformation space is called a
JSJ tree
.
The corresponding graph of groups decomposition of
G
is called a
JSJ decomposition
.
The vertex group of a JSJ tree is
rigid
if it is universally elliptic and
flexible
otherwise.
The vertices of the JSJ tree are called
rigid
and
flexible
accordingly.

Definition 6.2
.

A subgroup
Q
of
G
is
quadratically hanging
, or simply
QH
(over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
) if

(a)

Q
is the stabilizer of a vertex
v
of an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree,

(b)

Q
is an extension
1\to F\to Q\to\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
with
\Sigma
a compact hyperbolic
2
-orbifold; we call
F
the
fiber
, and
\Sigma
the
underlying orbifold
;

(c)

each edge group incident to
v
is an
extended boundary subgroup
: by definition, this means that its image in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is finite or contained in a boundary subgroup
B
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

Although it is not explicit in the terminology the fiber
F
and the orbifold
\Sigma
are part of the structure of a quadratically hanging subgroup.

6.2
Existence

In this section,
G
is a CSA group.
We denote by
\mathcal{A}
the collection all its abelian subgroups.
We write
\mathcal{H}
for a family of subgroups of
G
and assume that
G
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
As the notation suggests, we are interested in splittings of
G
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
In order to prove the existence of the JSJ deformation space Guirardel and Levitt use a stronger notion of domination, see
[
28
, Definition 7.10]
.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
T,T^{\prime}
be
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees.
The tree
T

\mathcal{A}
-dominates

T^{\prime}
if the following holds

(a)

T
dominates
T^{\prime}
;

(b)

every edge stabilizer of
T^{\prime}
is elliptic in
T
;

(c)

any subgroup which is elliptic in
T^{\prime}
but not in
T
belongs to
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{*}
be the set of all
non-trivial
groups
A\in\mathcal{A}
.
We endow
\mathcal{A}^{*}
with the following binary relation,
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
if
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
generate an abelian group.
Since
G
is CSA, this is actually an equivalence relation.
Moreover it is
admissible
in the sense of
[
28
, Definition 7.1]
, i.e.

(A1)

If
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
, then
gA_{1}g^{-1}\sim gA_{2}g^{-1}
, for every
g\in G
.

(A2)

If
A_{1}\subset A_{2}
then
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
.

(A3)

Let
T
be a simplicial
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree with non-trivial edge stabilizers.
If
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
and
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively fix vertices
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, then
G_{e}\sim A_{i}
, for every edge
e
contained in
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}
.

For every
A\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
, we denote by
[A]
the equivalence class of
A
.
In follows from
(C2)(A3)
that the action by conjugation induces an action of
G
on the set of equivalence classes.
Since
G
is CSA, the stabilizer of
[A]
is the maximal abelian subgroup containing
A
.
In particular, it belongs to
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

Consider now an
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H})
-tree
T
, i.e. an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree whose edge stabilizers are non-trivial.
We recall a construction called the
tree of cylinders
, see Guirardel and Levitt
[
27
]
.
We declare two edges
e,e^{\prime}
of
T
to be equivalent if
G_{e}\sim G_{e^{\prime}}
.
By
(C2)(A3)
, the union of all edges in a given equivalence class is a subtree called a
cylinder
.
Two distinct cylinders meet in at most one vertex.
The
tree of cylinders

T_{c}
is bipartite tree with vertex set
V=V_{0}\sqcup V_{1}
where
V_{0}
consists of all vertices of
T
that belong to at least two cylinders and
V_{1}
is the set of cylinders.
There is an edge
\varepsilon=(v,Y)
between
v\in V_{0}
and
Y\in V_{1}
if
v
belongs to
Y
.

Remark
.

In
[
28
]
Guirardel and Levitt use this construction for
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees with
infinite
edge stabilizers.
Nevertheless, as pointed out in
[
27
, Section 3.5]
, if
G
is CSA, the construction also works for
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees with non-trivial edge stabilizers.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}
the collection of all non-cyclic groups in
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
Its tree of cylinders
T_{c}
has the following properties

i.

T_{c}
is an
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree;

ii.

T

\mathcal{A}
-dominate
T_{c}
;

iii.

the action of
G
on
T_{c}
is
2
-acylindrical.

iv.

If
\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}\subset\mathcal{H}
, then
T
and
T_{c}
are in the same deformation space.

Proof.

Let
Y
be a cylinder of
T
.
One checks that the stabilizer
G_{Y}
of
Y
seen as a vertex of
T_{c}
is the maximal abelian subgroup containing
G_{e}
for some (hence any) edge
e
of
Y
.
Let
\varepsilon=(v,Y)
be an edge of
T_{c}
.
We fix an edge
e
of
Y
starting at
v
.
The stabilizer
G_{\varepsilon}
is contained in
G_{Y}
hence is abelian.
It also contains
G_{e}
, thus is non-trivial.
Consequently every edge stabilizer of
T_{c}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of
T
.
If
v
belongs to at least two cylinders, then it defines a vertex in
T_{c}
, hence
G_{v}
is elliptic in
T_{c}
.
If
v
belongs to a single cylinder
Y
.
Then
G_{v}
necessarily preserves
Y
, hence fixes the corresponding vertex of
T_{c}
.
This shows that
T
dominates
T_{c}
.
In particular, every subgroup
H\in\mathcal{H}
is elliptic in
T_{c}
.
By construction, edge stabilizers in
T_{c}
always fix a vertex in
T
.
Moreover the stabilizer of a vertex
v
of
T_{c}
is either elliptic in
T
(if
v\in V_{0}
) or abelian (if
v\in V_{1}
).
Thus
T

\mathcal{A}
-dominates
T
.

Consider two cylinders
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
of
T
.
Write
G_{Y}
and
G_{Y^{\prime}}
for their respective stabilizers (seen as vertices of
T_{c}
).
Suppose that
G_{Y}\cap G_{Y^{\prime}}\neq\{1\}
.
We choose
e
and
e^{\prime}
two edges of
T
lying in
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
respectively.
As we observed before
G_{Y}
is the maximal abelian subgroup containing
G_{e}
.
The same holds for
e^{\prime}
.
Since
G
is CSA, it forces that
G_{Y}=G_{Y^{\prime}}
, thus
e\sim e^{\prime}
consequently
Y=Y^{\prime}
.
We proved that if
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are distinct cylinders then their stabilizers have a trivial intersection.
Since
T_{c}
is bipartite, it implies that the action of
G
on
T_{c}
is
2
-acylindrical. In particular, every non-elliptic abelian subgroup of
G
is infinite cyclic.
Hence
T_{c}
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree.

Assume now that
\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}\subset\mathcal{H}
.
We claim that
T
and
T_{c}
are in the same deformation space.
Since
T
dominates
T_{c}
, it suffices to prove that
T_{c}
dominates
T
.
Let
v
be a vertex of
T_{c}
.
We need to show that
G_{v}
is elliptic in
T
.
Since
T

\mathcal{A}
-dominates
T_{c}
, the group
G_{v}
either is elliptic in
T
or belongs to
\mathcal{A}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the only case to consider is when
G_{v}
is infinite cyclic.
Let
e
be an edge of
T_{c}
adjacent to
v
.
Since edge stabilizers are non-trivial,
G_{e}
is necessarily a finite index subgroup of
G_{v}
.
However
G_{e}
is elliptic in
T
, hence the same holds for
G_{v}
.
∎

By definition the class
\mathcal{A}
contains all cyclic subgroups of
G
.
Note that if
G
is free indecomposable relative to
\mathcal{H}
, then any
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
T
has non-trivial edge stabilizers, hence
\mathcal{A}
-dominates its tree of cylinders
T_{c}
(
Lemma 6.4
)
The next statement is now an application of Guirardel and Levitt
[
28
, Theorem 8.7]
for
C=1
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Assume that
G
is freely indecomposable relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
The JSJ deformation space of
G
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
exists.
Moreover every flexible vertex group is either abelian or QH with trivial fiber.

6.3
Compatibility

We still denote by
G
a CSA group that is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Two
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are
compatible
if they a have a common refinement (i.e. there exists an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
S
and two collapse maps
S\to T_{1}
and
S\to T_{2})
.
The goal of this section is to prove that if
G
does not split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
, then the tree of cylinders of its JSJ decomposition is compatible with any other
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
The result is well-known if
G
is one-ended, see
[
28
, Corollary 11.5]

As before, we briefly show that the same arguments actually work in this more general setting.

We start by recalling a few additional properties of trees of cylinders.
First, one checks from the definition that if
T
is an
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H})
-tree, then
(T_{c})_{c}=T_{c}
.

Proposition 6.6

(Guirardel-Levitt
[
27
, Corollary 4.10]
)
.

If
T
and
T^{\prime}
are two minimal non-trivial
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H})
-trees belonging to the same deformation space, there is a canonical equivariant isomorphism between their trees of cylinders.

More generally any
G
-equivariant map
f\colon T\to T^{\prime}
induces a cellular
G
-equivariant map
f_{c}\colon T_{c}\to T^{\prime}_{c}
, which actually does not depend on
f
.
(A cellular map is a map which sends a vertex to a vertex, an edge to a vertex or an edge.)
This construction is functorial in the sense that
(f\circ f^{\prime})_{c}=f_{c}\circ f^{\prime}_{c}

[
27
, Proposition 4.11]
.

Proposition 6.7

(Guirardel-Levitt
[
27
, Proposition 8.1]
)
.

If
T
and
T^{\prime}
are two minimal non-trivial
(\mathcal{A}^{*},\mathcal{H})
-trees.
If
T
dominates
T^{\prime}
, then
T_{c}
is compatible with
T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}_{c}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Assume that
G
does not split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
S
and
T
be two
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
Assume that every vertex stabilizer of
T
either belongs to
\mathcal{A}
or is QH with trivial fiber.
If
S
refines
T
, then
S_{c}
refines
T_{c}
.

Proof.

If
G
is one-ended relative to
\mathcal{H}
, it is a particular case of
[
28
, Lemma 7.15]
.
The proof follows the exact same steps.
The goal is to show that the map
f_{c}\colon S_{c}\to T_{c}
induced by a collapse map
f\colon S\to T
is actually a collapse map as well.

Trees in the same deformation space have the same tree of cylinders (
Proposition 6.6
).
Thus, we can assume that no proper collapse of
S
refining
T
belongs to the same deformation space as
S
.
In particular, for every vertex
v
in
T
, the pre-image
S_{v}\subset S
of
v
is minimal for the action of
G_{v}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of
T
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree obtained from
S
by collapsing every edge that is mapped to a vertex that does not belong to the orbit of
v
.
Note that
S_{v}
embeds in
S^{\prime}
, so that we can view
S_{v}
as a subtree of
S^{\prime}
.
It suffices to prove the following facts.

i.

The stabilizer of any vertex of
S^{\prime}
either belongs to
\mathcal{A}
or is QH with trivial fiber.

ii.

S^{\prime}_{c}
refines
T_{c}
.

The result then follows from an induction argument.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
S_{v}
is not reduced to a point (otherwise
S^{\prime}=T
).
Note that the stabilizer of any vertex in
S^{\prime}
is either a vertex stabilizer for the action of
G
on
T
(and thus belongs to
\mathcal{A}
or is QH with trivial fiber) or is conjugated to the stabilizer of a vertex in
S_{v}
.
Hence to get
(C2)((A3))i
it suffices to prove that every vertex stabilizer of
S_{v}
either belongs to
\mathcal{A}
or is QH with trivial fiber.
We distinguish two cases.

Assume first that
G_{v}\in\mathcal{A}
.
The stabilizer of any vertex in
S_{v}
is contained in
G_{v}
and thus belongs to
\mathcal{A}
.
Since
G
does not split as a free product, the stabilizer
G_{e}
of any edge
e
in
S_{v}
is non-trivial.
Moreover it is contained in
G_{v}
.
Hence
G_{e}\sim G_{v}
.
It follows that all the edges of
S_{v}
are contained in the same cylinder of
S^{\prime}
.
In particular,
G_{v}
is elliptic for its action on
S^{\prime}_{c}
. Consequently we have domination maps

S^{\prime}\to T\to S^{\prime}_{c}.

They induce cellular maps

S^{\prime}_{c}\to T_{c}\to(S^{\prime}_{c})_{c}=S^{\prime}_{c}.

which forces
T_{c}=S^{\prime}_{c}
.

Assume now that
v
is QH with trivial fiber.
We denote by
\Sigma
the underlying orbifold.
Every component of
\partial\Sigma
is used, see
[
28
, Lemma 5.15]
.
Indeed otherwise, by
[
28
, Lemma 5.16]

G
would split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Combining Lemma 5.18 and Proposition 5.21 of
[
28
]
one observes that the splitting of
G_{v}
induced by the action of
G_{v}
on
S_{v}
is dual to a family of essential simple closed geodesics
\mathcal{L}
on
\Sigma
.
Consequently every vertex stabilizer in
S_{v}
is QH with trivial fiber.

We now claim that every cylinder of
S^{\prime}
containing an edge in
S_{v}
is actually entirely contained in
S_{v}
.
Let
e
be an edge of
S_{v}
.
Let
e^{\prime}
be an equivalent edge of
S^{\prime}
, i.e. such that
G_{e}\sim G_{e^{\prime}}
.
Suppose that contrary to our claim,
e^{\prime}
is not contained in
S_{v}
.
Since cylinders are connected, we can assume that
e^{\prime}
has an endpoint in
S_{v}
and thus is not collapsed in
T
.
Recall that
G
does not split over the trivial group of
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Hence the subgroup
G_{e^{\prime}}
is either finite (but distinct from
\{1\}
and
\mathbf{Z}/2
) or has finite index in a boundary subgroup of
G_{v}=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
According to our previous discussion, there is an essential simple closed curve
\gamma\in\mathcal{L}
such that
G_{e}=\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}
.
Since
G_{e}\sim G_{e^{\prime}}
, the groups
G_{e^{\prime}}
and
G_{e}
commute.
This is impossible and completes the proof of our claim.

It follows from our claim that the map
S^{\prime}\to T
is either injective or constant when restricted to cylinders.
It implies that
S^{\prime}_{c}
refines
T_{c}
, see
[
27
, Remark 4.13]

∎

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
G
be a CSA group.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the collection of all its abelian subgroups.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a class of subgroups of
G
.
Assume that
G
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
and does not split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
S
be a JSJ tree of
G
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
The tree of cylinders
S_{c}
of
S
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree which is compatible with any
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.

Proof.

Consider an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
T
.
According to
[
28
, Lemma 2.8(1)]
,
S
has a refinement
S^{\prime}
that dominates
T
.
It follows from
Proposition 6.7
that
S^{\prime}_{c}
and
T
have a common refinement, say
R
.
However
S^{\prime}_{c}
also refines
S_{c}
(
Proposition 6.8
).
Hence
R
is a common refinement of
S_{c}
and
T
.
∎

6.4
Modular group

Let
G
be a CSA group.
As before
\mathcal{A}
stands for the class of all abelian subgroups of
G
, while
\mathcal{H}
is an arbitrary class of subgroups of
\mathcal{A}
.
We assume that
G
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
and does not split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.

Consider a one edge splitting of
G
of the form
G=A\ast_{C}B
or
G=A\ast_{C}
.
Let
u\in G\setminus\{1\}
be an element centralizing
C
.
A
Dehn twist
by
u
is an automorphism which fixes
A
and

—

conjugates
B
by
u
, in the amalgamated product case;

—

sends the stabler letter
t
to
tu
, in the HNN case.

Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
Given an edge
e
of
T
, a
Dehn twist over
e
is a Dehn twist in the one edge splitting obtained by collapsing all the edges of
T
which are not in the orbit of
e
.
Let
v
be a vertex of
T
.
Any automorphism
\alpha_{v}
of the vertex group
G_{v}
which acts by conjugacy on the adjacent edge groups can be extended to an automorphism
\alpha
of
G
.
We call
\alpha
the
natural extension
of
\alpha_{v}
.
Assume now that
G_{v}
is abelian.
Let
E_{v}
be the subgroup of
G_{v}
generated by the stabilizer of every edge in
T
starting at
v
.
The
peripheral subgroup
of
v
is the subgroup

K_{v}=\bigcap_{\varphi}\ker\varphi,

where
\varphi
runs over all morphisms
\varphi\colon G_{v}\to\mathbf{Z}
whose kernel contains
E_{v}
.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.
The
modular group of
G
associated to
T
, denoted by
\operatorname{Mod}(G,T)
, is the subgroup of the automorphism group
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(G\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}
generated by

—

inner automorphisms,

—

Dehn twists over edges of
T
,

—

natural extensions of automorphisms of QH vertex groups (called
surface type automorphisms
),

—

natural extensions of automorphisms of abelian vertex groups
G_{v}
that fix the peripheral subgroup
K_{v}
(called
generalized Dehn twists
).

If
S
is a JSJ tree of
G
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
and
S_{c}
its tree of cylinders, we simply write
\operatorname{Mod}(G)
for
\operatorname{Mod}(G,S_{c})
.
We say that
\operatorname{Mod}(G)
is the
modular group
of
G
(over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}
).

The next statement is a consequence of the compatibility of the JSJ tree (
Theorem 6.9
).

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
G
be a CSA group.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the collection of all its abelian subgroups.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a class of subgroups of
G
.
Assume that
G
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
and does not split over the trivial group or
\mathbf{Z}/2
relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
For every
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
T
, we have
\operatorname{Mod}(G,T)\subset\operatorname{Mod}(G)
.

7
Action on a limit tree

This section is the core of the article.
In view of
Proposition 4.6
, we need to understand non-abelian, divergent limit groups over the class
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
(for some fixed value of
\delta
).
In this section, we prove that such a limit group
(L,\eta)
admits an action on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree.
Although this action is not super-stable, we have enough control to decompose it into a graph of actions whose vertex actions are either peripheral, simplicial, axial or of Seifert type (
Theorem 7.44
).
Compared to similar results in the literature, the peripheral action is a new kind of vertex action that comes from the action of the groups
H\in\mathfrak{H}
around cone points.
The decomposition into a graph of actions provides a splitting
S
of
L
over abelian subgroups.
We prove that if
S
is not a generalized root splitting, then we can shorten the morphisms that were used to define
L
(
Theorem 7.46
).

7.1
Building a limit tree

A limit of actions.

Recall that energies and divergent limit groups have been defined in Definitions
3.6
and
4.3
respectively.
Let
G
be a group and
U
a finite generating set of
G
.
Let
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Let
(L,\eta)
be a
non-abelian
,
divergent
limit group over
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}
.
By definition, there exists a sequence of morphisms
\varphi_{k}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{k},X_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k})
converging to
(L,\eta)
, where the triple
(\Gamma_{k},X_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k})
belongs to
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\tilde{\rho}_{k})
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, and such that the sequence
(\tilde{\rho}_{k})
as well as the energy
\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)
diverge to infinity
2
2

2

We assumed here that
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
diverges to infinity to be coherent with the configuration we will handle later. However for the remainder of
Section 7
it would be enough to assume that
\rho_{k}\geqslant C\delta_{k}
for some fixed sufficiently large
C
.

Since
L
is not abelian, we can assume without loss of generality that the image of
\varphi_{k}
is not abelian, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
According to
Lemma 3.8
, the energies of
\varphi_{k}
are related by

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)\leqslant 2\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U).

In addition there exists a point
o_{k}\in X_{k}
with the following properties.

—

\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U,o_{k})\leqslant\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)+20\delta
,

—

o_{k}
is at a distance at most
\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)+20\delta
from a point (almost) minimizing the restricted energy
\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)
.

For the moment we prefer not to give a name to the latter point, as it may eventually change later (see
Lemma 7.21
).
For simplicity we let

\varepsilon_{k}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\infty}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)}.

It follows from our assumptions that
(\varepsilon_{k})
converges to zero.
In the remainder of this section, unless mentioned otherwise, we work with the rescaled space
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
, i.e. for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{k}

\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|_{\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}}}=\varepsilon_{k}\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|_{X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X_{k}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X_{k}}}.

We endow
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
with the action by isometries of
G
induced by
\varphi_{k}
.
This space is
\delta_{k}
-hyperbolic, where
\delta_{k}=\varepsilon_{k}\delta
converges to zero.
For simplicity we write
\rho_{k}=\varepsilon_{k}\tilde{\rho}_{k}
for the rescaled version of the parameter
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
, so that the ratio
\rho_{k}/\delta_{k}
diverges to infinity.

Asymptotic properties.

We fix a non-principal ultra-filter
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{N})\to\{0,1\}
.
Recall that a property
P_{k}
holds
\omega
-almost surely
(
\omega
-as) if

\omega(\mathchoice{\left\{k\in\mathbf{N}\;:\ P_{k}\ \text{holds}\right\}}{\{k\in\mathbf{N}\;:\ P_{k}\ \text{holds}\}}{\{k\in\mathbf{N}\;:\ P_{k}\ \text{holds}\}}{\{k\in\mathbf{N}\;:\ P_{k}\ \text{holds}\}})=1.

A real valued sequence
(u_{k})
is
\omega
-essentially bounded
(
\omega
-eb) if there exists
M\in\mathbf{R}
, such that
\mathchoice{\left|u_{k}\right|}{|u_{k}|}{|u_{k}|}{|u_{k}|}\leqslant M
,
\omega
-as.
It
converges to
\ell
along
\omega
, if for every
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|u_{k}-\ell\right|}{|u_{k}-\ell|}{|u_{k}-\ell|}{|u_{k}-\ell|}<\varepsilon
,
\omega
-as.

The next statements and proofs involve several asymptotic comparisons.
To that end, we adopt Landau’s notation.
Let
f,g\colon\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}
be two maps.
We write

—

f=o(g)
, if for every
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|f(k)\right|}{|f(k)|}{|f(k)|}{|f(k)|}\leqslant\varepsilon\mathchoice{\left|g(k)\right|}{|g(k)|}{|g(k)|}{|g(k)|}

\omega
-as;

—

f=O(g)
, if there exists
C>0
such that
\mathchoice{\left|f(k)\right|}{|f(k)|}{|f(k)|}{|f(k)|}\leqslant C\mathchoice{\left|g(k)\right|}{|g(k)|}{|g(k)|}{|g(k)|}

\omega
-as.

We say that
f
and
g
are
equivalent
and write
f\sim g
, if
f=g+o(g)
.
If there is no ambiguity, we make the following abuse of notation and write

f(k)=o\left(g(k)\right),\quad f(k)=O\left(g(k)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad f(k)\sim g(k).

Limit tree.

We consider now an ultra-limit of metric spaces.
We refer the reader to Druţu and Kapovich
[
20
, Chapter 10]
for a detailed exposition of this construction.
As the sequence
(\delta_{k})
converges to zero, the limit space

X_{\omega}=\lim_{\omega}\left(\varepsilon_{k}X_{k},o_{k}\right)

is an
\mathbf{R}
-tree.
The action of
G
on
X_{k}
induces an action without global fixed point of
G
on
X_{\omega}

[
45
]
.
Moreover the stable kernel of
(\varphi_{k})
acts trivially on
X_{\omega}
.
Consequently, the limit group
L
acts on
X_{\omega}
without global fixed point.

We denote by
T
the minimal
L
-invariant subtree of
X_{\omega}
.
Although the construction does depend on the sequence
(\Gamma_{k},\varphi_{k})
we call
T
the
limit tree of
(L,\eta)
.

Lemma 7.1
.

The basepoint
o_{k}\in X_{k}
is such that
\omega
-as

2\rho_{k}\leqslant 2d(o_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k})+1+20\delta_{k}

Proof.

Assume that contrary to our claim, there exists
c_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
such that
2\rho_{k}>2d(o_{k},c_{k})+1+20\delta_{k}
,
\omega
-as.
Since any generator
u\in U
moves
o_{k}
by at most
1+20\delta_{k}
, the triangle inequality yields
\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(u)c_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)c_{k}-c_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)c_{k}-c_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)c_{k}-c_{k}|}<2\rho_{k}
,
\omega
-as, for every
u\in U
.
Nevertheless two distinct cone points in
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
are at a distance at least
2\rho_{k}
far appart.
Consequently
\varphi_{k}(u)
fixes
c_{k}
, for every
u\in U
, and thus
\varphi_{k}(G)
is contained in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}

\omega
-as.
Combining
Lemma 3.4
and
(H2)
we observe that
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}
is abelian.
It follows that the image of
\varphi_{k}
is abelian
\omega
-as, which contradicts our assumption.
∎

Limit radius.

Note that we have no control a priori on the behavior of
(\rho_{k})
, beside the fact that
\rho_{k}/\delta_{k}
diverges to infinity.
Nevertheless we define

\rho=\lim_{\omega}\rho_{k}.

It belongs to
\mathbf{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
.

7.2
Peripheral structure

We endow the tree
X_{\omega}
with an additional structure that will keep track of the elliptic elements fixing apices from
\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
First let

\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{(c_{k})\in\Pi_{\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{C}_{k}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}-\rho_{k}\ \text{is $\omega$-eb}\right\}}{\{(c_{k})\in\Pi_{\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{C}_{k}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}-\rho_{k}\ \text{is $\omega$-eb}\}}{\{(c_{k})\in\Pi_{\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{C}_{k}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}-\rho_{k}\ \text{is $\omega$-eb}\}}{\{(c_{k})\in\Pi_{\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{C}_{k}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}-\rho_{k}\ \text{is $\omega$-eb}\}}.

We write
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
for the quotient of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
by the equivalence relation which identifies two sequences
(c_{k})
and
(c^{\prime}_{k})
if
c_{k}=c^{\prime}_{k}

\omega
-as.
Given a sequence
(c_{k})\in\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
, we write
c=[c_{k}]
for its image in
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
The action of
\Gamma_{k}
on
X_{k}
induces an action of
L
on
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.

Busemann functions.

Let
c=[c_{k}]
be a point in
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
We define a map
b_{c}\colon X_{\omega}\to\mathbf{R}
by sending
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
to

b_{c}(x)=\lim_{\omega}\left(\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\rho_{k}\right).

It follows from our definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
and the triangle inequality that the quantity
\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\rho_{k}
is
\omega
-eb.
Hence
b_{c}
is well defined.
Note also that the collection
(b_{c})
is
L
-invariant in the following sense:
b_{gc}(gx)=b_{c}(x)
, for all
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
,
x\in X_{\omega}
, and
g\in L
.
The map
b_{c}
can be seen as the
Busemann function
at a point of
X_{\omega}\cup\partial X_{\omega}
.
We distinguish two cases.

—

Assume first that
\rho
is
finite
.
By definition of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
the distance
\mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}
is
\omega
-eb.
Hence
\lim_{\omega}c_{k}
is a well-defined point in
X_{\omega}
, which we still denote by
c
.
One checks that
b_{c}(x)=\mathchoice{\left|c-x\right|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}-\rho
, for every
x\in X_{\omega}
.

—

Assume now that
\rho
is
infinite
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.1
that
d_{k}=\mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}{|o_{k}-c_{k}|}
diverges to infinity.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
\sigma_{k}\colon[0,d_{k}]\to X_{k}
for a
(1,\delta_{k})
-quasi-geodesic joining
o_{k}
to
c_{k}
.
Passing to the limit, we get a geodesic ray
\sigma\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X_{\omega}
starting at
o
, and sending
t
to
\lim_{\omega}\sigma_{k}(t)
.
We denote by
\xi\in\partial X_{\omega}
the endpoint at infinity of
\sigma
.
Let
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
be a point of
X_{\omega}
.
It is a standard exercise of hyperbolic geometry to prove that for every
t\geqslant\mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}
, we have

\mathchoice{\left|\left(\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}\right)-\left(\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}\right)\right|}{|\left(\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}\right)-\left(\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}\right)|}{|\left(\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}\right)-\left(\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}\right)|}{|\left(\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}{|c_{k}-o_{k}|}\right)-\left(\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-x_{k}|}-\mathchoice{\left|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}\right|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}{|\sigma_{k}(t)-o_{k}|}\right)|}\leqslant 100\delta_{k}

Passing to the limit we get

b_{c}(x)-b_{c}(o)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\mathchoice{\left|\sigma(t)-x\right|}{|\sigma(t)-x|}{|\sigma(t)-x|}{|\sigma(t)-x|}-\mathchoice{\left|\sigma(t)-o\right|}{|\sigma(t)-o|}{|\sigma(t)-o|}{|\sigma(t)-o|}\right).

Consequently
b_{c}
is the Busemann function at
\xi
.

As a Busemann function in a tree,
b_{c}
is convex and
1
-Lipschitz.

Peripheral subtrees.

Let
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
c=[c_{k}]
be a point in
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
We define the
open
and
closed

peripheral subtrees
centered at
c
by

P(c,r)=\mathchoice{\left\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)<-r\right\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)<-r\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)<-r\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)<-r\}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{P}(c,r)=\mathchoice{\left\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)\leqslant-r\right\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)\leqslant-r\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)\leqslant-r\}}{\{x\in X_{\omega}\;:\ b_{c}(x)\leqslant-r\}}.

For simplicity, we write
P(c)
and
\bar{P}(c)
for
P(c,0)
and
\bar{P}(c,0)
respectively.
Peripheral subtrees are
strictly convex
, i.e. if
x
and
y
are two points in
\bar{P}(c,r)
, then the interior of the geodesic
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
is contained in
P(c,r)
.
As
(b_{c})
is
L
-invariant, we get that
gP(c,r)=P(gc,r)
, for every
g\in L
.
The same statement holds for closed peripheral subtrees.

Vocabulary
.

A subtree of
X_{\omega}
is
non-degenerate
, if it contains at least two distinct points.
It is
transverse
if its intersection with any peripheral subtree is degenerated.

Let us now give another description of peripheral subtrees.
Given a collection
(Y_{k})
where each
Y_{k}
is a subset of
X_{k}
, we define
\lim_{\omega}Y_{k}
as the subset of
X_{\omega}
given by

\lim_{\omega}Y_{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}\;:\ y_{k}\in Y_{k}\ \omega\text{-as}\right\}}{\{y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}\;:\ y_{k}\in Y_{k}\ \omega\text{-as}\}}{\{y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}\;:\ y_{k}\in Y_{k}\ \omega\text{-as}\}}{\{y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}\;:\ y_{k}\in Y_{k}\ \omega\text{-as}\}}.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
c=[c_{k}]
be a point in
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
For every
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we have

\bar{P}(c,r)=\lim_{\omega}B\left(c_{k},\rho_{k}-r\right).

Proof.

Let
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
For simplicity, we denote by
B\subset X_{\omega}
the limit of the balls
B(c_{k},\rho_{k}-r)
.
The inclusion
B\subset\bar{P}(c,r)
is straightforward.
Let us prove the converse inclusion.
Let
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
be a point of
X_{\omega}
such that
b_{c}(x)\leqslant-r
.
In particular, there is a sequence of positive numbers
(t_{k})
that converges to zero and such that

\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}<\rho_{k}-r+t_{k},\ \omega\text{-as}.

Recall that
X_{k}
is a length space, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Thus there exist points
x^{\prime}_{k}\in B(c_{k},\rho_{k}-r)
such that
\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}<t_{k}

\omega
-as.
In particular,
x=\lim_{\omega}x^{\prime}_{k}
.
Thus
x
belongs to
B
.
∎

We now interpret the distance in a peripheral subtree in terms of the asymptotic behavior of the angle cocycle given by Axiom
(H5)
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
c=[c_{k}]
be a point in
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by

\theta_{k}\colon\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\times\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta_{k}\mathbf{Z}

the angle cocycle provided by Axiom
(H5)
.
In addition, we choose sequences
x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})
and let
d=\lim_{\omega}\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}
.
Assume that there is
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, such that

\lim_{\omega}\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}-\rho_{k}=\lim_{\omega}\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}-\rho_{k}=-r.

i.

If
d+2r<2\rho
, then
\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)
converges to zero.
More precisely

\ln\mathchoice{\left|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)\right|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}\leqslant-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{d}{2\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),

with equality whenever
d\neq 0
.

ii.

Conversely, if there exists
\ell\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that
\ell+2r<2\rho
and

\ln\mathchoice{\left|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)\right|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}\leqslant-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{\ell}{2\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),

then
d\leqslant\ell
.

Remark 7.4
.

Let us make a few comments on this statement.
We keep the same notation.

—

Recall that
\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})
stands for the unique representative of
\theta_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})
in
(-\Theta_{k}/2,\Theta_{k}/2]
.

—

If
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
and
x^{\prime}=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
are two well-defined points of
X_{\omega}
, then our assumption can be reformulated as

b_{c}(x)=b_{c}(x^{\prime})=-r\quad\text{and}\quad\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}+2r<2\rho.

Nevertheless this more general form will be useful later when the sequences
\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}
and
\mathchoice{\left|x^{\prime}_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|x^{\prime}_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x^{\prime}_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x^{\prime}_{k}-o_{k}|}
may not be
\omega
-eb.

—

It follows from the triangle inequality that
d+2r\leqslant 2\rho
.
The parameter
\rho=\lim_{\omega}\rho_{k}
can be infinite, in which case the assumption
d+2r<2\rho
is automatically satisfied, provided
d
is finite.

—

By convention
\ln(0)=-\infty
, so that the inequalities hold even if
\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)
vanishes.

Proof.

Recall that, in this section, all distances are measured with the
rescaled
metric of the spaces
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
.
For simplicity we let

s_{k}=\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x_{k}|}\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}_{k}=\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|},

so that

s_{k}=\rho_{k}-r+o(1)\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}_{k}=\rho_{k}-r+o(1)

(12)

In particular,
\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}=o(1)
.
In addition, we let

\beta_{k}=\min\left\{\pi,\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})\right\},

and define the quantity
d_{k}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
by the relation

\operatorname{ch}\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)=\operatorname{ch}\left(\frac{s_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\operatorname{ch}\left(\frac{s^{\prime}_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)-\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{s_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{s^{\prime}_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\cos\beta_{k}.

(13)

It follows from Axiom
(H5)
that

d_{k}=\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}+o(1)=d+o(1).

(Equation (
13
) is the law of cosine in
\mathbf{H}^{2}
.
The triangle inequality in the hyperbolic plane implies in particular that
\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}\leqslant d_{k}
.)
A trigonometric computation shows that (
13
) is equivalent to

\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{s_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{s^{\prime}_{k}}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\beta_{k}}{2}\right)=\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{d_{k}+\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\frac{d_{k}-\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\right).

(14)

Note that
(s_{k})
and
(s^{\prime}_{k})
converge to
\rho-r
, which is assume to be positive both in
(C2)((A3))i
and
(C2)((A3))ii
.
Hence
(s_{k}/\varepsilon_{k})
and
(s^{\prime}_{k}/\varepsilon_{k})
diverge to infinity.
If
d>0
, then both sequences

\left(\frac{d_{k}+\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\frac{d_{k}-\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\right)

diverge to infinity.
This follows indeed from the fact that
(\varepsilon_{k})
converges to zero while
\mathchoice{\left|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}{|s_{k}-s^{\prime}_{k}|}=o(1)
.
Using the fact that
t\mapsto\ln\operatorname{sh}(t)-t
is bounded in a neighborhood of infinity, (
14
) becomes

\ln\circ\sin\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}}{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\left(s_{k}+s^{\prime}_{k}-d_{k}\right)+O(1).

Recall that
\ln\operatorname{sh}(t)\leqslant t
for every
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
If
d=0
, we can use this inequality to bound from above the right hand side of (
14
).
We get

\ln\circ\sin\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}}{2}\right)\leqslant-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\left(s_{k}+s^{\prime}_{k}-d_{k}\right)+O(1).

Plugging in the asymptotic behaviors of
s_{k}
and
s^{\prime}_{k}
given by (
12
) as well as the one of
d_{k}
we obtain

\ln\circ\sin\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}}{2}\right)\leqslant-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{d}{2\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),

(15)

with equality whenever
d>0
.

Assume now that
d+2r<2\rho
.
It follows that the right hand side of the previous inequality diverges to
-\infty
.
In particular,
\beta_{k}
and thus
\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})
converges to zero, moreover

\ln\circ\sin\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}}{2}\right)=\ln\mathchoice{\left|\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})\right|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})|}+O(1).

(16)

Combining the previous two identities leads to Point
(C2)((A3))i
.

Assume now that there exists
\ell\geqslant 0
such that
\ell+2r<2\rho
and

\ln\mathchoice{\left|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)\right|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}{|\tilde{\theta}_{k}\left(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}\right)|}\leqslant-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{\ell}{2\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),

Without loss of generality we can assume that
d>0
.
Otherwise the result is straightforward.
Since
\ell+2r<2\rho
, the right hand side of the previous inequality diverges to
-\infty
.
In particular,
\tilde{\theta}_{k}(x_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})
converges to zero and (
16
) still holds.
As
d>0
, Inequality (
15
) is actually an equality.
Combining these observations we get

-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{d}{2\varepsilon_{k}}-o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\leqslant\ln\circ\sin\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}}{2}\right)\leqslant-\frac{\rho_{k}-r}{\varepsilon_{k}}+\frac{\ell}{2\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right).

Hence
d\leqslant\ell
, which completes the proof of Point
(C2)((A3))ii
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
The following statements are equivalent.

i.

c=c^{\prime}

ii.

\bar{P}(c)
and
\bar{P}(c^{\prime})
have a non-degenerate intersection.

iii.

P(c)
and
P(c^{\prime})
have a non-empty intersection.

Proof.

The implication
(C2)((A3))i

\Rightarrow

(C2)((A3))ii
is trivial.
Assume that
\bar{P}(c)\cap\bar{P}(c^{\prime})
is non-degenerate.
Since balls/horoballs are strictly quasi-convex,
P(c)\cap P(c^{\prime})
is non-empty hence
(C2)((A3))ii

\Rightarrow

(C2)((A3))iii
holds.
Suppose now that
P(c)\cap P(c^{\prime})
is non-empty.
We write
c=[c_{k}]
and
c^{\prime}=[c^{\prime}_{k}]
where
(c_{k})
and
(c^{\prime}_{k})
are sequences of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Combining
Lemma 7.2
with the triangle inequality, one observes that
\mathchoice{\left|c_{k}-c^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|c_{k}-c^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-c^{\prime}_{k}|}{|c_{k}-c^{\prime}_{k}|}<2\rho_{k}

\omega
-as.
Recall that any two distincts apices in
\mathcal{C}_{k}
are at a distance at least
2\rho_{k}
far apart.
Thus
c_{k}=c^{\prime}_{k}

\omega
-as, i.e.
c=c^{\prime}
.
∎
