Lemma 7.6
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
and
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Let
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{\omega}\setminus P(c)
.
If
\mathchoice{\left[x,x^{\prime}\right]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}
intersects
\bar{P}(c,r)
, then
\mathchoice{\left[x,x^{\prime}\right]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is an geodesic of length at least
2r
.

Proof.

Since
\bar{P}(c)
is closed subtree, the intersection
\mathchoice{\left[x,x^{\prime}\right]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is a geodesic.
Fix a point
z\in\mathchoice{\left[x,x^{\prime}\right]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}{[x,x^{\prime}]}
such that
b_{c}(z)\leqslant-r
.
We denote by
y
a point of
\mathchoice{\left[z,x\right]}{[z,x]}{[z,x]}{[z,x]}
such that
b_{c}(y)=0
.
Such a point exists as
b_{c}
is continuous.
The point
y^{\prime}
on
\mathchoice{\left[z,x^{\prime}\right]}{[z,x^{\prime}]}{[z,x^{\prime}]}{[z,x^{\prime}]}
is defined in a similar way.
By construction
\mathchoice{\left[y,y^{\prime}\right]}{[y,y^{\prime}]}{[y,y^{\prime}]}{[y,y^{\prime}]}
is contained in
\bar{P}(c)
.
Since
b_{c}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have
\mathchoice{\left|z-y\right|}{|z-y|}{|z-y|}{|z-y|}\geqslant\mathchoice{\left|b_{c}(y)-b_{c}(z)\right|}{|b_{c}(y)-b_{c}(z)|}{|b_{c}(y)-b_{c}(z)|}{|b_{c}(y)-b_{c}(z)|}
, that is
\mathchoice{\left|z-y\right|}{|z-y|}{|z-y|}{|z-y|}\geqslant r
.
Similarly
\mathchoice{\left|z-y^{\prime}\right|}{|z-y^{\prime}|}{|z-y^{\prime}|}{|z-y^{\prime}|}\geqslant r
.
Hence the result.
∎

7.3
Action of the limit group

In this section we study the action of
L
on the
\mathbf{R}
-tree
X_{\omega}
built above.
Recall that all the groups
\Gamma_{k}
are CSA
[
40
]
.
Since CSA is a closed property for the topology or marked groups,
L
is CSA as well.

Definition 7.7
.

An element
g\in L
is
elusive
if given any pre-image
\tilde{g}\in G
of
g
, the element
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})\in\Gamma_{k}
is elusive
\omega
-as.
An element of
L
is
visible
if it is not elusive.
A subgroup
L
is
elusive
if all its elements are elusive.
It is
visible
otherwise.

Elliptic elements.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
g\in G
.
If
\varphi_{k}(g)
is elliptic (for its action on
X_{k}
)
\omega
-as, then
\eta(g)
is elliptic (for its action on
X_{\omega}
).

Proof.

Let
x_{k}
be a
\delta_{k}
-projection of
o_{k}
onto
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi_{k}(g),6\delta_{k})
.
It follows from (
7
) that

\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-\varphi_{k}(g)o_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-\varphi_{k}(g)o_{k}|}{|o_{k}-\varphi_{k}(g)o_{k}|}{|o_{k}-\varphi_{k}(g)o_{k}|}+3\delta_{k}.

Consequently
\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-o_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}{|x_{k}-o_{k}|}
is
\omega
-eb, thus
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
is a well-defined point of
X_{\omega}
.
Moreover
\eta(g)
fixes
x
.
∎

Elusive subgroups.

Lemma 7.9
.

Every non-trivial elusive element
g\in L
fixes a single point in
X_{\omega}
.
In particular, elusive subgroups are elliptic in
X_{\omega}
.

Proof.

We know by
Lemma 7.8
that
g
is elliptic.
Let us prove that it has a unique fix point.
Let
\tilde{g}\in G
a pre-image of
g
.
Let
y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}
and
z=\lim_{\omega}z_{k}
be two points fixed by
g
.
There exists a sequence
(d_{k})
converging to zero such that
y_{k}
and
z_{k}
belongs to
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}),d_{k})

\omega
-as.
By Axiom
(H3)
the diameter of
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}),d_{k})
is bounded above by
d_{k}+2\delta_{k}

\omega
-as.
Hence
y=z
, which proves the first part of our statement.

Let
H
be an elusive subgroup of
L
.
If
H
is not elliptic, then there exist
u_{1},u_{2}\in H
whose unique fixed point in
X_{\omega}
are distinct.
It follows that
u_{1}u_{2}
is loxodromic in
X_{\omega}
, hence cannot be elusive.
∎

Cone point stabilizers.

For every
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
, we write
L_{c}
for its stabilizer in
L
.

Lemma 7.10

(Cone point stabilizers are radial)
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
Every element
g\in L_{c}
is elliptic.
Moreover if
g
is non trivial, then there exists a finite
r\in\mathchoice{\left[0\,,\rho\right]}{[0\,,\rho]}{[0\,,\rho]}{[0\,,\rho]}
such that
\operatorname{Fix}(g)=\bar{P}(c,r)
.

Proof.

We write
c=[c_{k}]
where
(c_{k})
is a sequence of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Let
g\in L_{c}
and
\tilde{g}\in G
a pre-image of
g
.
It follows from the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
belongs to
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}

\omega
-as.
Hence
g
is elliptic by
Lemma 7.8
.

Assume now that
g
is not trivial.
We first prove that
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is contained in
\bar{P}(c)
.
Let
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
be a point in
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
.
By definition there exists a sequence
(d_{k})
converging to zero such that
x_{k}\in\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}),d_{k})

\omega
-as.
Since
g
is non trivial, so is
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})

\omega
-as.
In particular,
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is
(\rho_{k}+\delta_{k})
-thin at
c_{k}
by Axiom
(H4)
, thus

\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-c_{k}|}{|x_{k}-c_{k}|}{|x_{k}-c_{k}|}\leqslant\rho_{k}+d_{k}/2+\delta_{k},\ \omega\text{-as}.

Hence
b_{c}(x)\leqslant 0
as announced. We now let

r=\inf\mathchoice{\left\{-b_{c}(x)\;:\ gx=x,\ x\in X_{\omega}\right\}}{\{-b_{c}(x)\;:\ gx=x,\ x\in X_{\omega}\}}{\{-b_{c}(x)\;:\ gx=x,\ x\in X_{\omega}\}}{\{-b_{c}(x)\;:\ gx=x,\ x\in X_{\omega}\}}

It follows from our previous discussion that
r
is a non negative finite number.
Note also that
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is contained in
\bar{P}(c,r)
.
We now focus on the other inclusion.
We distinguish two cases.

Case 1.

Assume that
r=\rho
.
In particular,
\rho
is finite and
c=\lim_{\omega}c_{k}
is a well-defined point of
X_{\omega}
, which is fixed by
g
.
In addition
\bar{P}(c,\rho)
is reduced to
\{c\}
, hence is contained in
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
.

Case 2.

Assume that
r\in[0,\rho)
.

For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by

\theta_{k}\colon\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\times\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta_{k}\mathbf{Z}

the angle cocycle provided by Axiom
(H5)
.
Since
\theta_{k}
is a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}
-invariant cocycle, the quantity
\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k},x_{k})
does not depend on the point
x_{k}
.
For simplicity we denote by
\beta_{k}
its unique representative in
(-\Theta_{k}/2,\Theta_{k}/2]
.
By definition of
r
, for every
s>r
, the element
g
fixes a point
x\in X_{\omega}
, with
-b_{c}(x)\leqslant s
.
Since
2r<2\rho
, it follows from
Lemma 7.3
(C2)((A3))i
that the sequence

\varepsilon_{k}\ln\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}+\rho_{k}

admits an
\omega
-limit in
[-\infty,r]
.
Using
Lemma 7.3
(C2)((A3))ii
, we get that
\bar{P}(c,r)
is contained in
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
.
∎

Combined with
Lemma 7.5
, it yields the following statement.

Corollary 7.11
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
If
c\neq c^{\prime}
, then
L_{c}\cap L_{c^{\prime}}
is trivial.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
The global stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c)
coincides with
L_{c}
.
It is a maximal abelian subgroup, provided it is not trivial.

Proof.

We write
c=[c_{k}]
where
(c_{k})
is a sequence of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
The first claim is a direct consequence of
Lemma 7.5
.
Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in L_{c}
and
\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2}\in G
respective pre-images of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
It follows from the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
, that
\varphi_{k}(g_{i})c_{k}=c_{k}

\omega
-as.
According to Axiom
(H2)
,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}
is abelian.
Hence
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{1})
and
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{2})
commute
\omega
-as, thus so do
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.

Assume now that
L_{c}
is non trivial.
Let
g_{0}\in L_{c}\setminus\{1\}
.
Let
g\in L
which commutes with
g_{0}
.
In particular,
g_{0}
fixes
gc
.
According to
Corollary 7.11
,
gc=c
.
Hence every abelian subgroup containing
g_{0}
is contained in
L_{c}
.
∎

Proposition 7.13

(Root stability)
.

Let
g\in L\setminus\{1\}
.
If
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is non-degenerate, then for every
m\in\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, we have
\operatorname{Fix}(g)=\operatorname{Fix}(g^{m})
.
Moreover,
g
has infinite order.

Proof.

We fix a pre-image
\tilde{g}\in G
of
g
.
The set
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is contained in
\operatorname{Fix}(g^{m})
.
Let
x=\lim_{\omega}{x_{k}}
be a point in
\operatorname{Fix}(g^{m})
.
In order to show that
x\in\operatorname{Fix}(g)
, it suffices to establish that

\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k}-x_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k}-x_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k}-x_{k}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{m})x_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{m})x_{k}-x_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{m})x_{k}-x_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{m})x_{k}-x_{k}|}+1000\delta_{k},\ \omega\text{-as}.

(17)

We distinguish two cases.
Assume first that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is loxodromic
\omega
-as.
Then (
17
) is a standard exercise of hyperbolic geometry.
Note also that
g
cannot have finite order.
Indeed, otherwise we could find
p\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{p})=1

\omega
-as, which contradicts the fact that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is loxodromic
\omega
-as.

Assume now that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is elliptic
\omega
-as.
Note that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
cannot be elusive
\omega
-as, otherwise
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
would be reduced to a point (
Lemma 7.9
).
Hence
g
fixes a cone point
c=[c_{k}]
where
(c_{k})
is a sequence of
\Pi_{\omega}\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by

\theta_{k}\colon\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\times\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta_{k}\mathbf{Z}

the angle cocycle provided by Axiom
(H5)
.
Since
\theta_{k}
is a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}{\operatorname{Stab}(c_{k})}
-invariant co-cycle, the quantity
\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k},x_{k})
does not depend on the point
x_{k}\in\mathring{B}(c_{k},\rho_{k})
.
For simplicity we denote by
\beta_{k}
its unique representative in
(-\Theta_{k}/2,\Theta_{k}/2]
.
Since
g
is non-trivial and
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
non-degenerate, the latter has the form
\operatorname{Fix}(g)=\bar{P}(c,r)
with
r\in[0,\rho)
(
Lemma 7.10
).
It follows from
Lemma 7.3
that
(\beta_{k})
converges to zero.
Thus
m\beta_{k}
is the unique representative in
(-\Theta_{k}/2,\Theta_{k}/2]
of

\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{m})x_{k},x_{k})=m\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k},x_{k}).

Since
\mathchoice{\left|\beta_{k}\right|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}{|\beta_{k}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|m\beta_{k}\right|}{|m\beta_{k}|}{|m\beta_{k}|}{|m\beta_{k}|}\leqslant\pi
, Inequality (
17
) follows from Axiom
(H5)
.
Assume now that
g
has finite order.
There exists
p\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{p})=1

\omega
-as.
Using the cocycle
\theta_{k}
we get

0=\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}^{p})x_{k},x_{k})=p\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k},x_{k}).

Hence
\theta_{k}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})x_{k},x_{k})=0
.
It follows from
(H5)
that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})=1

\omega
-as, which contradicts our assumption.
∎

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
and
r,s\in[0,\rho)
.
Let
A_{r}
and
A_{s}
be the respective pointwise stabilizers of
\bar{P}(c,r)
and
\bar{P}(c,s)
.
If
r<s
, then the quotient
A_{s}/A_{r}
is torsion-free.

Arcs and tripods.

An
arc
is a subset homeomorphic to
[0,1]
.
A subtree
Y
of
X_{\omega}
is
stable
, if for every arc
I\subset Y
, the pointwise stabilizers of
I
and
Y
coincide.
The action of
G
on
X_{\omega}
is
super-stable
if any arc with non-trivial stabilizer is stable.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
[x,y]
be an arc in
X_{\omega}
.
Assume that its pointwise stabilizer
H
is non-trivial.
Then
H
is torsion-free and abelian.
Moreover, all its non-trivial elements are visible.

Proof.

We write
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
and
y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.9
that every element in
H\setminus\{1\}
is visible.
Moreover
H
is torsion-free (
Proposition 7.13
).
Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in H\setminus\{1\}
.
Let
\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2}\in G
be respective pre-images of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
By definition there exists a sequence
(d_{k})
converging to zero such that
x_{k},y_{k}\in\operatorname{Fix}(W_{k},d_{k})
, where
W_{k}=\{\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{1}),\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{2})\}
.
If
W_{k}
does not generate an elementary subgroup, then
(H1)
tells us that

\mathchoice{\left|x_{k}-y_{k}\right|}{|x_{k}-y_{k}|}{|x_{k}-y_{k}|}{|x_{k}-y_{k}|}\leqslant\operatorname{diam}\left(\operatorname{Fix}(W_{k},d_{k})\right)\leqslant 4d_{k}+\delta_{k}.

Passing to the limit we get
x=y
which contradicts our assumption.
The set
W_{k}
generates an elementary subgroup which is not elusive, hence abelian by
(H2)
.
Consequently
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{1})
and
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}_{2})
commute, hence so do
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
x
,
y
,
z
be three points of
X_{\omega}
.
We assume that they are not contained in a single peripheral subtree and they do not lie on a transverse geodesic.
Then the pointwise stabilizer of the tripod
[x,y,z]
is trivial.

Proof.

We write
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
,
y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}
and
z=\lim_{\omega}z_{k}
.
Assume that there exists
g\in L\setminus\{1\}
fixing
x
,
y
and
z
.
Let
\tilde{g}\in G
be a pre-image of
g
.
As usual there exists a sequence
(d_{k})
converging to zero such that
x_{k}
,
y_{k}
and
z_{k}
belong to
\operatorname{Fix}(\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g}),d_{k})
.
We distinguish two cases.

—

Assume first that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is elliptic
\omega
-as.
As
g
is not trivial,
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is non-trivial
\omega
-as.
If
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is elusive
\omega
-as, then
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is reduced to a single point (
Lemma 7.9
).
If
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is conical
\omega
-as, then it fixes some
c_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Hence
g
fixes
c=[c_{k}]
and
\operatorname{Fix}(g)
is contained in
\bar{P}(c)
(
Lemma 7.10
).
In both cases
x
,
y
and
z
belong to a single peripheral subtree, or they lie on a (degenerated) transverse geodesic.

—

Assume now that
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is loxodromic
\omega
-as.
We denote by
\xi_{k}^{-},\xi_{k}^{+}\in\partial X_{k}
the repulsive and attractive points of
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
.
It follows from Axiom
(H6)
that for every
t\in\{x_{k},y_{k},z_{k}\}
we have
\mathchoice{\left\langle\xi_{k}^{-},\xi_{k}^{+}\right\rangle_{t}}{\langle\xi_{k}^{-},\xi_{k}^{+}\rangle_{t}}{\langle\xi_{k}^{-},\xi_{k}^{+}\rangle_{t}}{\langle\xi_{k}^{-},\xi_{k}^{+}\rangle_{t}}\leqslant d_{k}/2+\delta_{k}
.
Hence up to permuting
x
,
y
and
z
, we observe that

\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{k},z_{k}\right\rangle_{y_{k}}}{\langle x_{k},z_{k}\rangle_{y_{k}}}{\langle x_{k},z_{k}\rangle_{y_{k}}}{\langle x_{k},z_{k}\rangle_{y_{k}}}\leqslant 3d_{k}/2+100\delta_{k},\ \omega\text{-as}.

Hence
y
lies on the geodesic
\mathchoice{\left[x,z\right]}{[x,z]}{[x,z]}{[x,z]}
.
We now claim that this geodesic is transverse.
Assume it is not.
Since
g
fixes pointwise this geodesic, it fixes a point in
P(c)
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
Hence by
Lemma 7.5
,
g
fixes
c
and
\varphi_{k}(\tilde{g})
is elliptic
\omega
-as, which contradicts our assumption. ∎

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
be an arc of
X_{\omega}
which is not contained in a peripheral subtree.
If its pointwise stabilizer is non trivial, then it is stable.

Remark 7.18
.

It follows from the above statement that if
\rho=0
, then the action of
L
on
X_{\omega}
is super-stable (i.e. any arc with non-trivial stabilizer is stable).
Similarly, if
H
is a subgroup of
L
preserving a transverse subtree
Y\subset X_{\omega}
, then the action of
H
on
Y
is super-stable.

Proof.

The proof goes as in
[
47
, Proposition 4.2]
.
Let
\mathchoice{\left[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}
be an arc contained in
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y
are ordered in this way on
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
.
Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be the pointwise stabilizers of
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
and
\mathchoice{\left[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}
respectively, so that
H
is contained in
H^{\prime}
.
Let
g^{\prime}
be an element of
H^{\prime}
.
By assumption
H
contains a non trivial element, say
g
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.15
that
g
and
g^{\prime}
commutes.
Hence
g
fixes
x
,
y
and
g^{\prime}y
.
According to our assumption,
x
and
y
are not in the same peripheral subtree.
Hence
Lemma 7.16
, tells us that
x
,
y
and
g^{\prime}y
lie on a (transverse) geodesic, which forces
g^{\prime}y=y
.
We prove in the same way that
g^{\prime}x=x
, hence
g^{\prime}
belongs to
H
.
∎

Small subgroups.

We say that a subgroup
H
of
L
is
small
(for its action on
X_{\omega}
) if it does not contain two elements acting loxodromically on
X_{\omega}
whose corresponding axes have a bounded intersection.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
H
be a small subgroup of
L
.
If
H
is not elliptic (for its action on
X_{\omega}
), then
H
is visible and abelian.
In addition, if
H
is not cyclic, then either there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
H
is contained in
L_{c}
or
H
preserves a transverse subtree.

Proof.

According to the classification of actions on trees, either
H
fixes a point in the boundary at infinity
\partial X_{\omega}
of
X_{\omega}
or
H
preserves a bi-infinite geodesic.
In both cases, it contains a non-trivial subgroup
H^{+}
of index at most
2
which fixes a point in
\xi\in\partial X_{\omega}
.
Moreover, if
[H:H^{+}]=2
, then
H
cannot be abelian.
Let
\sigma\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X_{\omega}
be a geodesic ray pointing to
\xi
.
If
\beta_{\xi}\colon X_{\omega}\to\mathbf{R}
, is the Busemann cocycle at
\xi
, then the map
\psi\colon H^{+}\to\mathbf{R}
sending
g
to
\beta_{\xi}(go,o)
is a homomorphism whose kernel
H_{0}^{+}
is exactly the set of elliptic elements of
H^{+}
.

Let us first show that
H
is abelian and visible.
We focus first on
H_{0}^{+}
.
For every
g\in H_{0}^{+}
, there exists
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that
g
fixes pointwise
\sigma
restricted to
[t,\infty)
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.15
that non-trivial elements of
H_{0}^{+}
are visible and pairwise commutes.
Suppose now that
H_{0}^{+}
is not trivial.
Note that
H
normalizes
H^{+}_{0}
.
Since
L
is CSA, it follows that
H
is abelian and visible.
If
H_{0}^{+}
is trivial, then
H^{+}
is isomorphic to its image under
\psi
, thus is abelian.
In addition, it cannot be elusive, otherwise
H
would be elliptic, thus
H^{+}
is abelian and visible.
Note that
H^{+}
is a normal subgroup of
H
.
Using again the fact that
L
is CSA, we conclude that
H
is abelian and visible.
As we observe earlier it implies that
H=H^{+}
, hence
H
fixes
\xi
.

Suppose that there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
H\cap L_{c}
is not trivial.
Since
L_{c}
is a maximal abelian subgroup (
Lemma 7.12
)
H
is contained in
L_{c}
.
From now on we assume that
H\cap L_{c}
is trivial for every
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
We are going to prove that either
H
preserves a transverse subtree or
H
is cyclic.
We distinguish two cases.
Suppose that there exists
t\in\mathbf{R}
such that
\sigma
restricted to
[t,\infty)
is contained in a transverse subtree, say
Y
.
Since
H
fixes
\xi
, for every
g\in H
,
g\sigma\cap\sigma
is an infinity ray.
It follows that
H
preserves
Y
.

Suppose now that for every
t\in\mathbf{R}
, the ray
\sigma
restricted to
[t,\infty)
is not transverse.
We claim that
H_{0}^{+}
is trivial.
Indeed, otherwise we could find
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
and
g\in H_{0}^{+}\setminus\{1\}
such that
g
fixes point in
\sigma\cap P(c)
.
In particular,
g
would belong to
H\cap L_{c}
(
Lemma 7.5
) which contradicts our assumption.
It follows from our claim that all elements of
H
are loxodromic.
Since
H
is abelian, it necessarily preserves a bi-infinite geodesic
\mu\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
.
Note that
\sigma\cap\mu
is a geodesic ray whose endpoint at infinity is
\xi
.
In particular,
\mu
is not transverse either.
There exists a cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
\mu\cap\bar{P}(c)
contains a bounded arc, say
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
.
Since
H\cap L_{c}
is trivial,
g\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}\cap\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
is degenerated, for every
g\in H\setminus\{1\}
.
It follows that
\mathchoice{\left\|g\right\|}{\|g\|}{\|g\|}{\|g\|}\geqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}>0
.
As all the translation lengths of the non-trivial elements of
H
are bounded from below,
H
is cyclic.
∎

7.4
Decomposition of the action

Recall that
T
is the minimal
L
-invariant subtree of
X_{\omega}
.
The goal of this section is to decompose the action of
L
on
T
into well understood “elementary bricks”, see
Theorem 7.44
.
We denote by
\mathcal{A}
( respectively
\mathcal{H}
) the class of all abelian ( respectively elusive) subgroups of
L
.
In addition
\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}
stands for the collection of all non-cyclic abelian subgroups of
L
.
From now on, we make the following additional assumption

Assumption 7.20
.

The group
L
is freely indecomposable relative to
\mathcal{H}
.

It is a standard fact that the basepoint
o=\lim_{\omega}o_{k}
belongs to the minimal subtree
T
.
However if
(x_{k})
is a sequence of points minimizing the restricted energy
\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)
there is a priori no reason, first that
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
is a well-defined point of
X_{\omega}
, and second that it belongs to
T
.
This is the purpose of the next statement.

Lemma 7.21
.

For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists a point
o^{+}_{k}\in X^{+}_{k}
such that

i.

the limit
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
is a well defined point of
X_{\omega}
which belongs to
T
.

ii.

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k})=\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)
.

Remark 7.22
.

Unlike in the rest of this section, the distance used to compute the energies
\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k})
and
\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)
is the one of
X_{k}
and not its rescaled version.
Exceptionally, the proof will also take place in the non-rescaled space.

Proof.

We chose the basepoint
o_{k}
using
Lemma 3.8
.
In particular, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists a point
x_{k}\in X^{+}_{k}
such that
\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,x_{k})\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)+20\delta
and
\mathchoice{\left|o_{k}-x_{k}\right|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}{|o_{k}-x_{k}|}\leqslant\left|U\right|\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)+20\delta
.
In particular,
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
is a well-defined point of
X_{\omega}
.
We denote by
p=\lim_{\omega}p_{k}
its projection onto
T
.

We claim that there exists a point
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
in
T
such that
o^{+}_{k}\in X^{+}_{k}

\omega
-as and
\mathchoice{\left|o^{+}-p\right|}{|o^{+}-p|}{|o^{+}-p|}{|o^{+}-p|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-p\right|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}
.
If
p_{k}
belongs to the thick part
X^{+}_{k}

\omega
-as then we choose
o^{+}_{k}=p_{k}
.
Let us assume now that there exists
c_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
such that
\mathchoice{\left|p_{k}-c_{k}\right|}{|p_{k}-c_{k}|}{|p_{k}-c_{k}|}{|p_{k}-c_{k}|}<\rho_{k}/\varepsilon_{k}

\omega
-as (recall that the set of cone points
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is
2\rho_{k}
-separated in the
rescaled
space
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
).
It follows that
c=[c_{k}]
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
and
p\in\bar{P}(c)
.
Since
T
is the minimal
L
-invariant subtree, the point
p
lies on a bi-infinite geodesic
\sigma
of
T
.
We denote by
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
the point on
\sigma
that is the closest to
p
such that
b_{c}(o^{+})=0
.
In particular, one can choose
o^{+}_{k}
in the thick part
X^{+}_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Note also that
\mathchoice{\left|p-o^{+}\right|}{|p-o^{+}|}{|p-o^{+}|}{|p-o^{+}|}=-b_{c}(p)
.
Since
x_{k}
lies in the thick part
X^{+}_{k}

\omega
-as, we necessarily have
b_{c}(x)\geqslant 0
.
As
b_{c}
is
1
-Lipschitz we get

\mathchoice{\left|p-o^{+}\right|}{|p-o^{+}|}{|p-o^{+}|}{|p-o^{+}|}\leqslant-b_{c}(p)\leqslant-b_{c}(p)+b_{c}(x)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|b_{c}(p)-b_{c}(x)\right|}{|b_{c}(p)-b_{c}(x)|}{|b_{c}(p)-b_{c}(x)|}{|b_{c}(p)-b_{c}(x)|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|p-x\right|}{|p-x|}{|p-x|}{|p-x|},

which completes the proof of our claim.

Let
g\in L
.
Recall that the point
p
is a projection of
x
onto
T
. Hence
\mathchoice{\left|gx-x\right|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}=\mathchoice{\left|gp-p\right|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}+2\mathchoice{\left|x-p\right|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}
.
Combining our previous claim with the triangle inequality, we get

\mathchoice{\left|go^{+}-o^{+}\right|}{|go^{+}-o^{+}|}{|go^{+}-o^{+}|}{|go^{+}-o^{+}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|gp-p\right|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}+2\mathchoice{\left|o^{+}-p\right|}{|o^{+}-p|}{|o^{+}-p|}{|o^{+}-p|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|gp-p\right|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}{|gp-p|}+2\mathchoice{\left|x-p\right|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}{|x-p|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|gx-x\right|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}{|gx-x|}.

We sum this inequality for
g=\eta(u)
when
u
runs over
U
.
The result we get exactly says that

\varepsilon_{k}\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k})\leqslant\varepsilon_{k}\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,x_{k})+o(1).

The point
x_{k}
has been chosen to (almost) minimize the restricted energy.
Thus

\varepsilon_{k}\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)\leqslant\varepsilon_{k}\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k})\leqslant\varepsilon_{k}\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)+o(1).\qed

The next definition is borrowed from Guirardel
[
26
, Definition 1.4]
.

Definition 7.23
.

A
transverse covering
of an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T
is a collection
\mathcal{Y}
of closed subtrees of
T
with the following properties.

i.

Every two distinct elements of
\mathcal{Y}
have a degenerated intersection

ii.

Any bounded arc of
T
can be covered by finitely many elements of
\mathcal{Y}
.

A family of transverse coverings.

We fix a parameter
r\in[0,\rho)
(its value will vary later).
Let
\mathcal{C}(r)
be the set of cone points
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is non-degenerate.
We write
\mathcal{Y}(r)
for the collection of closed subtrees of
T
which consists of

—

the peripheral subtrees
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
, where
c
runs over
\mathcal{C}(r)
, which we call
r
-peripheral components
, and

—

the connected components of

T\setminus\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}(r)}P(c,r)

which we call
r
-transverse components
.

The action of
L
on
X_{\omega}
induces an action on
\mathcal{Y}(r)
which preserves the set of
r
-peripheral components ( respectively
r
-transversal components).

Assumption 7.24
.

The definition above makes sense for any
r\in[0,\rho)
.
Our goal is to prove that
\mathcal{Y}(0)
is a transverse covering, see
Proposition 7.41
.
Nevertheless for the moment we suppose that
r>0
.

Lemma 7.25
.

A point of
T
belongs to at most two subtrees in
\mathcal{Y}(r)
: one
r
-transverse component and/or one
r
-peripheral component.

Proof.

Since
r>0
, any two distinct
r
-peripheral components are disjoint, see
Lemma 7.5
.
Similarly any two distinct
r
-transverse components are disjoint.
Whence the result.
∎

Proposition 7.26
.

The family
\mathcal{Y}(r)
is an
L
-invariant
transverse covering
.

Proof.

As we explained above, if two elements of
\mathcal{Y}(r)
have a non-empty intersection, one is an
r
-transverse component, say
Y
, while the other one is an
r
-transverse component, say
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
Recall that peripheral subtrees are strictly convex, hence
Y\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is degenerated.
Indeed otherwise
Y
would contain a point in
P(c,r)
which contradicts its definition.

Let us now prove that any bounded arc
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}
of
T
is covered by finitely many elements of
\mathcal{Y}(r)
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
such that
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}\cap P(c,r)
is non-empty.
If
x
and
y
do not belong to
P(c)
then
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is an arc of length at least
2r
(
Lemma 7.6
).
On the other hand
x
( respectively
y
) can only belong to a single open peripheral subtree
P(c)
.
Thus there are only finitely many
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
such that
\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}\cap P(c,r)
is non-empty.
In addition,

\mathchoice{\left[x,y\right]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}{[x,y]}\setminus\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}(r)}P(c,r)

consists of a finite number of arcs each of which is contained in an
r
-transverse component.
∎

We use this transverse covering to decompose the action of
L
on
T
as a graph of actions
\Lambda(r)
.
The construction goes as follows – compare with Guirardel
[
25
, Section 4.5]
.
We first define its skeleton
S(r)
.
Its vertex set is
\mathcal{Y}(r)
.
Two subtrees
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
seen as vertices in
S(r)
are connected by an edge if
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
The action of
L
on
T
induces an action by isometries on
S(r)
.
Observe that
S(r)
is bipartite.
The vertices are indeed of two types: the
r
-transverse components and the
r
-peripheral components.
We call them respectively
transverse
and
peripheral vertices
of
S(r)
.
The vertex trees and the attaching points of
\Lambda(r)
are the natural ones.
Since the action of
L
on
T
is minimal, so is the one of
L
on
S(r)
.
Let us now describe a bit more its properties.

Lemma 7.27
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
The stabilizer of
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
seen as a vertex of
S(r)
is
L_{c}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Lemma 7.5
.
∎

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
v
be a transverse vertex of
S(r)
.
Then
L_{v}
is non-abelian.

Proof.

Suppose that contrary to our claim
L_{v}
is abelian.
Since
S(r)
is minimal, there are two distinct edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
of
S(r)
starting at
v
.
The peripheral endpoints of
e
and
e^{\prime}
correspond to
r
-peripheral subtrees
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
and
T\cap\bar{P}(c^{\prime},r)
for distinct
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
In particular,
L_{e}=L_{v}\cap L_{c}
and
L_{e^{\prime}}=L_{v}\cap L_{c^{\prime}}
.
According
Assumption 7.20
,
L_{e}
and
L_{e^{\prime}}
are non trivial.
Since
L
is CSA, the groups
L_{c}
and
L_{c^{\prime}}
commute.
However
L_{c}
and
L_{c^{\prime}}
are maximal abelian subgroup (
Lemma 7.12
).
Hence
L_{c}=L_{c^{\prime}}
which contradicts
Corollary 7.11
.
∎

Lemma 7.29
.

Let
e
be an edge of
S(r)
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
such that one of the vertices of
e
corresponds to the
r
-peripheral component
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
The stabilizer in
S(r)
of
e
coincide with the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c,r)
.
In particular, it is torsion-free, abelian.

Proof.

Let
Y
be the
r
-transverse component corresponding to the other vertex of
e
and write
p
for the unique point in
Y\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
It follows from the construction that the edge stabilizer
L_{e}
is exactly the point stabilizer
L_{p}
of
p\in T
.
Since
p
belongs to
Y
, it does not lie in
P(c,r)
.
Hence
p\in\bar{P}(c,r)\setminus P(c,r)
, i.e.
b_{c}(p)=-r
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.10
that
L_{p}
is the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c,r)
.
∎

Lemma 7.30
.

Every non-cyclic abelian subgroup of
L
fixes a vertex in
S(r)
.
Hence
S(r)
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree.

Proof.

Let
A
be a non-cyclic abelian subgroup of
L
.
If
A
is elliptic (for its action on
T
) then it automatically fixes a point in
S(r)
.
Otherwise, we distinguish two cases, following
Proposition 7.19
.
If
A
preserves a transverse subtree, say
Y
, then it fixes the vertex of
S(r)
associated to the unique
r
-transverse component containing
Y
.
Assume now that there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
such that
A
is contained in
L_{c}
.
If
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
, then
A
fixes the vertex of
S(r)
associated to the
r
-peripheral tree
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
If
c
does not belong to
\mathcal{C}(r)
, then by definition
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is degenerated.
It follows that the closest point projection of
\bar{P}(c,r)
onto
T
is reduced to a single point say
y
, which is fixed by
A
.
Note that
y
cannot lie in an
r
-peripheral subtree
T\cap\bar{P}(c^{\prime},r)
for some
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
Indeed otherwise
A
would fix both
c
and
c^{\prime}
and thus be trivial (
Corollary 7.11
).
Hence
y
belongs to an
r
-transverse subtree
Y
, which is
A
-invariant.
In particular,
A
fixes the vertex of
S(r)
corresponding to
Y
.
∎

Recall that the action of a group on a simplicial tree is
k
-acylindrical
, if the pointwise stabilizer of any segment of length greater than
k
is trivial.

Lemma 7.31
.

The action of
L
on
S(r)
is
2
-acylindrical.

Proof.

Any segment
I
of length at least
3
in
S(r)
contains two vertices of the form
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
and
T\cap\bar{P}(c^{\prime},r)
for some distinct
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.27
that the pointwise stabilizer of
I
is contained in
L_{c}\cap L_{c^{\prime}}
which is trivial by
Corollary 7.11
.
∎

An
L
-tree
S
is
reduced
if it is minimal, and for every vertex
v\in S
whose image in
S/L
has valence two, for every
e
starting at
v
, the edge group
L_{e}
is properly contained in
L_{v}
, see
[
3
]
.
Although
S(r)
may not be reduced, it is not “far” from being so.

Lemma 7.32
.

There exists an
L
-tree
\bar{S}(r)
and an
L
-equivariant collapse map
S(r)\twoheadrightarrow\bar{S}(r)
with the following properties.

i.

The action of
L
on
\bar{S}(r)
is minimal and
2
-acylindrical

ii.

For every vertex
v
of
\bar{S}(r)
, for every edge
e
starting at
v
, the group
L_{e}
is properly contained in
L_{v}
.
In particular,
\bar{S}(r)
is reduced.

iii.

If
E
and
\bar{E}
stands for the edge sets of
S(r)/L
and
\bar{S}(r)/L
respectively, then
\left|E\right|\leqslant 2\left|\bar{E}\right|
.

Proof.

For simplicity we write
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
for the set of peripheral and transverse vertices of
S(r)
.
Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
be a set of representatives for the
L
-orbite of vertices in
V_{0}
.
In addition, we write
I
for the set of indices
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
for which there is an edge, say
e_{i}
, adjacent to
v_{i}
such that
L_{e_{i}}=L_{v_{i}}
.
For every
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, denote by
E_{i}
the set of edges of
S(r)
one of whose endpoint lies in the orbit of
v_{i}
.
Since
S(r)
is bi-partite, the collection
E_{1},\dots,E_{m}
forms an
L
-invariant partition of the edges of
S(r)
.
Moreover, since
S(r)
is minimal,
E_{i}
contains at least two distinct
L
-orbits whenever
i\in I
.

Denote by
\bar{S}(r)
the tree obtained from
S(r)
by collapsing the
L
-orbit of
e_{i}
for every
i\in I
.
By construction the collapse map
f\colon S(r)\twoheadrightarrow\bar{S}(r)
is
L
-equivariant.
In each class
E_{i}
we have collapsed at most one edge orbit.
According to our previous observation, we have collapsed in total at most half of the orbits of
S(r)
, whence
(C2)((A3))iii
.
Since the action of
L
on
S(r)
is
2
-acylindrical, so is the one on
\bar{S}(r)
, hence
(C2)((A3))i
holds.

We are left to prove
(C2)((A3))ii
.
Assume that contrary to our claim, there is an edge
e
of
\bar{S}(r)
starting at a vertex
v
such that
L_{e}=L_{v}
.
Denote by
\tilde{e}
the pre-image of
e
in
S(r)
and
\tilde{v}
the endpoint of
\tilde{e}
lifting
v
.
By construction
L_{e}=L_{\tilde{e}}
is abelian, hence so is
L_{v}
.
Note that
L_{\tilde{v}}
is contained in
L_{v}
, hence
\tilde{v}
cannot belongs to
V_{1}
.
Indeed it would contradict the fact that transverse vertex stabilizers are non-abelian.
Therefore there is
g\in L
and
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
such that
\tilde{v}=gv_{i}
.
Note that
i
does not belong to
I
.
Indeed otherwise the non-abelian subgroup fixing the other end of
ge_{i}
would also be contained in
L_{v}
.
Since
i\notin I
, no edges in the link of
v_{i}
has been collapsed, hence
L_{v}=L_{\tilde{v}}
properly contains
L_{e}=L_{\tilde{e}}
.
Contradiction
∎

First consequences.

We exploit now the collection of acylindrical splittings
S(r)
to get informations on the group
L
and its action on
T
.
For simplicity we write
\mathcal{C}
for
\mathcal{C}(0)
.
Observe that

\mathcal{C}=\bigcup_{r\in(0,\rho)}\mathcal{C}(r).

Lemma 7.33
.

For every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the group
L_{c}
is elliptic, for its action on
T
.

Proof.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
As we observed, there exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that
c
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(r)
.
Let
v
be the vertex of
S(r)
associated to
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
be a set of
L_{c}
-representatives of the edges of
S(r)
starting at
v
.
Since
L
is finitely generated,
L_{c}
is finitely generated relative to
L_{e_{1}},\dots,L_{e_{m}}
.
However by
Lemma 7.29
, those subgroups are contained in the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c,r)
, that we denote by
N_{c}(r)
.
Hence the quotient
Q_{c}=L_{c}/N_{c}(r)
is finitely generated.
The action of
L_{c}
on
T
induces an action of
Q_{c}
on
\bar{P}(c,r)
.
Every element of
Q_{c}
is elliptic by
Lemma 7.10
.
It follows from Serre’s Lemma that
Q_{c}
and thus
L_{c}
is elliptic.
∎

Proposition 7.34
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
The map
b_{c}\colon T\to\mathbf{R}
achieves its minimum at a unique point
z_{c}
.
Moreover
z_{c}
belongs to
T\cap\bar{P}(c)
and is fixed by
L_{c}
.

Proof.

Recall that balls/horoballs are strictly convex, hence the point
z_{c}
, if it exists, is necessarily unique, and therefore is fixed by
L_{c}
.

According to
Lemma 7.33
,
L_{c}
is elliptic.
Hence by
Lemma 7.10
there exists a finite number
r\in[0,\rho]
such that
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is non-empty and pointwise fixed by
L_{c}
(recall that
\rho
can be finite or infinite).
Since
\mathcal{C}
is the increasing union of all
\mathcal{C}(s)
for
s>0
, we can assume without loss of generality that
r>0
.
We distinguish two cases.
Suppose first that
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is reduced to a point, say
z_{c}
.
One checks that
b_{c}(z_{c})=-r
, while
b_{c}(x)\geqslant-r
, for every
x\in T
.
Hence
z_{c}
satisfies all the required properties.

Suppose now that
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is non-degenerate, so that
c
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(r)
.
The peripheral subtree
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
corresponds to a vertex
v
of the tree
S(r)
whose stabilizer is
L_{c}
.
We denote by
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
a set of
L_{c}
-representatives of the edges of
S(r)
starting at
v
and by
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
the corresponding attaching points of the graph of actions
\Lambda(r)
.
Since the action of
L
on
T
is minimal,
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is the convex hull of
L_{c}\cdot\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
.
Each
x_{i}
is fixed by
L_{c}
, hence
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is actually the convex hull of
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
.
In particular, it is compact.
Since
b_{c}
is continuous, it achieves its minimum at some point
z_{c}
in
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
∎

Comparing the splittings.

Let
r,r^{\prime}\in(0,\rho)
with
r^{\prime}<r
.
In order to compare the splittings
S(r)
and
S(r^{\prime})
, we build a map
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
.
First we define
f
on the set of vertices.
Let
v^{\prime}
be a vertex of
S(r^{\prime})
.

—

Suppose that
v^{\prime}
corresponds to an
r^{\prime}
-transverse component, say
Y^{\prime}
, then
f(v^{\prime})
is the unique
r
-transverse component of
T
containing
Y^{\prime}
.

—

Suppose that
v^{\prime}
corresponds to a peripheral subtree
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
for some cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}(r^{\prime})
, then we distinguish two cases.

i.

If
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
, that is
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is non-degenerate, then we define
f(v^{\prime})
to be the
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.

ii.

Otherwise,
f(v^{\prime})
is the unique
r
-transverse component of
T
containing
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
.

Second, we extend the definition of
f
to the edge set of
S(r^{\prime})
.
By definition an edge
e^{\prime}
of
S(r^{\prime})
consists of a pair of two subtrees in
\mathcal{Y}(r^{\prime})
: an
r
-transverse component, say
Y^{\prime}
and an
r
-peripheral component, say
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
where
c\in\mathcal{C}(r^{\prime})
.
We denote by
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
v^{\prime}_{2}
the corresponding vertices of
S(r^{\prime})
.
We also write for
Y
the (unique)
r
-transverse component containing
Y^{\prime}
.

i.

Assume that
c\in\mathcal{C}(r)
.
We claim that
Y\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
is non-empty.
Indeed choose a point in
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
, which is non-empty by definition of
\mathcal{C}(r)
.
Join this point to
Y^{\prime}
by an a minimal arc
I
.
By construction
(Y^{\prime}\cup I)\setminus P(c,r)
is contained in
Y
and intersects
\bar{P}(c,r)
.
Consequently
f(v^{\prime}_{1})
and
f(v^{\prime}_{2})
are joining by an edge
e
in
S(r)
.
We let
f(e^{\prime})=e
.

ii.

Assume now that
c\notin\mathcal{C}(r)
.
Then
Y
also contains
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
, that is
f(v^{\prime}_{1})=f(v^{\prime}_{2})
.
In this case we collapse the edge
e^{\prime}
to the vertex
f(v^{\prime}_{i})
.

Observe that
f
is the composition of a collapse map and a folding map, in the sense of
[
56
,
3
]
.
By construction,
f
is
L
-equivariant.
Since
S(r)
is minimal,
f
is also onto.

Proposition 7.35
.

There exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that for every
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
the map
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
induces an isomorphism from
S(r^{\prime})/L
onto
S(r)/L
.

Proof.

For every
r\in(0,\rho)
we write
\bar{S}(r)
for the reduced
L
-tree obtained from
S(r)
by
Lemma 7.32
.
The action of
L
on
\bar{S}(r)
is
2
-acylindrical.
Since
L
is finitely generated, it follows from acylindrical accessibility – see for instance
[
49
,
57
]
– that the number of edges in
\bar{S}(r)/L
is uniformly bounded from above.
Hence the same holds of the number of edges in
S(r)/L
.
However if given
r,r^{\prime}\in(0,\rho)
with
r^{\prime}<r
, the
L
-equivariant map
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
induces an epimorphism from
S(r^{\prime})/L
onto
S(r)/L
.
Therefore if
r
is sufficiently small, this map need be an isomorphism.
∎

Passing to the limit.

Recall that
L
is CSA.
In addition we assumed that
L
is freely indecomposable relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
It follows from
Theorem 6.5
that the JSJ deformation space
\mathcal{D}
of
L
over
\mathcal{A}
relative to
\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc}
exists.
We denote by
S_{\rm JSJ}
the tree of cylinders of an element in
\mathcal{D}
.
According to
Lemma 6.4
,
S_{\rm JSJ}
also belongs to
\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition 7.36
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Let
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
H
a non-abelian vertex group of
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
If
L_{c}\cap H
is not virtually cyclic, then it pointwise fixes
\bar{P}(c)
.

Proof.

We claim that
H
is rigid in
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-trees.
Assume indeed that it is not the case.
Since
H
is not abelian, it is quadratically hanging (see
Theorem 6.5
).
Recall that if
\Sigma
is a hyperbolic orbifold, the abelian subgroups of its fundamental group are all virtually cyclic.
In particular,
L_{c}\cap H
is virtually cyclic, which contradicts our assumption.

Let
r\in(0,\rho)
.
We assume that
r
is sufficiently small so that
c
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(r)
.
According to
Lemma 7.30
,
S(r)
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree.
Since
H
is rigid, it fixes a vertex in
S(r)
.
Such a vertex necessarily corresponds to an
r
-transverse component
Y
, otherwise
H
would be abelian.
On the other hand
L_{c}
fixes the vertex of
S(r)
associated to
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
.
Hence
L_{c}\cap H
fixes the first edge on the geodesic in
S(r)
from
T\cap\bar{P}(c,r)
to
Y
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.29
that
L_{c}\cap H
pointwise fixes
\bar{P}(c,r)
for every sufficiently small
r\in(0,\rho)
.
Hence
L_{c}\cap H
fixes
\bar{P}(c)=\bar{P}(c,0)
.
∎

Corollary 7.37
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
The group
L_{c}
is finitely generated relative to the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
L_{c}
is non cyclic.
It follows that
L_{c}
fixes a vertex in
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
We distinguish two cases.
Assume first that
L_{c}
is contained in a non-abelian vertex group
H
of
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
Applying
Proposition 7.36
, we see that
L_{c}
pointwise fixes
\bar{P}(c)
hence the result holds.
Assume now that
L_{c}
fixes an abelian vertex
v
of
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
Since
L_{c}
is a maximal abelian subgroup of
L
(
Lemma 7.12
) the stabilizer of
v
is exactly
L_{c}
.
We write
e_{1},\dots,e_{p}
for a set of representatives of
L_{c}
-orbits of edges from
S_{\rm JSJ}
starting at
v
.
We know that
L_{c}
is finitely generated with respect to the collection
\{L_{e_{j}}\}
.
Hence it suffices to prove that for every
j\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, either
L_{e_{j}}
is finitely generated or fixes
\bar{P}(c)
.
Recall that
S_{\rm JSJ}
is a tree of cylinders.
In particular, it is a bipartite tree where half of the vertices are non-abelian.
Thus
e_{j}
is adjacent to a non-abelian vertex group.
The statement now follows from
Proposition 7.36
.
∎

Proposition 7.38
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
and set

r=-\min_{x\in T}b_{c}(x).

There exists a finite partition
r_{0}<r_{1}<\dots<r_{m}
of
\mathchoice{\left[0\,,r\right]}{[0\,,r]}{[0\,,r]}{[0\,,r]}
with the following property.
For every
i\in\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket
, for every
s\in[r_{i},r_{i+1})
the pointwise stabilizers of
\bar{P}(c,s)
and
\bar{P}(c,r_{i})
coincide.

Proof.

For every
s\in[0,r]
, we denote by
A(s)
the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c,s)
.
According to
Corollary 7.37
, the quotient
Q_{c}=L_{c}/A(0)
is a finitely generated abelian group.
Let
p
be the rank of its maximal free abelian group.
We claim that the collection

\mathchoice{\left\{A(s)\;:\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant r\right\}}{\{A(s)\;:\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant r\}}{\{A(s)\;:\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant r\}}{\{A(s)\;:\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant r\}}

contains at most
p+2
distinct pairwise distinct elements.
Assume on the contrary that is not the case.
There exist
r_{0}<r_{1}<\dots r_{p+2}
in
[0,r]
, such that
A(r_{i})
is strictly increasing sequence.
Let
i\in\llbracket 0,p\rrbracket
.
By
Corollary 7.14
,
A(r_{i+1})/A(r_{i})
is a free abelian group of rank at least
1
– note that
A(r_{p+2})/A(r_{p+1})
may have torsion if
r_{p+2}=r=\rho
.
It follows that
L_{c}/A(0)
contains a free abelian group of rank at least
p+1
, which contradicts the definition of
p
.
∎

Corollary 7.39
.

There exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that for every
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
the simplicial map
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
is an isometry.

Proof.

In view of Propositions
7.35
and
7.38
, there exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that

i.

for every
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
the map
S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
induces an isomorphism from
S(r^{\prime})/L
onto
S(r)/L
;

ii.

for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
the pointwise stabilizers of
\bar{P}(c,r)
and
\bar{P}(c)
coincide.

Let
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
.
Assume that the map
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
(which is onto) is not an isometry.
Observe that
f
is one-to-one when restricted to the set of peripheral vertices.
It follows that there exist two distinct edges
e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
in
S(r^{\prime})
starting from the same peripheral vertex
v^{\prime}
such that
f(e^{\prime}_{1})=f(e^{\prime}_{2})
.
Since
S(r^{\prime})/L\to S(r)/L
is a bijection,
e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
are in the same orbit.
Hence there exists
g\in L
such that
ge^{\prime}_{1}=e^{\prime}_{2}
.
Recall that
S(r)
is bipartite.
Thus
g
necessarily fixes the peripheral vertex
v^{\prime}
.
According to
Lemma 7.29
, there is
c\in\mathcal{C}
such that the stabilizers of
e^{\prime}_{1}
and
f(e^{\prime}_{1})
are the pointwise stabilizers of
\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
and
\bar{P}(c,r)
respectively.
By construction
g
fixes
f(e^{\prime}_{1})
but not
e^{\prime}_{1}
, hence pointwise fixes
\bar{P}(c,r)
but not
\bar{P}(c,r^{\prime})
.
This contradicts
(C2)((A3))ii
.
Hence
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
is an isometry.
∎

We now investigate the consequences of the above discussion for the family
\mathcal{Y}(0)
.

Lemma 7.40
.

There exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in T\setminus P(c)
, the intersection
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is either degenerated or contains an arc of length
2r
.

Proof.

Let
r\in(0,\rho)
be the parameter given by
Corollary 7.39
.
Note that it suffices to prove the result when
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
lie in
\bar{P}(c)\setminus P(c)
, i.e. when
b_{c}(x_{1})=b_{c}(x_{2})=0
.
Assume that
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is an arc whose length is less than
2r
.
It follows from
Lemma 7.6
, that
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}
does not intersect
P(c,r)
.
Hence
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
lie in the same
r
-transverse component, say
Y
.
On the other hand, since
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}\cap P(c)
is non-empty, there exists
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
such that
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
lie in distinct
r^{\prime}
-transverse component, say
Y^{\prime}_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}_{2}
.
Observe that
Y^{\prime}_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}_{2}
correspond to two distinct transverse vertices of
S(r^{\prime})
which are mapped by
f\colon S(r^{\prime})\to S(r)
to the same vertex, namely
Y
.
This contradicts the fact that
f
is an isometry.
Hence
\mathchoice{\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}{[x_{1},x_{2}]}\cap\bar{P}(c)
is either degenerated or has length at least
2r
as announced.
∎

Proposition 7.41
.

The family
\mathcal{Y}(0)
is a transverse covering.

Proof.

The proof goes verbatim as the one of
Proposition 7.26
, using
Lemma 7.40
instead of
Lemma 7.6
.
∎

Note that this time, two elements of
\mathcal{Y}(0)
of the form
\bar{P}(c)
,
\bar{P}(c^{\prime})
for distinct
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
may have a non-empty intersection.
A transverse component may be reduced to a single point.

As in the previous section we define a bipartite tree
S(0)
.
Its vertex set is
\mathcal{Y}(0)
.
There are two type of vertices.
The
peripheral vertices
correspond to the subtrees of the form
T\cap\bar{P}(c)
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
The
transverse vertices
correspond to the transverse components of
T
.
A peripheral vertex and a transverse vertex are joined by an edge if the subtrees of
T
that they represent have a non-empty intersection.
One checks using
Proposition 7.41
, that this construction defines indeed a simplicial bipartite tree endowed with a minimal action by isometries of
L
.

Proceeding as above, one defines for every
r\in(0,\rho)
an
L
-equivariant simplicial map
f\colon S(0)\to S(r)
.
It follows from
Corollary 7.39
that there exists
r\in(0,\rho)
such that the map
f\colon S(0)\to S(r)
is an isometry.
In particular,
S(0)
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\rm nc})
-tree.
The action of
L
on
S(0)
is
2
-acylindrical.

The tree
S(0)
is the skeleton of a graph of actions
\Lambda(0)
(see
Definition 5.1
).
If
v
is a vertex of
S(0)
, the associated vertex tree is the subtree of
T
represented by
v
.
If
e
is an edge of
S(0)
joining a peripheral vertex
v_{1}
to a transverse vertex
v_{2}
, the associated attaching point is the unique intersection point of the subtrees represented by
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

Further splitting of
T
.

Our next task is to further refine the graph of actions
\Lambda(0)
defined above, into well-understood “elementary bricks”.
Before doing so, let us recall the kind of actions we are interested in.

Definition 7.42
.

Let
G
be a group acting by isometries on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
Y
.
We say that the action is

i.

simplicial
if
Y
is simplicial and the action of
G
on
Y
is simplicial;

ii.

axial
if
Y
is a line and the image of
G
in
\operatorname{Isom}(Y)
is a finitely generated group acting with dense orbits on
Y
;

iii.

of
Seifert type
if the action has a kernel
N
and the faithful action of
G/N
on
Y
is dual to an arational measured foliation on a closed
2
-orbifold with boundary.

The next theorem is a relative version of Sela
[
49
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Theorem 7.43

(
[
26
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a group and
\mathcal{H}
a finite collection of subgroups of
G
such that
G
, is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
Y
be an
\mathbf{R}
-tree endowed with a minimal, super-stable action of
G
so that each subgroup in
\mathcal{H}
is elliptic.
Then one of the following holds

—

The group
G
splits as a free product relative to
\mathcal{H}
.

—

The action of
G
on
Y
splits as a graph of actions where each vertex action is either simplicial, axial or of Seifert type.

Let us now move back to the study of the action of
L
on
T
.
Recall that
\mathcal{H}
stands for the collection of elusive subgroups of
L
.

Theorem 7.44
.

Assume that
L
does not split as a free product relative to
\mathcal{H}
.
Then the action of
L
on
T
decomposes as a graph of actions over abelian groups
\Lambda
whose skeleton
S_{\Lambda}
refines
S(0)
.
Each vertex action of
\Lambda
is either peripheral, simplicial, axial or of Seifert type.
Moreover,

i.

if
L_{v}
is a Seifert type vertex group of
\Lambda
, then
L_{v}
is quadratically hanging with trivial fiber and the corresponding vertex tree is transverse in
T
;

ii.

if
L_{v}
is an axial vertex group of
\Lambda
, then the corresponding vertex tree is transverse in
T
.

Remark
.

One can prove that the peripheral components are also simplicial.
Nevertheless we distinguish them as they cannot always be shorten.

Proof.

We start with the graph of actions
\Lambda(0)
defined above.
The strategy is to blow up each transverse vertex of
S(0)
to further refine this decomposition.
The first step is to replace
\mathcal{H}
be a
finite
collection of subgroups.
According to
[
28
, Lemma 8.2]
, there exists a finite collection
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{1},\dots,H_{m}\}
where each
H_{i}
is a finitely generated subgroup of an element in
\mathcal{H}
such that
L
does not split as a free product relative to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.
Let
v
be a transverse vertex of
S(0)
and
Y_{v}
the corresponding vertex tree.
Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
a set of
L_{v}
-representatives of the edges of
S(0)
starting at
v
.
For simplicity, we let

\mathcal{E}_{v}=\{L_{e_{1}},\dots,L_{e_{m}}\}.

Consider now all the conjugates (in
L
) of groups in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
which fix
v
an no other vertex in
S(0)
.
We define
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
by choosing a representative for each
L_{v}
-conjugacy class in this family.
Since
L
is finitely generated,
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
is finite and
L_{v}
is finitely generated relative to
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
, see for instance
[
26
, Lemma 1.12]
.
It follows from our assumption that
L_{v}
does not split as a free product relative to
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
.
Moreover each group in
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
is elliptic for its action on
Y_{v}
.

Let
Y^{\prime}_{v}
be the minimal
L_{v}
-invariant subtree of
T
. Since
L_{v}
preserves
Y_{v}
, the latter contains
Y^{\prime}_{v}
.
We claim that
Y^{\prime}_{v}=Y_{v}
.
If
e
is an edge of
S(0)
starting at
v
, we denote by
x_{e}\in T
the associated attaching point in the graph of actions
\Lambda(0)
and by
y_{e}
its projection on
Y^{\prime}_{v}
.
Since the action of
L
on
T
is minimal,
Y_{v}
is exactly

Y_{v}=Y^{\prime}_{v}\cup\left(\bigcup_{e\in\operatorname{lk}(v)}\mathchoice{\left[y_{e},x_{e}\right]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}\right)

Let
e\in\operatorname{lk}(v)
.
The group
L_{e}
is the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c)
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Since
L
is freely indecomposable relative to
\mathcal{H}
, the group
L_{e}
is not trivial.
According to
Lemma 7.10
, any point of
T
fixed by
L_{e}
belongs to
\bar{P}(c)
.
By construction the geodesic
\mathchoice{\left[y_{e},x_{e}\right]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}{[y_{e},x_{e}]}
is pointwise fixed by
L_{e}
, but belongs to the transverse subtree
Y_{v}
.
Hence it is necessary degenerated.
Consequently
Y^{\prime}_{v}=Y_{v}
, which completes our claim.
According to
Lemma 7.17
the action of
L_{v}
on
Y_{v}
is super-stable.
Consequently we can apply
Theorem 7.43
to the action of
L_{v}
on
Y_{v}
(relative to
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{v}
).
This provides a decomposition of
Y_{v}
as a graph of actions whose vertex actions fall in one of the three categories of the theorem.

We obtain the graph of actions
\Lambda
by assembling together all the decomposition of the vertex actions of
S(0)
.
Let
S_{\Lambda}
be its underlying skeleton.
By construction
S_{\Lambda}
refines
S(0)
and every vertex action of
\Lambda
is either peripheral, simplicial, axial or of Seifert type.
Moreover axial and Seifert type vertex trees are transverse in
T
.
Note that we can assume that transverse simplicial vertex trees are maximal, that is if
v
and
v^{\prime}
are two vertices in
S_{\Lambda}
such that the corresponding vertex trees
Y_{v}
and
Y_{v^{\prime}}
are simplicial and the images of
v
and
v^{\prime}
in
S(0)
are transverse vertices, then either
Y_{v}\cap Y_{v^{\prime}}=\emptyset
or
v=v^{\prime}
.

Let us now prove the additional properties of
\Lambda
.
Let
v
be a Seifert type vertex of
S_{\Lambda}
and
Y_{v}\subset T
the associated vertex tree.
We already mentioned that
Y_{v}
is transverse.
The kernel
N_{v}
of the action of
L_{v}
on
Y_{v}
fixes a non-degenerate tripod, hence is trivial by
Lemma 7.16
.
In particular,
L_{v}
is isomorphic to the fundamental group of a closed
2
-orbifold
\Sigma
with boundary.
Consider a subgroup
H
which is conjugated to an element of
\mathcal{H}
so that
H\cap L_{v}
fixes a point in
Y_{v}
.
Since
Y_{v}
is dual to an arational foliation, either
H\cap L_{v}
is contained in a cone point of
\Sigma
or in a boundary subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Consequently
H\cap L_{v}
is an extended boundary subgroup.
We prove in the same way that every edge group of
S_{\Lambda}
incident to
v
is an extended boundary subgroup.
It follows from this discussion that
L_{v}
is
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-quadratically-hanging.

We are left to prove that edge groups of
S_{\Lambda}
are abelian.
Let
e
be an edge of
S_{\Lambda}
.
If
e
comes from an edge of
S(0)
, then
L_{e}
is abelian.
Otherwise, since transverse simplicial trees are maximal, one of the end point
v
of
e
is either an axial vertex or a Seifert type vertex.
If
v
is an axial vertex, then
L_{v}
is abelian, thus so is
L_{e}
.
If
v
is a Seifert type vertex, then
L_{e}
is an extended boundary subgroup of the underlying manifold.
Since
L
is CSA,
L_{e}
is abelian.
∎

Definition 7.45
.

We call the graph of actions
\Lambda
given by
Theorem 7.44
the
complete decomposition of
T
(as a graph of actions).
Let
S_{\Lambda}
be its underlying skeleton.
We define a new splitting of
L
refining
S_{\Lambda}
as follows.
For each vertex
v
of
S_{\Lambda}
whose corresponding vertex tree
Y_{v}\subset T
is simplicial and transverse, we blow up the vertex
v
and replace it by a copy of
Y_{v}
.
We call the resulting tree
S
the
complete splitting of
L
with respect to
T
.

Remark
.

Note that both
\Lambda
and
S
depend on the sequence
(\Gamma_{k},\varphi_{k})
defining
L
and
T
.
However the sequence is normally clear from the context.
Hence we can safely ignore it in the terminology.

The tree
S
is not necessarily the underlying skeleton of a decomposition of
T
as a graph of actions.
Note that
\operatorname{Mod}(L,S_{\Lambda})\subset\operatorname{Mod}(L,S)
.
This larger modular group
\operatorname{Mod}(L,S)
has the advantage of containing all the automorphisms needed for the shortening argument in the next section.

7.5
Shortening argument

Let
(L,\eta)
be a divergent limit group as before.
We now explain how the action of
L
on its limit tree
T
can be used to (eventually) shorten the morphism
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{k}
, that where used to define
L
.
Unlike in the case of limit groups over a free group, a shortening does not always exist.
This comes from the fact that
L
may have no modular automorphism beside the inner ones.
Such a situation occurs for instance if
L
is obtained by adjoining a root to a rigid group.

Recall that strong covers and limit of strong covers have been defined in
Section 5.4
.
This section is dedicated to the proof of the following statement.

Theorem 7.46

(Shortening argument)
.

Let
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Let
G
be a group and
U
a finite generating set of
G
.
Let
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{k}
be a sequence of marked groups where
(\Gamma_{k},X_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k})\in\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\tilde{\rho}_{k})
and such that both
(\tilde{\rho}_{k})
and
\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi_{k},U)
diverge to infinity.
We make the following assumptions.

—

The sequence
(\Gamma_{k},\varphi_{k})
converges to a limit group
(L,\eta)
, which is non-abelian and freely indecomposable relative to its collection
\mathcal{H}
of elusive subgroups.

—

The complete splitting
S
of
L
with respect to its limit tree
T
is not a generalized root splitting.

Let
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}_{k})
be a sequence of strong covers of
(L,\eta,S)
converging to
(L,\eta,S)
.
Let
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L
be the covering map associated to
\hat{L}_{k}
.
Assume that for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, the morphism
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{k}
factors through
\hat{\eta}_{k}\colon G\to\hat{L}_{k}
and write
\hat{\varphi}_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\to\Gamma_{k}
for the resulting morphism, so that
\varphi_{k}=\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}
.

There exists
\kappa>0
with the following property: for infinitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exist automorphisms
\alpha_{k}\in\operatorname{Mod}(L,S)
and
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
preserving
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha_{k}\circ\zeta_{k}
and

\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U\right)\leqslant(1-\kappa)\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)\ \omega\text{-as}.

Remark
.

In the statement, the energies are computed in the
non-rescaled
space
X_{k}
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of the theorem.
The material below comes from
[
47
]
.
We follow the exposition given in
[
46
,
58
]
.
Let us recall the notation from Sections
7.1
-
7.4
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}
the collection of all abelian subgroups of
L
and by
\mathcal{H}
the collection of elusive subgroups of
L
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we let

\varepsilon_{k}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\infty}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)}.

The group
L
acts on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree obtained
X_{\omega}
as the
\omega
-limit the rescaled spaces
\varepsilon_{k}X_{k}
, where
\omega
is a non-principal ultra-filter fixed
as in the beginning of
Section 7.1
.
As before we let
\rho_{k}=\varepsilon_{k}\tilde{\rho}_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

Let
T
be the minimal
L
-invariant subtree of
X_{\omega}
.
By
Lemma 7.21
, there exists a sequence of point
o^{+}_{k}\in X^{+}_{k}
such that
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
belongs to
T
and

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k})=\lambda^{+}_{1}(\varphi_{k},U)+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right).

(18)

The action of
L
on
T
splits as a graph of actions
\Lambda
whose vertex actions are either peripheral, simplicial, axial, or Seifert type (
Theorem 7.44
).
We call a vertex
v
of its skeleton
S_{\Lambda}

peripheral
,
simplicial
,
axial
, or
Seifert type
depending whether the corresponding component
Y_{c}\subset T
is peripheral, simplicial, axial, or Seifert type.
We denote by
S
the complete splitting of
L
with respect to
T
, which refines the skeleton
S_{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda
.
We denote by
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,S)
and
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
the respective subgroups of
\operatorname{Mod}(L,S)
and
\operatorname{Mod}(S,\Lambda)
generated by the following elements

—

inner automorphisms of
L
,

—

Dehn twists over an edge
e
of
S
( respectively
S_{\Lambda}
) by an element
u\in L_{e}
,

—

surface type automorphisms,

—

generalized Dehn twists,

(see
Section 6.4
for the definitions).
The only difference between with regular modular groups concerns the (regular) Dehn twists: we require that the twister
u
belongs to the edge group
L_{e}
(and not its centralizer).
Note that
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
is contained in
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,S)
as the edge set of
S
is larger than the one of
S_{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 7.47
.

Let
\alpha\in\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,S)
.
There exist
C\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and automorphisms
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
preserving
\hat{S}_{k}
, such that for all but finitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha\circ\zeta_{k}
and
\lambda_{\infty}(\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U)\leqslant C
.

Remark
.

In the above statement, the energy
\lambda_{\infty}(\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U)
is measured in the Cayley graph of
\hat{L}_{k}
with respect to the generating set
\hat{\eta}_{k}(U)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for every element in the generating set of
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,S)
.
It is obvious for inner automorphisms.

Let
e
be an edge of
S
.
Let
u
be an element of
L_{e}
and
\alpha
the Dehn twist of
L
over
e
by
u
.
Let
\tilde{u}\in G
be a pre-image of
u
.
If
k
is sufficiently large, then
\hat{u}_{k}=\hat{\eta}_{k}(\tilde{u})
fixes a pre-image
\hat{e}_{k}
in
\hat{S}_{k}
of
e
.
We choose for
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
the Dehn twist of
\hat{L}_{k}
over
\hat{e}_{k}
by
\hat{u}_{k}
.

Let
v
be a quadratically hanging vertex of
S
and
\Sigma
the underlying
2
-orbifold.
Let
\alpha
be the natural extension of a local automorphism
\beta
induced by a homeomorphism of
\Sigma
fixing the boundary.
If
k
is sufficiently large, there exists a pre-image
\hat{v}_{k}
of
v
in
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
onto
L_{v}
.
Hence the local automorphism
\beta
of
L_{v}
we started with lifts to a local automorphism
\hat{\beta}_{k}
of
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
.
We choose for
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
the natural extension of
\hat{\beta}_{k}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of
S
whose vertex group
L_{v}
is abelian.
We denote by
K_{v}
the peripheral subgroup of
v
(see
Section 6.4
for the definition).
Let
\alpha
be the natural extension of a local automorphism
\beta
of
L_{v}
fixing
K_{v}
.
Unlike in the previous case, if
L_{v}
is a priori not finitely generated, thus one cannot find a vertex
\hat{v}_{k}
in
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
and
L_{v}
are isomorphic.
Nevertheless,
L_{v}
is finitely generated relative to
K_{v}
.
Consequently, if
k
is sufficiently large, there exists a pre-image
\hat{v}_{k}
of
v
in
\hat{S}_{k}
with the following properties.
Let
\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
be the peripheral subgroup of
\hat{v}_{k}
.
The map
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L
sends
\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
into
K_{v}
and induces an isomorphism from
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}/\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
onto
L_{v}/K_{v}
.
By definition,
\beta
fixes pointwise
K_{v}
.
By construction,
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
splits as
\mathbf{Z}^{p}\oplus\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
, where the abelian free factor
\mathbf{Z}^{p}
is isomorphic to
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}/\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
and thus to
L_{v}/K_{v}
.
Hence one can find an automorphism
\hat{\beta}_{k}
of
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
that lifts
\beta
and fixes
\hat{K}_{k,\hat{v}_{k}}
.
We choose for
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
the natural extension of
\hat{\beta}_{k}
.
∎

7.5.1
Axial and Seifert type component

We handle first the case where
\Lambda
has a vertex action which is axial or Seifert type.
To that end, we some recall results of Rips-Sela
[
47
]
that can be used to shorten an action in this situation.

Theorem 7.48
.

Let
L
be a finitely generated group acting on an
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T
which admits a decomposition as a graph of actions
\Lambda
.
Let
x\in T
.
Let
U
be a finite subset of
L
.
There exists an element
\alpha\in\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
such that for every element
u\in U
,

—

if the geodesics
\mathchoice{\left[x,ux\right]}{[x,ux]}{[x,ux]}{[x,ux]}
has a non-degenerate intersection with a Seifert type component of
\Lambda
, then
\mathchoice{\left|\alpha(u)x-x\right|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}<\mathchoice{\left|ux-x\right|}{|ux-x|}{|ux-x|}{|ux-x|}
,

—

otherwise
\alpha(u)=u
.

Proof.

The proof is detailed in Perin’s thesis
[
46
, Theorem 5.12]
.
Note that Perin only asserts that
\alpha
belongs to
\operatorname{Mod}(L,\Lambda)
which is a priori bigger that
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
.
Nevertheless one checks from the proof that
\alpha
is a product of surface type automorphisms, whence the result.
∎

Theorem 7.49
.

Let
L
be a finitely generated group.
Suppose that
L
acts on a real tree
T
with abelian arc stabilizers and that
T
admits a decomposition as a graph of actions
\Lambda
.
In addition, we assume that every solvable subgroup of
L
is either elliptic (for its action on
T
) or abelian.
Let
x\in T
.
Let
U
be a finite subset of
L
.
There exists an element
\alpha\in\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
such that for every element
u\in U
,

—

if the geodesics
\mathchoice{\left[x,ux\right]}{[x,ux]}{[x,ux]}{[x,ux]}
has a non-degenerate intersection with an axial type component of
\Lambda
, then
\mathchoice{\left|\alpha(u)x-x\right|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}{|\alpha(u)x-x|}<\mathchoice{\left|ux-x\right|}{|ux-x|}{|ux-x|}{|ux-x|}
,

—

otherwise
\alpha(u)=u
.

Proof.

Let
v
be vertex of
\Lambda
whose corresponding component
Y_{v}
is axial.
We claim that
L_{v}
splits as a direct product
L_{v}=N_{v}\oplus A_{v}
, where
N_{v}
is the kernel of the map
L_{v}\to\operatorname{Isom}(Y_{v})
.
Since pointwise arc stabilizers are abelian, the group
L_{v}
is the extension of the abelian group
N_{v}
by a subgroup of
\operatorname{Isom}(Y_{v})
, say
Q_{v}
, which is solvable.
In particular,
L_{v}
is solvable, but not elliptic, hence abelian.
Moreover
Y_{v}
is a line on which
Q_{v}
acts with dense orbits.
Hence
Q_{v}
is necessarily free abelian.
Since
L_{v}
is abelian, the projection
L_{v}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{v}
admits a section
\sigma\colon Q_{v}\to L_{v}
.
In particular,
L_{v}
splits as a semi-direct product
L_{v}=N_{v}\rtimes A_{v}
where
A_{v}
is the image of
\sigma
(which is isomorphic to
Q_{v}
).
As
L_{v}
is abelian, the action by conjugation on
N_{v}
of any element of
L_{v}
is trivial.
Hence the above semi-direct product is actually a direct product, which completes the proof of our claim.
The same argument also shows that
N_{v}
is the peripheral subgroup of
v
, as well as the stabilizer of any edge of
S
adjacent to
v
.

One can now follow the proof given by Perin in
[
46
, Theorem 5.17]
.
Indeed the statement given there is almost the same as our theorem.
The main difference is that Perin assumes that every solvable subgroup of
L
is free abelian.
Nevertheless this assumption is only used to prove that the vertex groups of
L
associated to an axial component splits as a direct sum as we described above.
Therefore the rest of the proof can be carried verbatim in our settings.

Perrin’s statement asserts that
\alpha
belongs to
\operatorname{Mod}(L,\Lambda)
.
As for
Theorem 7.48
, one can check from the proof that
\alpha
is actually a product of generalized Dehn twists, hence belongs to
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
.
∎

Recall that for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, the morphism
\hat{\varphi}_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\to\Gamma_{k}
is obtained by factoring
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{k}
through the cover
\hat{\eta}_{k}\colon G\to\hat{L}_{k}
.

Proposition 7.50
.

Assume that the graph of actions
\Lambda
contains an axial or a Seifert type vertex action.
There exist
\ell\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
with the following property.
For every sufficiently large
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
preserving
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha\circ\zeta_{k}
and

\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U\right)\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)-\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon_{k}}\ \omega\text{-as},

Proof.

Recall that
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
is a point of
T
, where
o_{k}^{+}\in X^{+}_{k}
(almost) minimizes the restricted energy of
\varphi_{k}
.
We write
U_{0}
for the set of all elements
u\in U
such that
\mathchoice{\left[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}\right]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}
has a non-degenerate intersection with an axial or Seifert type component of
\Lambda
.
Observe that
U_{0}
is non-empty.
Indeed otherwise

L\cdot\left(\bigcup_{u\in U}\mathchoice{\left[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}\right]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}{[o^{+},\eta(u)o^{+}]}\right)

would be an
L
-invariant subtree of
T
covered only by peripheral and simplicial subtrees.
By minimality it should equal
T
, which contradicts our assumption.
Since
L
is CSA, every solvable subgroup of
L
is abelian, see
[
51
, Lemma 1.4]
.
According to Theorems
7.48
and
7.49
, there exist
\ell\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
\alpha
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)\subset\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,S)
such that

i.

\mathchoice{\left|\alpha\circ\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}\right|}{|\alpha\circ\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}{|\alpha\circ\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}{|\alpha\circ\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}\right|}{|\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}{|\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}{|\eta(u)o^{+}-o^{+}|}-\ell
, for all
u\in U_{0}

ii.

\alpha\circ\eta(u)=\eta(u)
, for all
u\in U\setminus U_{0}
.

Let us translate this fact to the action of
\Gamma_{k}
on
X_{k}
.
According to
Lemma 7.47
, there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every integer
k\geqslant k_{0}
, there is an automorphism
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
preserving
\hat{S}_{k}
that lifts
\alpha
, while
\lambda_{\infty}(\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U)
is uniformly bounded.
Up to increasing the value of
k_{0}
, we can therefore assume that for every
k\geqslant k_{0}
and
u\in U\setminus U_{0}
, we have
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)=\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)
, hence

\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)=\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)=\varphi_{k}(u).

On the other hand, for every
u\in U_{0}
, Inequality
(C2)((A3))i
yields

\mathchoice{\left|\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}\right|}{|\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}{|\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}{|\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(u)o^{+}_{k}-o^{+}_{k}|}-\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),\ \omega\text{-as}.

Since
U
is finite, we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U,o^{+}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\varphi_{k},U,o^{+}_{k}\right)-\frac{\ell|U_{0}|}{\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right).

Recall that
o^{+}_{k}
(almost) minimizes the restricted energy – see Inequality (
18
). Hence we get

\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U\right)\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)-\frac{\ell|U_{0}|}{\varepsilon_{k}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right).

Recall that
\left|U_{0}\right|\geqslant 1
.
Hence the result follows from the previous inequality.
∎

7.5.2
Peripheral component

We now focus on the peripheral components.
This case is new compared to the situations handled in the literature.
Our goal in this section is to prove the following statement.

Theorem 7.51
.

Suppose that
\rho>0
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Let
v_{c}
be the vertex of
S_{\Lambda}
corresponding to
T\cap\bar{P}(c)

Assume that one of the following holds:

(A)

either the link of
v_{c}
contains at least two
L_{c}
-orbits, or

(B)

the pointwise stabilizer of
\bar{P}(c)
has infinite index in
L_{c}
,

Then there is
\ell\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that for every sufficiently large
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there exist
\alpha_{k}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(L,\Lambda)
and
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
preserving
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha_{k}\circ\zeta_{k}
and

\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U\right)\leqslant\lambda^{+}_{1}\left(\varphi_{k},U\right)-\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon_{k}}\ \omega\text{-as}

Moreover, in Case (A),
\alpha_{k}
is a Dehn twist over an edge of
S
adjacent to
v
, while in Case (B),
\alpha_{k}
is a generalized Dehn twist.

The proof of the statement is quite long and somewhat technical.
Therefore we break it in several parts.
A summary of the proof is given at the end of this section.
For this proof we actually do not need to work with the complete splitting
S
of
L
with respect to
T
.
We will only consider the skeleton
S_{\Lambda}
of the graph of action decomposition
\Lambda
of
T
given by
Theorem 7.44
.
Let us start with some notations.

Recall that
o^{+}=\lim_{\omega}o^{+}_{k}
is a base point in
T
such that
o^{+}_{k}
lies in the thick part of
X^{+}_{k}
of
X_{k}
as (almost) minimizes the restricted energy
\lambda_{+}(\varphi_{k},U)
of
\varphi_{k}
.
Consequently
o^{+}
belongs to a transverse component of
T
.
In particular there is a vertex
v^{+}
in
S_{\Lambda}
which does not lie in the
L
-orbit of a peripheral vertex of
S_{\Lambda}
and whose corresponding component
Y_{v^{+}}\subset T
contains
o^{+}
.

Remark
.

This property is precisely the reason that motivated the definition of the restricted energy.
It will greatly simplify the arguments to have the point
o^{+}
outside of the peripheral subtree
P(c)
.

We start with a lemma to provide more informations about the action of peripheral subgroups.

Lemma 7.52
.

Suppose that
\rho>0
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega}
.
Let
x=\lim_{\omega}x_{k}
and
x^{\prime}=\lim_{\omega}x^{\prime}_{k}
be two points in
X_{\omega}
such that
b_{c}(x)=b_{c}(x^{\prime})=0
. Let
g\in G
be such that
\eta(g)
is a non-trivial element fixing
\bar{P}(c)
pointwise.
Let
n\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
There exists a sequence
(g_{k})
of elements of
\mathchoice{\left\langle g\right\rangle}{\langle g\rangle}{\langle g\rangle}{\langle g\rangle}
with the following properties.

i.

\eta(g_{k})
pointwise fixes
\bar{P}(c)
for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

ii.

\lim_{\omega}\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(g^{n}_{k})x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{n}_{k})x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{n}_{k})x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{n}_{k})x_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}|}=0
.

iii.

If
y=\lim_{\omega}y_{k}
is a point of
X_{\omega}
such that
2b_{c}(y)\leqslant-\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}
, then for every
q\in\mathbf{Z}
,

\lim_{\omega}\mathchoice{\left|\varphi_{k}(g^{q}_{k})y_{k}-y_{k}\right|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{q}_{k})y_{k}-y_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{q}_{k})y_{k}-y_{k}|}{|\varphi_{k}(g^{q}_{k})y_{k}-y_{k}|}=0.

iv.

If
z=\lim_{\omega}z_{k}
and
z^{\prime}=\lim_{\omega}z^{\prime}_{k}
are two points of
X_{\omega}
with
\mathchoice{\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|}{|z-z^{\prime}|}{|z-z^{\prime}|}{|z-z^{\prime}|}\geqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}
and
