\mathbf{Z}
.
Hence it necessarily fixes a vertex
v
of
X_{\omega}
.
In particular, every generator of
U
fixes
v
.
We write
v
as the limit
v=\lim_{\omega}v_{k}
of a sequence of vertices
v_{k}\in X_{k}
.
Since
U
is finite, for every
u\in U
, the element
\varphi_{k}(u)
fixes
v_{k}

\omega
-as.
Hence the image of
\varphi_{k}
fixes
v_{k}

\omega
-as, or in other words the image of
\varphi_{k}
is contained in a conjugate of
A_{k}
or
B_{k}
.
This contradicts our assumptions and completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.9
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated, freely indecomposable group with no essential root splitting.
There exist a finite subset
W\subset G\setminus\{1\}
and a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, with the following properties.

Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
be two non-trivial CSA groups.
For every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to A\ast^{n}B
whose image is not abelian, one of the following holds:

—

there exists an automorphism
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(G\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}
such that
W\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha)\neq\emptyset
;

—

the image of
\varphi
is contained in a conjugate of
A
or
B
.

Proof.

Let
U
be a finite generating set of
G
and
F
the free group generated by
U
.
Let
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
be the corresponding epimorphism.
We denote by
W\subset G\setminus\{1\}
and
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
the first two parameters given by
Proposition 8.5
to lift morphisms from
G
to periodic groups.
Now that
\lambda
is fixed,
Proposition 8.8
gives us a finite subset
V\subset F
to analyze morphisms from
G
to a free product.
According to
Proposition 8.5
, there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the following holds.
Let
(\Gamma,X)
be a
1
-bootstrap for the exponent
n
.
For every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
whose image is not abelian, there exists
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(G\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}
such that one of the following holds:

—

W\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha)\neq\emptyset
;

—

there is a morphism
\psi\colon F\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi
relative to
V
such that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi,U)\leqslant\lambda
.

Note that we can always replace
W
by a larger subset so that it also contains
\pi(V)\setminus\{1\}
.

Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
be two non-trivial CSA groups.
Let
\varphi\colon G\to A\ast^{n}B
be a morphism whose image is not abelian.
Assume that
W\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha)
is empty for every
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(G\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}
.
We denote by
\Gamma
the (regular) free product
\Gamma=A\ast B
and let it act on its Bass-Serre tree
X
.
Hence
(\Gamma,X)
is a
1
-bootstrap for the exponent
n
.
According to our choice of
N
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(G\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}{\operatorname{Aut}(G)}
and a morphism
\psi\colon F\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi
relative to
V
such that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi,U)\leqslant\lambda
.
We claim that
V\cap\ker\psi=V\cap\ker\pi
.
Since
\psi
is a lift of
\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi
relative to
V
, it suffices to show that
V\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi)=V\cap\ker\pi
.
Note that
\ker\pi
is contained in
\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi)
.
By construction
\pi(V)\setminus\{1\}
is contained in
W
while
W\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha)=\emptyset
.
Hence
V\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi)\subset V\cap\ker\pi
, which completes the proof of our claim.

Combining this claim with
Proposition 8.8
, we obtain that the image of
\psi
is contained in a conjugate of
A
or
B
(seen as a subgroup of
A\ast B
).
Recall that
\psi
is a lift of
\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\pi
.
Consequently the image of
\varphi
is contained in a conjugate of
A
or
B
(seen this time as a subgroup of
A\ast^{n}B
).
∎

8.3
Shortening limit groups with no root splitting

In this section, we fix once and for all a non-elementary hyperbolic group
\Gamma
acting properly co-compactly on a hyperbolic length space
X
, so that
(\Gamma,X)
is a
\tau
-bootstrap for some
\tau\in(0,1)
.
The goal is to prove the existence of shortening quotients that will allow us to apply the material of
Section 4.3
.

Recall that the definition of a shortening limit group (
Definition 4.11
) relies on a group property
\mathcal{P}
.
The one we use here is the following: a group
H
has
\mathcal{P}
if all its abelian subgroups are finitely generated.
This property being fixed for the remainder of this section, we make an abuse of vocabulary and say that a sequence / limit group is
shortenable
if it is shortenable with respect to this property.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
G
be a finitely presented group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
If
L
is freely indecomposable with no essential root splitting, then
(L,\eta)
is shortenable.

Proof.

We fix once and for all a finite generating set
U
of
G
.
Note that if
L
is abelian, then the conclusion directly follows from
Remark 4.12
.
Therefore, from now on we assume that
L
is non-abelian.
Consider a sequence
(n_{k})
of odd integers diverging to infinity and a sequence of morphisms
\varphi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
such that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta)
.

We denote by
S_{\rm JSJ}
the tree of cylinders of a JSJ tree of
L
(over the abelian subgroups of
L
).
In addition we fix a sequence
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}_{k})
of finitely presented strong covers of
(L,\eta,S_{\rm JSJ})
converging to
(L,\eta,S_{\rm JSJ})
.
We denote by
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L_{k}
the projection satisfying
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}=\eta
.
By construction there is a finite subset
W_{k}\subset G
which generates
\ker\eta_{k}
as a normal subgroup of
G
.
We denote by
\ell_{k}
the length of the longest element in
W_{k}
, seen as a word over
U
.
Up to replacing
(\varphi_{k})
by a subsequence, we can assume that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\rho(n_{k})}{\ell_{k}}=\infty,

while every
\varphi_{k}
factors through
\hat{\eta}_{k}
. We write
\hat{\varphi}_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
for the resulting morphism.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{k}
be the set of all automorphisms
\hat{\alpha}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
lifting a modular automorphism
\alpha\in\operatorname{Mod}(L)
, i.e. such that
\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}=\alpha\circ\zeta_{k}
.
In addition we let

\mathcal{P}_{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}\;:\ \hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\right\}}{\{\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}\;:\ \hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\}}{\{\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}\;:\ \hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\}}{\{\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}\;:\ \hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\}}.

For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we fix an approximation sequence

(\Gamma_{j,k},X_{j,k},\mathcal{C}_{j,k})_{j\in\mathbf{N}}

of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
, as given by
Theorem 3.11
.
Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we chose
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
such that the morphism
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}=\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}
is
\varepsilon
-short among
\mathcal{P}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
, in the sense of
Definition 8.1
.
Up to passing to a subsequence
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
converges to a limit group that we denote by
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
.
We are going to prove that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})
is a shortening of
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi_{k})
.

Recall that
\hat{\eta}_{k}
is onto while
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
is an automorphism of
\hat{L}_{k}
.
Consequently
\varphi_{k}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
have the same image of every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, proving Point
1
of
Definition 4.11
.
By construction
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is a quotient of
(L,\eta)
, which corresponds to Point
2
of
Definition 4.11
.
We now focus on Point
3
of this definition.
Assume that
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=(L,\eta)
.

Claim 8.11
.

There exists
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for all but finitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there is a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
, such that
\lambda_{\infty}(\varphi^{\prime}_{k},U)\leqslant\lambda
.

Proof of the claim.

For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we denote by
\psi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
an optimal approximation of
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
(see
Definition 8.1
).
By construction

W_{k}\subset\ker\hat{\eta}_{k}\subset\ker\varphi^{\prime}_{k}

Hence
W_{k}
is also contained in
\ker\psi_{k}
.
We chose
W_{k}
in such a way that it generates
\ker\hat{\eta}_{k}
as a normal subgroup.
Consequently
\psi_{k}
factors through
\hat{\eta}_{k}
.
The resulting morphism
\hat{\psi}_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\to\Gamma_{j_{k},k}
is an approximation of
\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}
.
It follows in particular that
(\Gamma_{j_{k},k},\psi_{k})
converges to
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=(L,\eta)
.

Assume now that our claim fails, that is up to passing to a subsequence, any lift
\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma
of
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
, if it exists, satisfies
\lambda_{\infty}(\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k},U)\geqslant k
.
We are going to prove that for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
,

\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{\rho(n_{k})}{100\ell_{k}},k\right\}

Let
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
If
j_{k}\geqslant 1
, then the result follows from
Proposition 3.14
.
If on the contrary
j_{k}=0
, that is
\Gamma_{j_{k},k}=\Gamma
, then
\psi_{k}\colon G\to\Gamma
is a lift of
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
, and the statement follows from our assumption.
It follows that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
diverges to infinity, that is
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is a divergent limit group.

Proceeding as in
Section 7
, we build an action of
L^{\prime}
on a limit
\mathbf{R}
-tree
T
.
This action decomposes as a graph of actions, where each vertex action is either peripheral, simplicial, axial or has Seifert type (
Theorem 7.44
).
We denote by
S^{\prime}
the complete splitting of
L
with respect to
T
(see
Definition 7.45
).

Recall that
L
is freely indecomposable, without even torsion.
According to
Theorem 6.9
, the trees
S_{\rm JSJ}
and
S^{\prime}
are compatible.
We write
R
for a common refinement.
By definition of the JSJ decomposition,
R
is obtained from
S_{\rm JSJ}
by splitting some of its flexible vertices, which are either surface groups or abelian groups. Let
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
It follows from the definition of strong cover that the
\zeta_{k}
-equivariant map
\hat{S}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow S_{\rm JSJ}
induces a bijection from
\hat{S}_{k}/\hat{L}_{k}
to
S_{\rm JSJ}/L
.
Let
\hat{v}
be a vertex of
\hat{S}_{k}
and
v
its image in
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
If
L_{v}
is a surface group ( respectively abelian group) then the projection
\zeta_{k}
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
onto
L_{v}
( respectively an embedding of
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
onto
L_{v}
).
Splitting some vertices of
\hat{S}_{k}
, as we did with
S_{\rm JSJ}
to obtain
R
, we build a refinement
\hat{R}_{k}
of
\hat{S}_{k}
such that
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{R}_{k})
is a strong cover of
(L,\eta,R)
.
We now collapse in
\hat{R}_{k}
edges corresponding to edges of
R
which are collapsed in
S^{\prime}
.
We obtained is this way a new
\hat{L}_{k}
-tree
\hat{S}^{\prime}_{k}
such that
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}^{\prime}_{k})
is a strong cover of
(L,\eta,S^{\prime})
, provided
k
is sufficiently large.
It follows from the construction that the following equivariant diagram commute.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{S}^{\prime}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{R}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{S}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rm JSJ}}

One checks that
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}^{\prime}_{k})
converges to
(L,\eta,S^{\prime})
in the sense of
Definition 5.7
.

According to our assumption
L^{\prime}=L
is freely indecomposable and does not admit any essential root splitting.
Hence we can perform the shortening argument (
Section 7.5
) with
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}^{\prime}_{k})
as the sequence of strong covers. By
Theorem 7.46
, there exists
\kappa>0
, as well as automorphisms
\beta_{k}
in
\operatorname{Mod}(L,S^{\prime})
and
\hat{\beta}_{k}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\hat{L}_{k})}
such that for infinitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
\beta_{k}\circ\zeta_{k}=\zeta_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k}
and

\lambda_{1}^{+}(\hat{\psi}_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U)<(1-\kappa)\lambda_{1}^{+}(\psi_{k},U)

By compatibility
\beta_{k}
(and thus
\alpha_{k}\circ\beta_{k}
) is also an element of
\operatorname{Mod}(L)
(
Proposition 6.11
).
Hence
\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{k}
.
Recall that
\psi_{k}
is an optimal approximation of
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}=\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}
.
Observe that
\hat{\psi}_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}
is an approximation of
\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ(\hat{\alpha}_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k})\circ\hat{\eta}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
.
Since
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
is
\varepsilon
-short among
\mathcal{P}_{k}
relative to
W_{k}
, this approximation is necessarily minimal.
Moreover it satisfies

\lambda_{1}^{+}(\psi_{k},U)\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{+}(\hat{\psi}_{k}\circ\hat{\beta}_{k}\circ\hat{\eta}_{k},U)+\varepsilon\leqslant(1-\kappa)\lambda_{1}^{+}(\psi_{k},U)+\varepsilon.

Recall that
\lambda_{\infty}(\psi_{k},U)
diverges to infinity.
This leads to a contradiction if
k
is sufficiently large, and completes the proof of our claim.
∎

We observe as above that
W_{k}\subset\ker\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k}
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, so that
(\Gamma,\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k})
converges to
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
as well.
Up to conjugacy there are only finitely many morphisms
G\to\Gamma
whose energy is at most
\lambda
.
Hence, up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
\ker\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k}
is constant, equal to
K
say.
In particular,
L=G/K
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, hence every abelian subgroup of
L
is finitely generated.
In addition all but finitely many
\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
.
Thus all but finitely many
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of Point
3
of
Definition 4.11
.

The proof of Point
4
in
Definition 4.11
follows the standard strategy, see for instance
[
51
,
52
,
58
]
.
We quickly go through the main arguments.
Assume that every abelian subgroup of
L^{\prime}
is finitely generated.
Given
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we partition the vertices of
\hat{S}_{k}
as follows: a vertex
\hat{v}
belongs to
V_{k}^{0}
( respectively
V_{k}^{1}
) if the stabilizer of its image
v
in
S_{\rm JSJ}
is a surface group or an abelian group ( respectively a rigid group).
If
\hat{v}
belongs to
V_{k}^{1}
then
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
acts on the vertex group
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
by conjugation.
It follows that the projection
L\twoheadrightarrow L^{\prime}
is one-to-one when restricted to every rigid vertex group of
L
.

We now claim that the edge groups of
S_{\rm JSJ}
are finitely generated.
Consider an edge
e
of
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
Since
L
is CSA, it cannot connect two vertices whose stabilizers are both abelian.
Consequently
L_{e}
is either contained in a surface group or a rigid group.
If
L_{e}
is contained in a surface subgroup, then
L_{e}
is virtually cyclic.
If
L_{e}
is contained in a rigid vertex group, then according to our previous discussion,
L_{e}
embeds in
L^{\prime}
.
It follows from our assumption that
L_{e}
is finitely generated, which completes the proof of our claim.

We now show that every abelian subgroup
A
of
L
is finitely generated.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
A
is not virtually cyclic.
Since the action of
L
on
S_{\rm JSJ}
is acylindrical,
A
fixes a vertex
v
.
This vertex cannot be a surface group, otherwise
A
would be virtually cyclic.
Assume that the stabilizer of
v
is abelian.
Since
L
is finitely generated,
L_{v}
is finitely generated relative to the stabilizers of the edges in the link of
v
.
We just proved that edge groups are finitely generated.
Consequently
L_{v}
and thus
A
are finitely generated.
Suppose now that the stabilizer of
v
is rigid.
As previously
A
embeds in
L^{\prime}
, and thus is finitely generated.

We proved that every edge group and every abelian vertex group of
S_{\rm JSJ}
is finitely generated.
Consequently if
k
is sufficiently large, the strong cover
(\hat{L}_{k},\hat{\eta}_{k},\hat{S}_{k})
satisfies the additional following properties.

—

Let
\hat{v}
be a vertex of
V_{k}^{0}
and
v
its image in
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
The map
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
onto
L_{v}
.

—

Let
\hat{e}
be a an edge of
\hat{S}_{k}
and
e
its image in
S_{\rm JSJ}
.
The map
\zeta_{k}\colon\hat{L}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow L
induces an isomorphism from
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{e}}
onto
L_{e}
.

Hence the kernel of
\zeta_{k}
is generated (as a normal subgroup) by a subset
U_{k}
with the following property: for every
g\in U_{k}
, there exists a vertex
\hat{v}
of
V_{k}^{1}
such that
g
belongs to
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
.

Suppose now that all but finitely many
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
factor through
\eta^{\prime}
.
Since
(L^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\prec(L,\eta)
those morphisms also factor through
\eta
. Consequently if
k
is sufficiently large
U_{k}
is contained in
\ker\hat{\varphi}_{k}\circ\hat{\alpha}_{k}
.
As we observed before, for every
\hat{v}\in V_{k}^{1}
, the automorphism
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
acts on
\hat{L}_{k,\hat{v}}
by conjugation.
Consequently,
U_{k}
is also contained in
\ker\hat{\varphi}_{k}
.
However
U_{k}
generates the kernel of
\zeta_{k}
(as a normal subgroup).
It follows that
\hat{\varphi}_{k}
factors through
\zeta_{k}
.
Thus
\varphi_{k}
factors through
\eta
.
This complete the proof of Point
4
of
Definition 4.11
.
∎

The next statement extends the previous result, when
L
is not necessarily freely indecomposable.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
G
be a finitely presented group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
.
If
L
has no essential root splitting, then
(L,\eta)
is shortenable.

Proof.

In view of
Proposition 8.10
, we can assume that
L
is freely decomposable.
Consider the Grushko decomposition
L
,

L=L_{0}\ast L_{1}\ast\dots\ast L_{m}\ast F

where
L_{i}
is a non-trivial, freely indecomposable subgroup and
F
a finitely generated free group.
We fix a finitely presented cover
(\hat{L},\hat{\eta})
of
(L,\eta)
with the following properties

—

\hat{L}
splits as
\hat{L}=\hat{L}_{0}\ast\dots\ast\hat{L}_{m}\ast\hat{F}
.

—

The morphism
\zeta\colon\hat{L}\twoheadrightarrow L
maps each
\hat{L}_{i}
onto
L_{i}
.
Moreover it induces an isomorphism from
\hat{F}
onto
F
.

For every
i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, we write
\zeta_{i}\lx@nobreakspace\colon\hat{L}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow L_{i}
for the restriction of
\zeta
to
\hat{L}_{i}
.
Similarly we write
\zeta_{F}\colon\hat{F}\twoheadrightarrow F
for the isomorphism induced by
\zeta
.
Note that no
L_{i}
admits an essential root splitting, otherwise so would
L
.
Hence by
Proposition 8.10
, the group
(L_{i},\zeta_{i})\in\mathfrak{G}(\hat{L}_{i})
is shortenable.
Since
F
is free, it has the factorization property, hence
(F,\zeta_{F})
is shortenable (see
Remark 4.12
). According to
Proposition 4.14
,
(L,\zeta)\in\mathfrak{G}(\hat{L})
is shortenable.
It follows then from
Lemma 4.13
that
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
is shortenable.
∎

In this context,
Corollary 4.18
yields the following statement.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
G
be a finitely presented group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
If no quotient of
L
has an essential root splitting, then
L
satisfies the factorization property.

We now explore the consequences of this fact for the lifting problem tackled in the introduction.
We start with the case of morphisms with abelian image.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated abelian group.
Let
p
the least common multiple of the orders of its elements.
There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd exponent
n\geqslant N
co-prime with
p
, every morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
lifts to a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma
.

Proof.

There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, every abelian subgroup of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is cyclic, see
Theorem 3.11

2
.
Consider a morphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
where
n\geqslant N
is an odd exponent co-prime with
p
.
We decompose
G
as
G=G_{0}\oplus G_{1}
where
G_{0}
is free abelian and
G_{1}
finite.
The order of any element of
G_{1}
is co-prime with
n
.
Hence
\varphi
maps
G_{1}
to the trivial group.
Consequently, it suffices to lift
\varphi
restricted to
G_{0}
.
This is always possible since every abelian subgroup of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is cyclic.
∎

Theorem 8.15
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated free group and
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
an epimorphism where no quotient of
G
admits an essential root splitting.
For every finite subset
V\subset F
there are
N\in\mathbf{N}
and a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
with the following property.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer and
\varphi\colon F\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
be a morphism whose image is not abelian.
If
W\subset\ker\varphi
, then there is a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon F\to\Gamma
such that
\tilde{\varphi}
lifts
\varphi
relative to
V
.

Proof.

Let
V\subset F
be a finite subset.
We fix a non-decreasing exhaustion
(W_{k})
of
\ker\pi
by finite subsets.
Assume that the statement is false.
There is a sequence of morphisms
\varphi_{k}\colon F\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
with the following properties:

—

(n_{k})
is a sequence of odd integers diverging to infinity,

—

W_{k}\subset\ker\varphi_{k}
, for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
,

—

the image of every
\varphi_{k}
is non abelian,

—

no
\varphi_{k}
can be lifted relative to
V
.

Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi_{k})
converges to a limit group
(L,\eta)
.
Since
W_{k}\subset\ker\varphi_{k}
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we observe that
(L,\eta)\prec(G,\pi)
.
In particular, no quotient of
L
admits a root splitting.
Hence
(L,\eta)
has the factorization property (
Proposition 8.13
).
Up to passing again to a subsequence, every
\varphi_{k}
factors through
\eta
.
We write
\mu_{k}\colon L\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
for the resulting morphism.

Note that the image of
\mu_{k}
is non abelian (since the one of
\varphi_{k}
is non abelian either).
We claim that if
k
is sufficiently large, then
\mu_{k}
cannot be lifted.
Assume on the contrary that there is a morphism
\tilde{\mu}_{k}\colon L\to\Gamma
lifting
\mu_{k}
.
Then the map
\tilde{\varphi}_{k}=\tilde{\mu}_{k}\circ\eta
lifts
\varphi_{k}
so that
V\cap\ker\tilde{\varphi}_{k}\subset V\cap\ker\varphi_{k}
.
In addition if
k
is sufficiently large, then
V\cap\ker\varphi_{k}=V\cap\ker\eta
.
Hence
V\cap\ker\varphi_{k}\subset V\cap\ker\tilde{\varphi}_{k}
.
In other words,
\tilde{\varphi}_{k}
lifts
\varphi_{k}
relative to
V
.
It contradicts the definition of
\varphi_{k}
and completes the proof of our claim.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we now assume that none of the
\mu_{k}
can be lifted.

We are now going to produce an infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L,\eta)

(L,\eta)=(L_{0},\eta_{0})\succ(L_{1},\eta_{1})\succ(L_{2},\eta_{2})\succ\dots

and for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, a sequence of morphisms
\mu_{k}^{i}\colon L_{i}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
so that, up to passing to a subsequence, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, the image of
\mu_{k}^{i}
is non abelian, and
\mu_{k}^{i}
does not lift.
We begin with
(L_{0},\eta_{0})=(L,\eta)
and let
\mu_{k}^{0}=\mu_{k}
, for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

Let
i\in\mathbf{N}
for which all the above objects have beed already defined.
If needed, we pass to a subsequence so that for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, the image of
\mu_{k}^{i}
is non abelian and
\mu_{k}^{i}
does not lift to a morphism
\tilde{\mu}_{k}^{i}\colon L_{i}\to\Gamma
.

Case 1.

Assume first that
L_{i}
is freely indecomposable.
If follows from
Corollary 8.7
that up to passing to a subsequence, there is an element
g_{i}\in L_{i}\setminus\{1\}
and for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, an automorphism
\alpha_{k}^{i}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(L_{i}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(L_{i})}{\operatorname{Aut}(L_{i})}{\operatorname{Aut}(L_{i})}
such that
\mu_{k}^{i}\circ\alpha_{k}^{i}(g_{i})=1
.
For every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we let

\varphi_{k}^{i+1}=\mu_{k}^{i}\circ\alpha_{k}^{i}\circ\eta_{i}.

Up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\varphi_{k}^{i+1})
converges to a limit group
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})\prec(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
Note that
L_{i+1}
is a proper quotient of
L_{i}
.
Indeed by construction, the element
g_{i}
lies in the kernel of the projection
\zeta_{i}\colon L_{i}\twoheadrightarrow L_{i+1}
.
Since
L_{i+1}
is a quotient of
L
, no quotient of
L_{i+1}
admits an essential root splitting.
Consequently
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
has the factorization property (
Proposition 8.13
).
Up to passing to a subsequence, every
\varphi_{k}^{i+1}
factors through
\eta_{i+1}
.
By construction the resulting morphism
\mu_{k}^{i+1}\colon L_{i+1}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}}
satisfies

\mu_{k}^{i}=\mu_{k}^{i+1}\circ\zeta_{i}\circ\left(\alpha_{k}^{i}\right)^{-1},\quad\forall k\in\mathbf{N}.

Consequently the image of
\mu_{k}^{i+1}
is non abelian.
Moreover
\mu_{k}^{i+1}
cannot lift to a morphism
\tilde{\mu}_{k}^{i+1}\colon L_{i+1}\to\Gamma
.
Otherwise it would contradict the properties of
\mu_{k}^{i}
.

Case 2.

Assume now that
L_{i}
is freely decomposable.
Since
\mu_{k}^{i}
does not lift, its restrictions to the free factors of a Grushko decomposition of
L_{i}
cannot lift all simultaneously.
We can decompose
L_{i}
as

L_{i}=M\ast L_{i+1}

where
L_{i+1}
is a freely indecomposable free factor of
L_{i}
and
M
a non-trivial subgroup of
L_{i}
.
Moreover, up to passing to a subsequence, the restriction of
\mu_{k}^{i}
to
L_{i+1}
, that we denote by
\mu_{k}^{i+1}
, does not lift to a morphism
L_{i+1}\to\Gamma
.
We denote by
\eta_{i+1}
the morphism
\eta_{i}
composed with projection
L_{i}\twoheadrightarrow L_{i+1}
(obtained by “killing” the factor
M
) so that
(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
is a limit group and a proper quotient of
(L_{i},\eta_{i})
.
We are left to prove that (up to passing to a subsequence) the image of
\mu_{k}^{i+1}
is not abelian, for infinitely many
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Assume that it is not the case.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n_{k}},\mu_{k}^{i+1}\circ\eta_{i+1})
converges to an abelian limit group
(B,\beta)\prec(L_{i+1},\eta_{i+1})
.
We decompose
B
as
B=B_{0}\oplus B_{1}
where
B_{0}
is a free abelian and
B_{1}
finite.
Note that
B_{1}
is actually trivial.
Indeed, otherwise the group
Q=M\ast B
, which is a quotient of
L
, admits the following non-essential root splitting

Q=(M\ast B_{0})\ast_{B_{0}}B,

which contradicts our assumption.
In other words
B
is free abelian.
Up to passing to a subsequence, every
\mu_{k}^{i+1}
factors through
B
and thus can be lifted (
Lemma 8.14
), which contradicts our construction.

In both cases, we have built all the announced objects for the index
i+1
.
Hence the induction is complete.

No quotient of
L
admits a root splitting.
It follows from
Corollary 8.12
that every limit group
(Q,\nu)\prec(L,\eta)
is shortenable.
Hence there is no infinite descending sequence of limit groups starting at
(L,\eta)
(
Proposition 4.17
).
This contradicts our previous construction and completes the proof of the result.
∎

Corollary 8.16
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group, none of whose quotients admits an essential root splitting.
For every finite subset
V\subset G
, there is a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the following holds.
For every homomorphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
whose image is not abelian, there exists a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon G\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi
relative to
V
.

Proof.

We fix a projection
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
, where
F
is a finitely generated free group.
Being hyperbolic,
\Gamma
is equationally noetherian, thus there is a finite subset
\tilde{V}_{0}\subset\ker\pi
such that every morphism
\varphi\lx@nobreakspace\colon F\to\Gamma
whose kernel contains
\tilde{V}_{0}
factors through
\pi
.

Let
V
be a finite subset of
G
.
We fix a finite subset
\tilde{V}\subset F
containing
\tilde{V}_{0}
such that
\pi(\tilde{V})=V
.
We write
W\subset\ker\pi
and
N\in\mathbf{N}
for the data given by
Theorem 8.15
applied with
\tilde{V}
.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer and
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
a homomorphism.
Note that
W
is contained in the kernel of
\varphi\circ\pi
.
According to
Theorem 8.15
, there is a morphism
\psi\colon F\to\Gamma
lifting
\varphi\circ\pi
relative to
\tilde{V}
.
In particular,
\tilde{V}_{0}
is contained in the kernel of
\psi
, and thus
\psi
factors through
\pi
.
We write
\tilde{\varphi}\colon F\to\Gamma
for the resulting morphism.
It follows from the construction that
\tilde{\varphi}
lifts
\varphi
relative to
V
.
∎

The next two corollaries are obtained in the exact same way, using the fact that
\Gamma
is equationally noetherian.
Their proofs are left to the reader.

Corollary 8.17
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated group and
\pi\colon F\twoheadrightarrow G
a projection so that no quotient of
G
admits an essential root splitting.
There exist a finite subset
W\subset\ker\pi
and a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
with the following property.
For every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, for every homomorphism
\varphi\colon G\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, if
W\subset\ker\varphi
, then
\varphi
factors through
\pi
.

Corollary 8.18
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group.
Let
(L,\eta)\in\mathfrak{G}(G)
be a limit group.
If no quotient of
L
admits an essential root splitting, then one of the following holds

—

L
is an abelian group whose torsion part is not trivial

—

(L,\eta)
is a limit group over
\Gamma
.

Remark 8.19
.

If we restrict ourselves to limit groups over the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
with
prime
exponents, then the first case never happens and every limit group none of whose quotient admits an essential root splitting is a limit group over
\Gamma
.

9
Applications

9.1
Groups with no root splitting

We focus here on freely indecomposable, hyperbolic groups
\Gamma
without essential root splitting.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a torsion-free, hyperbolic group, none of whose quotients admit an abelian splitting.
There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the periodic quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is Hopfian.

Proof.

If
\Gamma
is cyclic, then
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is finite, hence the result is straightforward.
We now assume that
\Gamma
is non-elementary.
We apply
Corollary 8.4
with the group
G=\Gamma
.
Let
N
be the corresponding critical exponent and
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m}
the associated list of morphisms
G\to\Gamma
.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
For simplicity we write
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
for the canonical projection.
Let
\varphi
be a surjective morphism from
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
onto itself.
Assume that contrary to our claim
\varphi
is not injective.
Hence
K_{p}=\ker(\varphi^{p})
is an increasing sequence of subgroups of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
Let
p\in\mathbf{N}
.
The composition
\varphi^{p}\circ\pi
is a homomorphism from
\Gamma
onto
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
In particular, its image is not abelian.
It follows from
Corollary 8.4
there is
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
such that a conjugate of
\psi_{i}
lifts
\varphi^{p}\circ\pi
.
Hence there exist two integers
p>q
, together with
\gamma\in\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, such that
\varphi^{p}=\iota_{\gamma}\circ\varphi^{q}
.
Hence
K_{p}=K_{q}
which contradicts the fact that the sequence
(K_{p})
is increasing.
∎

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
If
\Gamma
has no essential root splitting, then the following are equivalent.

A.

The group
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable.

B.

For every
N\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists an odd integer
n\geqslant N
such that the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

C.

There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd exponent
n\geqslant N
, the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Proof.

Observe that
(C2)(T2)C

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)B
is obvious.
Let us prove
(C2)(T2)B

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)A
.
Assume that
(C2)(T2)A
does not holds, that is
\Gamma
splits as a free product
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\freep\Gamma_{2}
.
Note that
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are quasi-convex subgroups of
\Gamma
.
Hence they are either infinite cyclic or non-elementary torsion-free hyperbolic groups.
In particular, there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd exponent
n\geqslant N
, their
n
-periodic quotients are non-trivial.
Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be the respective
n
-periodic quotients of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
.
One checks that
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is isomorphic to
B_{1}\ast^{n}B_{2}
.
Hence
(C2)(T2)B
does not hold.

Observe first that if
\Gamma
is cyclic, then
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
provided
n
is sufficiently large.
Indeed assume on the contrary that
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is isomorphic to
A_{1}\ast^{n}A_{2}
where
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are two non-trivial
n
-periodic groups.
If
n
is sufficiently large then the projection
A_{1}\ast A_{2}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is one-to-one on the set of elements whose word length is at most four, see
Theorem 3.11
4
.
In particular if
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are non-trivial element of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively, then the commutator
[a_{1},a_{2}]
is non trivial in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
This contradicts the fact that
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is cyclic.

From now on we assume that
\Gamma
is non-elementary.
Let us now focus on
(C2)(T2)A

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)C
.
We now apply
Corollary 8.9
with
G=\Gamma
.
We write
N\in\mathbf{N}
and
W\subset\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
for the data given by this statement.
Up to increasing the value of
N
we can assume that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the intersection
W\cap\Gamma^{n}
is empty (see
Remark 3.12
).

Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
For simplicity we write
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
for the canonical projection.
Assume that contrary to our claim,
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
splits as a free product in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, i.e. there exist two non-trivial groups
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and an isomorphism
\theta\colon\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\to B_{1}\ast^{n}B_{2}
.
In particular,
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
can be seen as subgroups of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
, hence they are CSA.

The projection
\pi
induces a map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
that we write
\alpha\mapsto\alpha_{n}
.
In particular,
\theta\circ\pi\circ\alpha=\theta\circ\alpha_{n}\circ\pi
, for every
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}
.
As
\theta
and
\alpha_{n}
are one-to-one, the kernel of the map
\theta\circ\alpha_{n}\circ\pi
is
\Gamma^{n}
.
Hence
W\cap\ker(\theta\circ\pi\circ\alpha)
is empty, for every
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}
.
According to
Corollary 8.9
, the image of
\theta
is contained in a conjugate of
B_{1}
or
B_{2}
, which contradicts the fact that
\theta
is onto.
∎

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two torsion-free, hyperbolic groups.
Assume that
\Gamma_{1}
is freely indecomposable with no essential root splitting and
\Gamma_{2}
is non-elementary.
There exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the following holds.

Given any monomorphism
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
, there exists a monomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\Gamma_{1}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}
lifting
\varphi
, i.e. such that the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}}
\tilde{\varphi}
\varphi

Remark
.

Note that we allow
\Gamma_{1}
to be cyclic.

Proof.

Let
X_{2}
be a Cayley graph of
\Gamma_{2}
.
It is a
\tau
-bootstrap for some
\tau\in(0,1)
.
We now apply
Corollary 8.7
with
G=\Gamma_{1}
.
We write
N\in\mathbf{N}
and
W\subset\Gamma_{1}\setminus\{1\}
for the data given by this proposition.
Up to increasing the value of
N
we can assume that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the intersection
W\cap\Gamma_{1}^{n}
is empty (see
Remark 3.12
).

Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer.
For simplicity we write
\pi_{i}\colon\Gamma_{i}\to\Gamma_{i}/\Gamma_{i}^{n}
for the canonical projection.
Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
be a monomorphism.
Recall that
\pi_{1}
induces a map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{1}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n})}
that we write
\alpha\mapsto\alpha_{n}
.
In particular,
\varphi\circ\pi_{1}\circ\alpha=\varphi\circ\alpha_{n}\circ\pi_{1}
, for every
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{1}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}
.
As
\varphi
and
\alpha_{n}
are one-to-one, the kernel of
\varphi\circ\alpha_{n}\circ\pi_{1}
coincides with
\Gamma_{1}^{n}
.
Hence
W\cap\ker(\varphi\circ\pi_{1}\circ\alpha)
is empty, for every
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{1}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{1})}
.
Applying
Corollary 8.7
, there exists a monomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
such that
\pi_{2}\circ\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi\circ\pi_{1}
, whence the result.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic groups.
Assume that
\Gamma_{1}
has no essential root splitting.
There exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the following holds.

Given any monomorphism
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
, there exists a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
lifting
\varphi
whose restriction to every freely indecomposable, free factors of
\Gamma_{1}
is one-to-one.

Proof.

Consider the Grushko decomposition of
\Gamma_{1}

\Gamma_{1}=A_{1}\ast\dots\ast A_{m}\ast F,

where each
A_{j}
is non-cyclic, freely indecomposable and
F
is a free group.
Since each
A_{j}
is quasi-convex in
\Gamma_{1}
it is also torsion-free hyperbolic.
Observe also that
A_{j}
has no non-essential root splitting, for otherwise
\Gamma_{1}
would admit a non-essential root splitting.
Hence we can apply for each
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket

Theorem 9.3
replacing
\Gamma_{1}
by
A_{j}
.
We write
N_{j}
for the critical exponent given by this theorem.
Let
N=\max\{N_{1},\dots,N_{m}\}
.

Let
n\geqslant N
be an odd integer and
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\to\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
a monomorphism.
As we observed in the proof
Proposition 1.5
the periodic quotient
A_{j}/A_{j}^{n}
embeds into
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
.
Consequently
\varphi
induces an embedding of
A_{j}/A_{j}^{n}
into
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
.
According to
Theorem 9.3
the latter lifts to a monomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}_{j}\colon A_{j}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}
.
Since
F
is a free group the map

\mu\colon F\to\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi\ }\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}

also lifts to a map
\tilde{\mu}\colon F\to\Gamma_{2}
(which may not be one-to-one though, unless
F
is cyclic).
We write
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
for the map whose restriction to
A_{j}
( respectively
F
) is
\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
( respectively
\tilde{\mu}
).
By construction it is a lift of
\varphi
.
∎

Corollary 9.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, freely indecomposable, hyperbolic group with no essential root splitting.
There exists a critical exponent
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, the following holds.

A.

The map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
is onto.

B.

The quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is co-Hopfian

Proof.

It suffices to prove that if
n
is a sufficiently large odd integer then every monomorphism of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
belongs to the image of the map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)}\to\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
.
To that end, we apply
Theorem 9.3
with
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
.
Hence every monomorphism
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is the image of a monomorphism
\Gamma\hookrightarrow\Gamma
(provided
n
is large enough).
Since
\Gamma
is freely indecomposable, it is co-Hopfian.
Thus every monomorphism
\varphi\colon\Gamma\hookrightarrow\Gamma
is actually an automorphism, whence the result.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group splitting as a free product
\Gamma=A_{1}\ast A_{2}
.
For every
n\in\mathbf{N}
, the groups
A_{1}/A_{1}^{n}
and
A_{2}/A_{2}^{n}
embed in
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
with trivial intersection.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
The natural embedding
A_{1}\hookrightarrow\Gamma
as well as the canonical projections
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow A_{1}\times A_{2}
and
A_{1}\times A_{2}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}
induce maps on the corresponding periodic quotients so that the following diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\times A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}/A_{i}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}/A_{1}^{n}\times A_{2}/A_{2}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}/A_{i}^{n}}

Note also the top row is just the identity of
A_{i}
.
The result now follows from chasing diagram.
∎

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two torsion-free, hyperbolic groups with no essential root splitting.
There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that the following are equivalent.

A.

\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are isomorphic.

B.

There exists an odd integer
n\geqslant N
, such that the groups
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic.

C.

For every integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, the groups
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Observe that
(C2)(T2)A

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)C
and
(C2)(T2)C

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)B
are obvious, whatever the value of
N
is.
Note that if
\Gamma_{1}
or
\Gamma_{2}
is cyclic, the result is straightforward.
Indeed if
n
is a sufficiently large odd integer, then the
n
-periodic quotient of a non-elementary torsion-free hyperbolic group is not cyclic.
From now on, we assume that
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are non-elementary.
Let us now focus on
(C2)(T2)B

\Rightarrow

(C2)(T2)A
.
Denote by

\Gamma_{i}=A_{i,1}\ast\dots\ast A_{i,m_{i}}\ast F_{i}

the Grushko decomposition of
\Gamma_{i}
where each
A_{i,j}
is non-cyclic, freely indecomposable and
F_{i}
is a free group.
We apply
Theorem 9.3
twice, first with
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, then with
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{1}
.
Hence there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd integer
n\geqslant N
, every monomorphism
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
( respectively
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
) comes from a morphism
\Gamma_{1}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}
( respectively
\Gamma_{2}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{1}
) whose restriction to non-cyclic, freely indecomposable free factors is one-to-one.
Up to increasing the value of
N
we can assume that
A_{i,j}/A_{i,j}^{n}
is always non-trivial, for every
n\geqslant N
.

Assume now that there exists an automorphism
\theta\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\to\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
for some
n\geqslant N
.
We denote by
\pi_{1}\colon\Gamma_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
the canonical projection.
According to our choice of
N
, there are two morphisms
\varphi_{1}\colon\Gamma_{1}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{2}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon\Gamma_{2}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{1}
as above respectively lifting
\theta
and its inverse.
Since
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are one-to-one when restricted to freely indecomposable free factors there are two maps
\sigma_{1}\colon\llbracket 1,m_{1}\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,m_{2}\rrbracket
and
\sigma_{2}\colon\llbracket 1,m_{2}\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,m_{1}\rrbracket
such that

\varphi_{1}(A_{1,j})\subset A_{2,\sigma_{1}(j)}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{2}(A_{2,j})\subset A_{1,\sigma_{2}(j)},\quad\forall j.

We claim that
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are bijections inverse of one another.
For simplicity we let
\varphi=\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}
so that

\varphi(A_{1,j})\subset A_{1,\sigma(j)},\quad\forall j\in\llbracket 1,m_{1}\rrbracket.

Let
j\in\llbracket 1,m_{1}\rrbracket
.
By construction
\varphi
is a lift of the identity on
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
.
Hence

\pi_{1}\left(A_{1,j}\right)\subset\pi_{1}\left(A_{1,\sigma(j)}\right).

It follows from
Lemma 9.6
that either
\sigma(j)=j
or
A_{1,j}/A_{1,j}^{n}
is trivial.
We made sure when choosing
N
that
A_{1,j}/A_{1,j}^{n}
was not trivial.
Consequently
\sigma=\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}
is the identity map.
The same argument with
\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{2}
shows that
\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}
is also the identity, which completes the proof of our claim.
In particular,
m_{1}=m_{2}
which we now denote by
m
.
Moreover, up to reordering the free factors of
\Gamma_{2}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\sigma_{1}
(hence
\sigma_{2}
) is the identity.

Let
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
According to the above discussion,
\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
induces an embedding from
A_{1,j}
into itself.
However
A_{1,j}
is a freely indecomposable hyperbolic group, hence co-Hopfian.
It follows that
\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
induces an automorphism of
A_{1,j}
.
Similarly we prove that
\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{2}
induces an automorphism of
A_{2,j}
.
Hence
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
respectively induce isomorphisms

\varphi_{1}\colon A_{1,j}\to A_{2,j},\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{2}\colon A_{2,j}\to A_{1,j},

for every
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
Let now us write

A_{i}=A_{i,1}\ast\dots\ast A_{i,j},\quad\forall i\in\{1,2\}.

According to the above discussion
\varphi_{1}
( respectively
\varphi_{2}
) induces an isomorphism from
A_{1}
onto
A_{2}
( respectively
A_{2}
onto
A_{1}
).
Consequently, if we consider the quotients of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
by
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively, we observe that the automorphism
\theta\colon\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}\to\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
induces an automorphism from
F_{1}/F_{1}^{n}
onto
F_{2}/F_{2}^{n}
.
However
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are free abelian groups.
Looking at the abelianizations of
F_{1}/F_{1}^{n}
and
F_{2}/F_{2}^{n}
we observe that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
have the same rank, hence are isomorphic.
It follows that
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are isomorphic as well.
∎

Remark
.

Note that in the last step of the proof we do not claim that there is an isomorphism
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
lifting
\theta
.
Indeed
Example 1.11
can be adapted to show that such a statement is false in general.

9.2
Root towers

As we observed in the introduction, all the above statements fail if one does not ask that
\Gamma
has no essential root-splitting.
The notion of root tower defined below is designed to get rid of the root splittings.

Definition 9.8

(Root tower)
.

A
root tower
is a finite sequence of groups
G_{0},G_{1},\lx@nobreakspace\dots,G_{m}
where each
G_{i+1}
is obtained from
G_{i}
by adjoining roots.
The
orders
of this tower is the set
P=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\}
where each
p_{i}
is the order of the root splitting defining
G_{i}
.
We respectively call
G_{0}
and
G_{m}
the
floor
and the
roof
of the tower.

The next statement is a consequence of Louder and Touikan
[
35
]
.

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Then
\Gamma
is the roof of a root tower whose floor does not admit any essential root splitting.

Given a subset
P\subset\mathbf{N}
, we say that an integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
is
co-prime with
P
if it is co-prime with every element in
P
.

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary torsion-free hyperbolic group.
Assume that
\Gamma
is the roof of a root tower
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{m}=\Gamma
.
Let
P\subset\mathbf{N}
be the orders of this tower.
Then the following holds

A.

\Gamma_{0}
is a non-elementary, quasi-convex subgroup of
\Gamma
.

B.

For every exponent
n\in\mathbf{N}
, which is co-prime with
P
the inclusion
\Gamma_{0}\hookrightarrow\Gamma
induces an isomorphism
\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

Proof.

Observe that for each
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, the group
\Gamma_{i-1}
is a quasi-convex subgroup of
\Gamma_{i}

[
32
, Lemma 3.5]
.
This is essentially due to the fact that the edge groups of the root splitting of
\Gamma_{i}
are cyclic, hence quasi-convex in
\Gamma_{i}
.
A proof by induction shows that
\Gamma_{0}
is quasi-convex in
\Gamma_{i}
for every
i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, whence
(C2)(T2)A
.
Recall that
\Gamma
is CSA.
Hence
\Gamma_{0}
cannot be cyclic, for otherwise
\Gamma
would be cyclic as well.
Point
(C2)(T2)B
is a simple induction using
Corollary 5.4
.
∎

We can now study more generally all the
n
-periodic quotient of a torsion-free hyperbolic group which are freely indecomposable in the Burnside variety
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
We first give a characterization of those groups using root towers.

Theorem 9.11
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic group.
Assume that
\Gamma
is the roof of a root tower whose floor
\Gamma_{0}
has no essential root-splitting.
Let
P
be the orders of this tower.
Then the following are equivalent.

A.

The group
\Gamma_{0}
is freely indecomposable.

B.

There exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every odd exponent
n\geqslant N
which is co-prime with
P
, the quotient
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
does not split as a free product in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 9.10
, the
n
-periodic quotients of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{0}
are isomorphic, for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
that is co-prime with
P
.
The result follows from
Theorem 9.2
.
∎

We now generalize the results of the previous section when
\Gamma
is the roof of a root tower whose floor is freely indecomposable with no essential root splitting, i.e. when the
n
-periodic quotients
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
are freely indecomposable in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Theorem 9.12
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic.
Assume that
\Gamma
is the roof of a root tower whose floor
\Gamma_{0}
is freely indecomposable, with no essential root splitting.
Let
P
be the orders of this tower.
Then there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
, such that for every
n\geqslant N
, which is co-prime with
P
, the following holds.

A.

The inclusion
\Gamma_{0}\hookrightarrow\Gamma
induces an isomorphism
\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n}\to\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

B.

The group
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
is co-Hopfian

C.

The canonical map from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{0}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0})}
to
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{0}^{n})}=\mathchoice{\operatorname{Aut}\left(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}\right)}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma/\Gamma^{n})}
is onto.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma 9.10
, that for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
that is co-prime with
P
, the
n
-periodic quotients of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{0}
are isomorphic, whence
(C2)(T2)A
.
Points
(C2)(T2)B
and
(C2)(T2)C
respectively follows from
Corollary 9.5
.
∎

Theorem 9.13
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two non-elementary, torsion-free, hyperbolic groups.
Assume that
\Gamma_{i}
is the roof of a root tower whose floor
\Gamma_{i,0}
has no essential root splitting.
Let
P_{i}
be the orders of this tower.
Then there exists
N\in\mathbf{N}
such that the following are equivalent

A.

\Gamma_{1,0}
and
\Gamma_{2,0}
are isomorphic.

B.

\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic for some integer
n\geqslant N
that is co-prime with
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
.

C.

\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}/\Gamma_{2}^{n}
are isomorphic for every integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, that is co-prime with
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma 9.10
, that for every
n\geqslant N
that is co-prime with
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
, the
n
-periodic quotients of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1,0}
( respectively
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{2,0}
) are isomorphic.
Hence the results follows from
Corollary 9.7
.
∎

Appendix A
Approximation of periodic groups

This appendix is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem 3.11
.
We first recall the construction of Delzant and Gromov
[
19
]
to investigate small cancellation groups and then prove the existence of a class of approximations of a periodic group as in
Section 3
.
We follow Coulon
[
12
,
13
,
15
]
.

A.1
Small cancellation theory

A.1.1
Cone and cone-off

Cone over a metric space.

Let
Y
be a metric space.
The
cone of radius
\rho
over
Y
, denoted by
Z_{\rho}(Y)
or simply
Z(Y)
, is the quotient of
Y\times\left[0,\rho\right]
by the equivalence relation that identifies all the points of the form
(y,0)
. The equivalence class of
(y,0)
, denoted by
c
, is called the
apex
or
cone point
of
Z(Y)
.
By abuse of notation we still write
(y,r)
for the equivalence class of
(y,r)
.
The cone over
Y
is endowed with a metric characterized as follows
[
6
, Chapter I.5, Proposition 5.9]
.
If
x=(y,r)
and
x^{\prime}=(y^{\prime},r^{\prime})
are two points of
Z(Y)
, then

\operatorname{ch}\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-x^{\prime}|}=\operatorname{ch}r\operatorname{ch}r^{\prime}-\operatorname{sh}r\operatorname{sh}r^{\prime}\cos\left(\min\left\{\pi,\frac{\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}\right\}\right).

(29)

This metric is modeled on the one of the hyperbolic place
\mathbf{H}^{2}
, see
[
12
]
for the geometric interpretation.
If
Y
is a length space, then so is
Z(Y)
.
The embedding
\iota\colon Y\to Z(Y)
sending
y
to
(y,\rho)
satisfies

\mathchoice{\left|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})\right|}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|}=\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}{|y-y^{\prime}|}\right),\quad\forall y,y^{\prime}\in Y,

where
\mu\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
is the non-decreasing concave map characterized by

\operatorname{ch}\mu(t)=\operatorname{ch}^{2}\rho-\operatorname{sh}^{2}\rho\cos\left(\min\left\{\pi,\frac{t}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}\right\}\right),\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}.

In addition, the cone comes with a
radial projection

p\colon Z(Y)\setminus\{c\}\to Y
mapping
(y,r)
to
y
.
The next proposition is an exercise using the geometric interpretation of the distance in a cone.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume that
f\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
is a
(1,\ell)
-quasi-isometric embedding.
Then
f
extends to a
(1,\ell)
-quasi-isometric embedding
Z(Y_{1})\to Z(Y_{2})
sending
(y,r)
to
(f(y),r)
.

In particular, if
H
is a group acting by isometries on
Y
, then this action extends to an action by isometries on
Z(Y)
fixing its apex.
Moreover, if the original action of
H
on
Y
is proper, then the quotient
Z(Y)/H
is a metric space isometric to
Z(Y/H)
.

Cone-off space.

Let
\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Let
X
be a
\delta
-hyperbolic length space and
\mathcal{Y}
a collection of strongly quasi-convex subsets of
X
.
Given
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we denote by
|\ .\ |_{Y}
the length metric on
Y
induced by the restriction of
|\ .\ |
on
Y
.
We write
Z_{\rho}(Y)
for the cone of radius
\rho
over
(Y,|\ .\ |_{Y})
.

The
cone-off of radius
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{Y}
, denoted by
\dot{X}_{\rho}(\mathcal{Y})
or simply
\dot{X}
is the space obtained by attaching for every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, the cone
Z_{\rho}(Y)
on
X
along
Y
according to
\iota\lx@nobreakspace\colon Y\to Z_{\rho}(Y)
.
We write
|\ .\ |_{\dot{X}}
for the largest pseudo-metric on
\dot{X}
such that the canonical maps
X\to\dot{X}
as well as
Z(Y)\to\dot{X}
are
1
-Lipschitz.
It turns out that
|\ .\ |_{\dot{X}}
is a metric on
\dot{X}

[
12
, Proposition 5.10]
.
The metrics of
X
and
\dot{X}
are related as follows
[
12
, Lemma 5.8]
.
For every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
we have

\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}\right)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}{|x-x^{\prime}|_{X}}

(30)

By construction, the embedding
Z(Y)\to\dot{X}
is
1
-Lipschitz for every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Since we passed to the length metric on
Y
before building the cones, this map is not an isometry in general.
Nevertheless we have the following statement.

Proposition A.2
.

For every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, the map
Z(Y)\to\dot{X}
is
(1,8\delta)
-quasi-isometric embedding.

Proof.

The proof involves a variation on the cone-off construction.
For every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we write
Z^{\prime}(Y)
for the cone of radius
\rho
over the space
(Y,|\ .\ |_{X})
.
Note that this time, the metric on
Y
is the one from
X
and not the induced length metric.
In particular,
Z^{\prime}(Y)
may fail to be a length space, but this is not important here.
As a set of points
Z(Y)
and
Z^{\prime}(Y)
are the same though.
We denote by
\dot{X}^{\prime}
the space obtained by attaching for every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, the cone
Z^{\prime}(Y)
on
X
along
Y
according to
\iota\lx@nobreakspace\colon Y\to Z^{\prime}(Y)
.
We endow
\dot{X}^{\prime}
with the largest (pseudo-)metric such that the canonical maps
X\to\dot{X}^{\prime}
as well as
Z^{\prime}(Y)\to\dot{X}^{\prime}
are
1
-Lipschitz, see
[
11
, Section 3]
.
For every
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, the identity map
(Y,|\ .\ |_{Y})\to(Y,|\ .\ |_{X})
is
1
-Lipschitz.
It follows that the natural map
\dot{X}\to\dot{X}^{\prime}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
Let
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
For every
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
, we have

\mathchoice{\left|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})\right|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|\iota(y)-\iota(y^{\prime})|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}\leqslant\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{X}}\right)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|_{\dot{X}^{\prime}}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}^{\prime}}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}^{\prime}}}{|y-y^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}^{\prime}}},

see for instance
[
11
, Lemma 3.1.16]
.
It follows that
Z^{\prime}(Y)
is
isometrically
embedded in
\dot{X}^{\prime}
.
Combining these facts, we get that for every
z,z^{\prime}\in Z(Y)

\mathchoice{\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}(Y)}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}{|z-z^{\prime}|_{\dot{X}}}.

By (
4
) the map
(Y,|\ .\ |_{Y})\to(Y,|\ .\ |_{X})
is a
(1,8\delta)
-quasi-isometric embedding.
The conclusion now follows from the fact that the map
Z(Y)\to Z^{\prime}(Y)
is a
(1,8\delta)
-quasi-isometric embedding (
Lemma A.1
).
∎

A.1.2
The small cancellation theorem

General settings.

In this section
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic length space endowed with a non-elementary action by isometries of a group
\Gamma
.
In addition we fix a collection
\mathcal{Q}
of pairs
(H,Y)
where
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
and
Y
an
H
-invariant strongly-quasi-convex subset of
X
.
We assume that
\mathcal{Q}
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma
defined by
\gamma\cdot(H,Y)=(\gamma H\gamma^{-1},\gamma Y)
, for every
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
and every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
We denote by
K
the (normal) subgroup of
\Gamma
generated by all
H
where
(H,Y)
runs over
\mathcal{Q}
.
The goal is to study the quotient
\bar{\Gamma}=\Gamma/K
and the corresponding projection
\pi\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
.
More precisely we are going to build a hyperbolic space
\bar{X}
endowed with a non-elementary action of
\bar{\Gamma}
, and relate the geometry of
\bar{X}
to the one of
X
.
To that end, we define the following small cancellation parameters

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathcal{Q},X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\mathchoice{\left\{\operatorname{diam}\left(Y_{1}^{+3\delta}\cap Y_{2}^{+3\delta}\right)\;:\ (H_{1},Y_{1})\neq(H_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}\right\}}{\{\operatorname{diam}\left(Y_{1}^{+3\delta}\cap Y_{2}^{+3\delta}\right)\;:\ (H_{1},Y_{1})\neq(H_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{\operatorname{diam}\left(Y_{1}^{+3\delta}\cap Y_{2}^{+3\delta}\right)\;:\ (H_{1},Y_{1})\neq(H_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{\operatorname{diam}\left(Y_{1}^{+3\delta}\cap Y_{2}^{+3\delta}\right)\;:\ (H_{1},Y_{1})\neq(H_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}\}}

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{Q},X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\mathchoice{\left\{\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}\;:\ h\in H,\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\right\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}\;:\ h\in H,\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}\;:\ h\in H,\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}{\|h\|}\;:\ h\in H,\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}.

They respectively play the role of the length of the largest piece and the smallest relation.

Metric spaces.

We fix a paramater
\rho\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Its value will be made precise later (see
Theorem A.3
).
We make an abuse of notation and write
\dot{X}_{\rho}(\mathcal{Q})
or simply
\dot{X}
for the cone-off of radius
\rho
over
X
relative to the collection

\mathcal{Y}=\mathchoice{\left\{Y\;:\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\right\}}{\{Y\;:\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{Y\;:\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}{\{Y\;:\ (H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}\}}.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\dot{X}
be the set of apices of all the cones.
Recall that
\mathcal{Q}
is
\Gamma
-invariant.
Hence the action of
\Gamma
on
X
naturally extends to an action by isometries on
\dot{X}
: for every
g\in\Gamma
,
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
, and
x=(y,r)
in
Z(Y)
, we define
gx
to be the point of
Z(gY)
given by
gx=(gy,r)
.
The
radial projection

p\colon\dot{X}\setminus\mathcal{C}\to X
is the map whose restriction to
X
is the identity and whose restriction to any punctured cone
Z(Y)\setminus\{c\}
is the radial projection defined above.

The
quotient space
, denoted by
\bar{X}_{\rho}(\mathcal{Q})
or simply
\bar{X}
is the quotient of
\dot{X}_{\rho}(\mathcal{Q})
by the normal subgroup
K
.
The metric of
\dot{X}
induces a pseudo-metric on
\bar{X}
.
The group
\bar{\Gamma}
naturally acts by isometries on
\bar{X}
so that the canonical projection
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
is a
1
-Lipschitz,
\Gamma
-equivariant map.
If
x
is a point in
X
we write
\bar{x}=f(x)
for its image in
\bar{X}
.
Similarly, we denote by
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
the image of
\mathcal{C}
in
\bar{X}
.
It is a
\bar{\Gamma}
invariant subset of
\bar{X}
.
Moreover for every distinct
\bar{c},\bar{c}^{\prime}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{c}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{c}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{c}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{c}^{\prime}|}\geqslant 2\rho
.
We let

\bar{X}^{+}=\bar{X}\setminus\bigcup_{\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}}B(\bar{c},\rho),

which is also the image of
X
under the map
f
.
As explained in
Section 3
, we think of
\bar{X}^{+}
as the thick part of thin-thick decomposition, where the thin parts correspond to the balls
B(\bar{c},\rho)
centered at a cone point
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
The radial projection
p\colon\dot{X}\setminus\mathcal{C}\to X
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
Hence it induces a
\bar{\Gamma}
-equivariant map,
\bar{p}\colon\bar{X}\setminus\bar{\mathcal{C}}\twoheadrightarrow\bar{X}^{+}
, that we still call the
radial projection
.

The next statement is a combination of Proposition 6.4, Proposition 6.7, Corollary 3.12 and Proposition 3.15 in
[
12
]

Theorem A.3
.

There exist
\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\Delta_{0},\rho_{0}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
, which do not depend on
X
,
\Gamma
or
\mathcal{Q}
, with the following property.
Assume that
\rho\geqslant\rho_{0}
.
If
\delta\leqslant\delta_{0}
,
\Delta(\mathcal{Q},X)\leqslant\Delta_{0}
and
T(\mathcal{Q},X)\geqslant 10\pi\operatorname{sh}\rho
, then the following holds

A.

The cone-off space
\dot{X}
is
\dot{\delta}
-hyperbolic with
\dot{\delta}\leqslant\delta_{1}
.

B.

The quotient space
\bar{X}
is
\bar{\delta}
-hyperbolic with
\bar{\delta}\leqslant\delta_{1}
.

C.

Let
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
\bar{c}
be the image in
\bar{X}
of the apex
c
of
Z(Y)
.
The projection
\pi\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}/H
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
.

D.

For every
r\in(0,\rho/20]
, and
x\in\dot{X}
, if
d(x,\mathcal{C})\geqslant 2r
, then the map
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
B(x,r)
onto
B(\bar{x},r)
.

E.

For every
x\in\dot{X}
, and
g\in K\setminus\{1\}
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|gx-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|gx-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|gx-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|gx-x|_{\dot{X}}}\geqslant\min\{2r,\rho/5\}
, where
r=d(x,\mathcal{C})
.
In particular,
K
acts freely on
\dot{X}\setminus\mathcal{C}
and the map
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces a covering map
\dot{X}\setminus\mathcal{C}\to\bar{X}\setminus\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark
.

Note that the constants
\delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
( respectively
\rho_{0}
) can be chosen arbitrarily small ( respectively large).
From now on, we will always assume that
\rho_{0}>10^{20}\delta_{1}
whereas
\delta_{0},\Delta_{0}<10^{-10}\delta_{1}
.
Similarly we suppose that
\operatorname{th}(\rho_{0})\geqslant 1/2
.
These estimates are absolutely not optimal.
We chose them very generously to ensure that all the inequalities which we need later are satisfied.
What really matters is their orders of magnitude recalled below

\max\left\{\delta_{0},\Delta_{0}\right\}\ll\delta_{1}\ll\rho_{0}\ll\pi\operatorname{sh}\rho_{0}.

From now on and until the end of
Section A.1
we assume that
X
,
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{Q}
are as in
Theorem A.3
.
In particular,
\dot{X}
and
\bar{X}
are respectively
\dot{\delta}
- and
\bar{\delta}
-hyperbolic.
Up to increasing one constant or the other, we can actually assume that
\dot{\delta}=\bar{\delta}
.
Nevertheless we still keep two distinct notations, to remember which space we are working in.

A.1.3
Lifting properties

Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)C
and
(C2)(T2)D
state that any “small scale picture” in
\bar{X}
can be lifted in
X
.
The next statement is an illustration of this phenomenon.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}
such that
d(\bar{x},\bar{\mathcal{C}})\geqslant\rho/2
.
Let
\bar{U}
be a subset of
\bar{\Gamma}
.
If
\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}\leqslant\rho/100
, for every
\bar{\gamma}\in\bar{U}
, then there exists a subset
U
of
\Gamma
with the following properties:

A.

the epimorphism
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\bar{\Gamma}
induces a bijection from
U
onto
\bar{U}
;

B.

for every
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in U
, if
\pi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})
belongs to
\bar{U}
, then
\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\in U
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\dot{X}
be a pre-image of
\bar{x}
.
According to
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)D
, for every
\bar{\gamma}\in\bar{U}
, there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
, such that
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}=\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}
.
We denote by
U
the set of pre-images of all elements of
\bar{U}
obtained in this way. By
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)E
for every
\gamma\in K\setminus\{1\}
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}\geqslant\rho/5
.
It follows that the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces a bijection from
U
onto
\bar{U}
.
Let us focus now on the second part of the statement.
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in U
.
Assume that
\pi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})
belongs to
\bar{U}
.
By construction,
\pi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})
has a unique pre-image in
U
, say
\gamma_{0}
.
Hence
\gamma_{0}^{-1}\gamma\gamma^{\prime}
is an element of
K
which moves
x
by at most
3\rho/100
.
By
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)E
,
\gamma_{0}^{-1}\gamma\gamma^{\prime}
is trivial, hence
\gamma\gamma^{\prime}
belongs to
U
.
∎

Proposition A.5
.

Let
F
be the free group generated by a finite set
U
.
Let
\ell\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
and
V\subset F
be the closed ball of radius
\ell
(for the word metric relative to
U
).
Let
\varphi\colon F\to\bar{\Gamma}
be a morphism whose image does not fix a cone point in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Assume that

\lambda_{\infty}\left(\varphi,U\right)<\frac{\rho}{100\ell},

(the energy is measured with the metric of
\bar{X}
).
Then there exists a map
\tilde{\varphi}\colon F\to\Gamma
such that
\varphi=\pi\circ\tilde{\varphi}
and
V\cap\ker\varphi=V\cap\ker\tilde{\varphi}
.

Proof.

By definition of the energy, there exist
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}
such that for every
g\in U
,

\mathchoice{\left|\varphi(g)\bar{x}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\varphi(g)\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\varphi(g)\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\varphi(g)\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}<\frac{\rho}{100\ell}.

(31)

Note that

d(\bar{x},\bar{\mathcal{C}})\geqslant\left(1-\frac{1}{200\ell}\right)\rho\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\rho.

Indeed if it was not the case, then by the triangle inequality, the image of
\varphi
would fix a cone point in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Set
\bar{W}=\varphi(V)
.
It follows from (
31
) that for every
\bar{\gamma}
in
\bar{W}
, we have

\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{x}-\bar{x}|}<\frac{\rho}{100}.

By
Lemma A.4
, there exists a finite subset
W\subset\Gamma
such that

A.

the epimorphism
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\bar{\Gamma}
induces a bijection from
W
onto
\bar{W}
;

B.

for every
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in W
, if
\pi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})
belongs to
\bar{W}
, then
\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\in W
.

We define a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon F\to\Gamma
by sending
g\in U
to the unique pre-image of
\varphi(g)
in
W
.
In particular,
\pi\circ\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi
.
If follows from
(C2)(T2)B
that
\tilde{\varphi}(g)\in W
, for every
g\in V
.
Combined with
(C2)(T2)A
wet get
V\cap\ker\varphi=V\cap\ker\tilde{\varphi}
.
∎

Theorem A.3
can be used to lift small scale pictures.
More generally any quasi-convex of
\bar{X}
avoiding the cone points lifts in
\dot{X}
.
This is the purpose of the next statement which is a combination of Lemmas 4.17 and 4.18 of
[
15
]
.
See also
[
12
, Proposition 3.21]

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\bar{Z}
be a subset of
\bar{X}
such that for every
\bar{z},\bar{z}^{\prime}\in\bar{Z}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
, we have
\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}^{\prime}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}^{\prime}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}^{\prime}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}>13\bar{\delta}
.
Let
\bar{z}_{0}
be a point of
\bar{Z}
and
z_{0}
a pre-image of
\bar{z}_{0}
in
\dot{X}
.
Then there is a unique subset
Z
of
\dot{X}
containing
z_{0}
such that the map
\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
.
Moreover the following holds.

A.

For every
z_{1},z_{2}\in Z
, for every
\bar{\gamma}\in\bar{\Gamma}
, if
\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}_{1}=\bar{z}_{2}
, then there exists a unique pre-image
\gamma\in\Gamma
of
\bar{\gamma}
such that
\gamma z_{1}=z_{2}
.
Moreover for every
z,z^{\prime}\in Z
, if
\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}=\bar{z}^{\prime}
then
\gamma z=z^{\prime}
.

B.

The projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{Z}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}
.

Lemma A.6
holds in particular if
\bar{Z}
is an
\alpha
-quasi-convex subset of
\bar{X}
such that
d(\bar{c},\bar{Z})>\alpha+13\bar{\delta}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
To handle configurations lying in the neighborhood of the cone point, we use the following statement.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
c
be the apex of
Z(Y)
and
\bar{c}
its image in
\bar{X}
.
The map
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
B(c,\rho-2\dot{\delta})/H
onto
B(\bar{c},\rho-2\bar{\delta})
.

Remark A.8
.

Recall that we chose the hyperbolicity constants so that
\dot{\delta}=\bar{\delta}
.
For the experts, one can prove using the Greendlinger lemma that
B(c,\rho)/H
and
B(\bar{c},\rho)
are isometric.
However the proof below is much more elementary and illustrates the benefits of the geometry of
\bar{X}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is
2\rho
-separated, it follows from the triangle inequality that

A.

\mathchoice{\left|c-x\right|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}{|c-x|}=\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}
, for every
x\in B(c,\rho)
;

B.

f
maps
B(c,\rho)
onto
B(\bar{c},\rho)
;

C.

if
x,y\in B(c,\rho)
have the same image in
\bar{X}
then there exists
h\in H
such that
y=hx
.

In particular,
f
maps
B(c,\rho-2\dot{\delta})
onto
B(\bar{c},\rho-2\bar{\delta})
.
Let
x,y\in B(c,\rho-2\dot{\delta})
and
\bar{x},\bar{y}
their respective images in
\bar{X}
.
Assume first that
\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\right\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}=0
, i.e.

\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{y}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}=\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{c}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{c}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{c}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{c}|}+\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{y}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{y}|}=\mathchoice{\left|x-c\right|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}+\mathchoice{\left|c-y\right|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}{|c-y|}.

Since
f
is
1
-Lipschitz, it follows that
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}=\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{y}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}
.
Assume now that
\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\right\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}{\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{\bar{c}}}>0
.
For every sufficiently small
\eta>0
, there exists a
(1,\eta)
-quasi-geodesic
\bar{\sigma}\colon\mathchoice{\left[a\,,b\right]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}\to\bar{X}
joining
\bar{x}
to
\bar{y}
such that
\bar{c}
does not lie on
\bar{\sigma}
.
Since balls in
\bar{X}
are
2\bar{\delta}
-quasi-convex, we may assume also that
\bar{\sigma}
is contained in
B(\bar{c},\rho)
.
Recall that
\dot{X}\setminus\mathcal{C}\to\bar{X}\setminus\bar{\mathcal{C}}
is a covering map and a local isometry
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)E
.
Let
\sigma\colon\mathchoice{\left[a\,,b\right]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}\to\dot{X}
be the path starting at
x
and lifting
\bar{\sigma}
.
By construction
\sigma
lies in
B(c,\rho)
.
Hence its endpoint, which is also a pre-image of
\bar{y}
can be written
hy
for some
h\in H
.
In addition
\sigma
and
\bar{\sigma}
have the same length, thus

\mathchoice{\left|x-hy\right|}{|x-hy|}{|x-hy|}{|x-hy|}\leqslant L(\sigma)\leqslant L(\bar{\sigma})\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{y}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}+\eta.

In both cases we proved that for every
\eta>0
, there exist
h\in H
such that
\mathchoice{\left|x-hy\right|}{|x-hy|}{|x-hy|}{|x-hy|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{y}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|}+\eta
, hence
f
induces an isometry from
B(c,\rho-2\dot{\delta})/H
onto
B(\bar{c},\rho-2\bar{\delta})
.
∎

A.1.4
Isometries of
\bar{X}

In this section we study elementary subgroups of
\bar{\Gamma}
.
The goal is to get a control in the spirit of Axioms
(H3)
-
(H6)
provided the same holds for
\Gamma
.
To that end we make the following additional assumptions (we refer the reader to
Definition 2.2
for the definition of thin isometries).

Assumption A.9
.

—

Both
\nu(\Gamma,X)
and
A(\Gamma,X)
are finite.

—

\Gamma
has no even torsion.

—

Every elementary subgroup of
\Gamma
which is not elliptic is loxodromic and cyclic.
We say that a loxodromic element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is
primitive
if it generates the maximal loxodromic subgroup containing it.

—

There exists
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with the following property: for every
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
, there is an
\alpha
-thin primitive element
\gamma\in\Gamma
generating
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
and an odd integer
n\geqslant 100
such that
H=\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}
.

According to
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)C
it implies that
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
is cyclic for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
It also follows from these assumption that the action of
\Gamma
on
X
is weakly properly discontinuous (WPD), see
[
4
]
for the definition.
In particular, all the results of
[
13
, Section 5]
apply here.

Elliptic subgroups of
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Lemma A.10

(
[
13
, Proposition 5.18]
)
.

Let
\bar{E}
be an elliptic subgroup of
\bar{\Gamma}
(for its action on
\bar{X}
).
One of the following holds.

—

There exists an elliptic subgroup
E
of
\Gamma
(for its action on
X
) such that the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
E
onto
\bar{E}
.

—

There exists
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
such that
