\bar{E}\subset\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
.

Lemma A.11
.

Assume that there exists
\beta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that every non-trivial elliptic element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is
\beta
-thin (for its action on
X
).
Let
\bar{\gamma}\in\bar{\Gamma}
be an elliptic element.
If
\bar{\gamma}
does not fix a cone point in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
, then
\bar{\gamma}
is
\bar{\beta}
-thin, where
\bar{\beta}=\beta+\pi\operatorname{sh}(10\bar{\delta})
.

Proof.

For simplicity we let
\bar{Z}=\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma},6\bar{\delta})
.
Since
\bar{\gamma}
does not fix any apex in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
, if follows from the triangle inequality that
d(\bar{c},\bar{Z})\geqslant\rho-3\bar{\delta}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
By
Lemma A.6
, there exists a subset
Z
of
\dot{X}
such that the map
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
;
moreover the projection
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{Z}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}
.
We denote by
\gamma\in\Gamma
the pre-image of
\bar{\gamma}
in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
.
Let
\bar{E}=\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{\gamma}\right\rangle}{\langle\bar{\gamma}\rangle}{\langle\bar{\gamma}\rangle}{\langle\bar{\gamma}\rangle}
.
Fix a point
\bar{z}_{0}\in\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{E},6\bar{\delta})
.
Note that
\bar{E}\bar{z}_{0}\subset\bar{Z}
.
Let
z_{0}
be its pre-image in
Z
and
y_{0}
the radial projection of
z_{0}
onto
X
so that
\mathchoice{\left|z_{0}-y_{0}\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|z_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|z_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|z_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 3\dot{\delta}
.
It follows from (
30
) and the definition of
\bar{z}_{0}
, that for every
k\in\mathbf{Z}
, we have

\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}\right|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}\right)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma^{k}z_{0}-z_{0}\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}z_{0}-z_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}z_{0}-z_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma^{k}z_{0}-z_{0}|_{\dot{X}}}+6\dot{\delta}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}^{k}\bar{z}_{0}-\bar{z}_{0}\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}^{k}\bar{z}_{0}-\bar{z}_{0}|}{|\bar{\gamma}^{k}\bar{z}_{0}-\bar{z}_{0}|}{|\bar{\gamma}^{k}\bar{z}_{0}-\bar{z}_{0}|}+6\bar{\delta}\leqslant 12\bar{\delta}.

Thus

\mathchoice{\left|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}\right|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}{|\gamma^{k}y_{0}-y_{0}|_{X}}\leqslant\pi\operatorname{sh}(6\bar{\delta}),

see for instance
[
12
, Proposition 4.4]
.
In particular, the
\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}{\langle\gamma\rangle}
-orbit of
y_{0}
is bounded, hence
\gamma
is elliptic (for its action on
X
).
According to our assumption
\gamma
is
\beta
-thin at some point
x\in X
.

Let
\bar{x}
be the image of
x
in
\bar{X}
.
We now claim that
\bar{Z}
is contained in
{B(\bar{x},\bar{\beta}-2\bar{\delta})}
.
Let
\bar{z}\in Z
.
Let
z
be its pre-image in
Z
and
y\in X
the radial projection of
z
.
The same computation as above shows that the point
y\in X
is moved by
\gamma
by at most
\pi\operatorname{sh}(6\bar{\delta})
.
By the very definition of thinness we get
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}\leqslant\beta+\pi\operatorname{sh}(6\bar{\delta})
.
Recall that the map
f\colon X\to\bar{X}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{z}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{z}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{z}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{z}|}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}{|x-y|_{X}}+\mathchoice{\left|y-z\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant\beta+\pi\operatorname{sh}(6\bar{\delta})+6\bar{\delta}\leqslant\bar{\beta}-2\bar{\delta}.

This inequality holds for every
\bar{z}\in\bar{Z}
, which completes the proof of our claim.
The result now follows from
Lemma 2.4
.
∎

Let
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
c
be the apex of the cone
Z(Y)
and
\bar{c}
its image in
\bar{X}
.
We now focus on the behavior of
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
.
According to our assumptions,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
is generated by an
\alpha
-thin primitive element
h\in\Gamma
and
H=\mathchoice{\left\langle h^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle h^{n}\rangle}{\langle h^{n}\rangle}{\langle h^{n}\rangle}
for some odd integer
n\geqslant 100
.
It follows from
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)C
that
\pi\colon\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}/H\equiv\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
.
We think of
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
acting on
B(\bar{c},\rho)
as a rotation group acting on a hyperbolic disc.
Let us make this idea more rigorous.

Angle cocycle.

Let
(z_{n})
be a sequence points in
X
converging to
h^{+}
(the unique attractive point of
h
in
\partial X
).
Fix an non-principal ultra-filter
\omega
.
We define a “Busemann cocycle”
b_{0}\colon X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
at
h^{+}
by

b_{0}(x,x^{\prime})=\lim_{\omega}\left(\mathchoice{\left|x-z_{n}\right|}{|x-z_{n}|}{|x-z_{n}|}{|x-z_{n}|}-\mathchoice{\left|x^{\prime}-z_{n}\right|}{|x^{\prime}-z_{n}|}{|x^{\prime}-z_{n}|}{|x^{\prime}-z_{n}|}\right),\quad\forall x,x^{\prime}\in X

Although
h^{+}
is fixed by
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
, the cocycle
b_{0}
may not be
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
-invariant.
Nevertheless
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
is amenable.
Proceeding as in
[
15
, Section 4.3]
we can average the orbit of
b_{0}
under
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
to get a new cocycle
b\colon X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
, with the following properties

A.

\mathchoice{\left|b(x,x^{\prime})-b_{0}(x,x^{\prime})\right|}{|b(x,x^{\prime})-b_{0}(x,x^{\prime})|}{|b(x,x^{\prime})-b_{0}(x,x^{\prime})|}{|b(x,x^{\prime})-b_{0}(x,x^{\prime})|}\leqslant 6\delta
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,

B.

b(\gamma x,\gamma x^{\prime})=b(x,x^{\prime})
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
\gamma\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
,

C.

\mathchoice{\left|b(x,\gamma x)\right|}{|b(x,\gamma x)|}{|b(x,\gamma x)|}{|b(x,\gamma x)|}=\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}{\|\gamma\|^{\infty}}
, for every
x\in X
and
\gamma\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
.

We rescale
b
to get a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
-invariant cocycle
\theta_{c}\colon Y\times Y\to\mathbf{R}
given by

\theta_{c}(y,y^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}b(y,y^{\prime}),\quad\forall y,y^{\prime}\in Y,

which we extend radially to a cocycle

\theta_{c}\colon Z(Y)\setminus\{c\}\times Z(Y)\setminus\{c\}\to\mathbf{R}

as follows:
if
x=(y,r)
and
x^{\prime}=(y^{\prime},r^{\prime})
are two points of
Z(Y)\setminus\{c\}
, then
\theta_{c}(x,x^{\prime})=\theta_{c}(y,y^{\prime})
.
Let

\Theta_{\bar{c}}=\frac{n\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}

It follows from the small cancellation assumption that
\Theta_{\bar{c}}\geqslant 9\pi
.
By construction
\theta_{c}
induces a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
-invariant cocycle

\theta_{\bar{c}}\colon\mathring{B}(\bar{c},\rho)\times\mathring{B}(\bar{c},\rho)\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta_{\bar{c}}\mathbf{Z}.

Recall that
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
is assumed to be cyclic.
Hence the map
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}\to\mathbf{R}/\Theta_{\bar{c}}\mathbf{Z}
sending
\bar{\gamma}
to
\theta_{\bar{c}}(\bar{\gamma}\bar{x},\bar{x})
is an injective homomorphism, which does not depend on the point
\bar{x}\in\mathring{B}(\bar{c},\rho)
.

Comparison map.

Consider the action of
\mathchoice{\left\langle h\right\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}
on
\mathbf{R}
by translation of length
\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}
.
The quotient
\mathbf{R}/H
is isometric to a circle whose length is
n\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{D}
the cone of radius
\rho
over
\mathbf{R}/H
and by
o
its apex.
Observe that
\mathcal{D}
is a hyperbolic cone (i.e. locally isometric to
\mathbf{H}^{2}
everywhere except maybe at the cone point
o
) whose total angle at
o
is
\Theta_{\bar{c}}
.

Let
y_{0}
be an arbitrary point in
Y
.
One checks that the map
\varphi\colon Y\to\mathbf{R}
sending
y
to
b(y_{0},y)
is a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
-equivariant
(1,150\delta)
-quasi-isometric embedding, see Coulon
[
15
, Section 4.3]
.
Hence it induces a
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
-equivariant
(1,150\delta)
-quasi-isometric embedding

\bar{\varphi}\colon Z(Y/H)\to\mathcal{D}.

Note that
Z(Y/H)
, which is also isometric to
Z(Y)/H
, is endowed here with the metric defined by (
29
).
Although
Z(Y)/H
can be identified, as a set of points, with the closed ball of radius
\rho
centered at
\bar{c}
, the metric may be slightly different.
Nevertheless, in view of Propositions
A.2
and
A.7
the map
\bar{\varphi}\colon B(\bar{c},\rho)\to\mathcal{D}
induced by
\bar{\varphi}\colon Z(Y/H)\to\mathcal{D}
is a
(1,10\bar{\delta})
-quasi-isometric embedding.
Moreover, for every
\bar{x}\lx@nobreakspace\in B(\bar{c},\rho)
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}=\mathchoice{\left|o-\bar{\varphi}(\bar{x})\right|}{|o-\bar{\varphi}(\bar{x})|}{|o-\bar{\varphi}(\bar{x})|}{|o-\bar{\varphi}(\bar{x})|}
.
As a consequence we get the following statement.

Proposition A.12
.

For every
\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}\in\mathring{B}(\bar{c},\rho)
, we have
\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}-\ell\right|}{|\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}-\ell|}{|\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}-\ell|}{|\mathchoice{\left|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{x}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}-\ell|}\leqslant 10\bar{\delta}
, where

\operatorname{ch}\ell=\operatorname{ch}\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}\operatorname{ch}\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}-\operatorname{sh}\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}|}\operatorname{sh}\mathchoice{\left|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}\right|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}{|\bar{c}-\bar{x}^{\prime}|}\cos\left(\min\left\{\pi,\tilde{\theta}_{\bar{c}}(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\right\}\right)

and
\tilde{\theta}_{\bar{c}}(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
is the unique representative of
\theta_{\bar{c}}(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
in
(-\Theta_{\bar{c}}/2,\Theta_{\bar{c}}/2]
.
Moreover,
\theta_{\bar{c}}(\bar{\gamma}\bar{x},\bar{x})\lx@nobreakspace\neq 0
, for every
\bar{\gamma}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}\setminus\{1\}
, and
\bar{x}\in\mathring{B}(\bar{c},\rho)
.

Corollary A.13
.

Let
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
If
\bar{\gamma}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
is non-trivial, then there exists
q\in\mathbf{Z}
, such that
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}^{q},6\bar{\delta})
is contained in
B(\bar{c},8\bar{\delta})
.
In particular,
\bar{c}
is the unique cone point fixed by
\bar{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Since
\bar{\gamma}
is non-trivial, there exists
p\in\mathbf{Z}\setminus n\mathbf{Z}
such that
\bar{\gamma}=\bar{h}^{p}
.
As
n
is odd, the greatest common divisor
d
of
p
and
n
satisfies
1\leqslant d\leqslant n/3
.
On the other hand
\Theta_{\bar{c}}\geqslant 9\pi
, so that

\frac{\Theta_{\bar{c}}}{2}-\pi>\frac{\Theta_{\bar{c}}}{3}\geqslant\frac{d\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}.

Therefore, there exists
q,m\in\mathbf{Z}
such that

\pi\leqslant(qp+mn)\frac{\mathchoice{\left\|h\right\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}{\|h\|^{\infty}}}{\operatorname{sh}\rho}\leqslant\frac{\Theta_{\bar{c}}}{2}.

In other words
\bar{\gamma}^{q}
acts on
\mathcal{D}
as rotation whose angle is at least
\pi
.
By
Proposition A.12
,
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}^{q},6\bar{\delta})\cap B(\bar{c},\rho)
is contained in
B(\bar{c},8\bar{\delta})
.
However
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}^{q},6\bar{\delta})
is
8\bar{\delta}
-quasi-convex.
Thus it cannot contain a point outside
B(\bar{c},\rho)
without violating the previous conclusion.
Consequently
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}^{q},6\bar{\delta})
is contained in
B(\bar{c},8\bar{\delta})
.
∎

Corollary A.14
.

Let
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
\bar{\gamma}_{0}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}\setminus\{1\}
and
\bar{\gamma}\in\bar{\Gamma}
.
If
\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}
fixes
\bar{c}
, then so does
\bar{\gamma}
.

Proof.

For simplicity we write
\bar{\gamma}_{1}=\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}
so that
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
fixes
\bar{c}
and
\bar{\gamma}\bar{c}
.
However by
Corollary A.13
,
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
cannot fixed two distinct cone points hence
\bar{\gamma}\bar{c}=\bar{c}
.
∎

Proposition A.15
.

Let
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Every non-trivial element
\bar{\gamma}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
is
(\rho+\bar{\beta})
-thin at
\bar{c}
, where
\bar{\beta}=\alpha+\pi\operatorname{sh}(20\bar{\delta})
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\gamma}
be a non-trivial element of
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
.
In view of
Lemma 2.4
it suffices to prove that
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma},6\bar{\delta})
is contained in
B(\bar{c},\rho+\bar{\beta}-2\bar{\delta})
.
Let
\bar{x}\in\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma},6\bar{\delta})
.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
\bar{x}
does not belongs to
B(\bar{c},\rho)
.
Let
\bar{\sigma}\colon\mathchoice{\left[0\,,a\right]}{[0\,,a]}{[0\,,a]}{[0\,,a]}\to\bar{X}
be a
(1,\bar{\delta})
-quasi-geodesic from
\bar{c}
to
\bar{x}
.
Using the thinness of triangles we observe that
\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}\bar{\sigma}(t)-\bar{\sigma}(t)\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{\sigma}(t)-\bar{\sigma}(t)|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{\sigma}(t)-\bar{\sigma}(t)|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{\sigma}(t)-\bar{\sigma}(t)|}\leqslant 12\bar{\delta}
, for every
t\in\mathchoice{\left[0\,,a\right]}{[0\,,a]}{[0\,,a]}{[0\,,a]}
, see for instance
[
12
, Lemma 2.2(3)]
.
For simplicity we let
b=\rho/2
and
\bar{z}=\bar{\sigma}(b)
.
We write
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
for the path
\bar{\sigma}
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[b\,,a\right]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}
.
Let
(H,Y)
such that
\bar{c}
is the image of the apex
c
of
Z(Y)
.
We fix a pre-image
z\in Z(Y)
of
\bar{z}
.

Since
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
is a quasi-geodesic, it is contained in
\bar{X}\setminus B(\bar{c},\rho/2-\bar{\delta})
.
Observe also that
d(\bar{c}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}_{0})\geqslant\rho-6\bar{\delta}
for every
\bar{c}^{\prime}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{\bar{c}\}
.
Indeed
\bar{\gamma}
hardly moves the points on
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
.
By the triangle inequality, any other cone point closed to
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
should be fixed by
\bar{\gamma}
, which contradicts
Corollary A.13
.
We now apply
Lemma A.6
with the
12\bar{\delta}
-neighborhood of
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
as the set
\bar{Z}
.
In particular, there exists a
(1,\dot{\delta})
-quasi-geodesic
\sigma_{0}\colon\mathchoice{\left[b\,,a\right]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}\to\dot{X}
starting at
z
and lifting
\bar{\sigma}_{0}
and a pre-image
\gamma\in\Gamma
of
\bar{\gamma}
such that
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma\sigma_{0}(t)-\sigma_{0}(t)\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma\sigma_{0}(t)-\sigma_{0}(t)|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma\sigma_{0}(t)-\sigma_{0}(t)|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma\sigma_{0}(t)-\sigma_{0}(t)|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 12\dot{\delta}
, for every
t\in\mathchoice{\left[b\,,a\right]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}{[b\,,a]}
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma z-z\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 12\dot{\delta}
.
It follows from the triangle inequality that
\gamma
necessarily fixes
c
, thus is a non-trivial element from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}=\mathchoice{\left\langle h\right\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}{\langle h\rangle}
.
The point
x=\sigma_{0}(b)
is pre-image of
\bar{x}
.
We observed earlier that
d(\bar{x},\bar{\mathcal{C}})\geqslant\rho-6\bar{\delta}
.
Hence the radial projection
y
of
x
onto
X
satisfies
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-y|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 6\dot{\delta}
.
Using (
30
) we get

\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}\right)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma x-x|_{\dot{X}}}+2\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}\leqslant 24\dot{\delta}<2\rho.

Hence
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}\leqslant D
where
D=\pi\operatorname{sh}(12\dot{\delta})
, see for instance
[
12
, Proposition 4.4]
.
Since
\gamma
is
\alpha
-thin,
\mathchoice{\left\langle h^{-},h^{+}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle h^{-},h^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle h^{-},h^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle h^{-},h^{+}\rangle_{y}}\leqslant D/2+\alpha
.
Hence
y
lies in the
(D/2+\alpha)
-neighborhood of
Y
.
Recall that any point of
Y
is at a distance
\rho
from
c
in
\dot{X}
.
Since the maps
X\to\dot{X}
and
\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
are
1
-Lipschitz, we obtain with the triangle inequality that
\bar{x}
belongs to
B(\bar{c},\rho+\bar{\beta}-2\bar{\delta})
.
∎

Parabolic subgroups of
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Lemma A.16
.

The group
\bar{\Gamma}
has no parabolic subgroup.

Proof.

It follows from
[
13
, Proposition 5.25]
that every parabolic subgroup of
\bar{\Gamma}
is the image of a parabolic subgroup of
\Gamma
for its action on
X
.
However, according to our assumption, this action does not admit parabolic subgroups.
∎

Loxodromic subgroups of
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Lemma A.17

(
[
13
, Proposition 5.26]
)
.

Let
\bar{E}
be a loxodromic subgroup of
\bar{\Gamma}
.
There exists a loxodromic subgroup
E
of
\Gamma
(for its action on
X
) such that the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
E
onto
\bar{E}
.

Combining the previous statement with our assumptions, we get that every loxodromic subgroup of
\bar{\Gamma}
is cyclic.

Lemma A.18
.

Assume that there exists
\beta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that every loxodromic element of
\Gamma
is
\beta
-thin (for its action on
X
).
Then every loxodromic element of
\bar{\Gamma}
is
\bar{\beta}
-thin, where
\bar{\beta}=\beta+10\pi\operatorname{sh}(100\bar{\delta})
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\gamma}
be a loxodromic element of
\bar{\Gamma}
.
If
\mathchoice{\left\|\bar{\gamma}\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}>\bar{\delta}
, then
\bar{\gamma}
is
200\bar{\delta}
-thin, by
Remark 2.3
.
Therefore we can assume that
\mathchoice{\left\|\bar{\gamma}\right\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|^{\infty}}\leqslant\bar{\delta}
, hence
\mathchoice{\left\|\bar{\gamma}\right\|}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|}{\|\bar{\gamma}\|}\leqslant 10\bar{\delta}
.
For simplicity we let
\bar{Z}=\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma},100\bar{\delta})
.
In addition we denote by
\bar{\sigma}\colon\mathbf{R}\to\bar{X}
an
L
-local
(1,\bar{\delta})
-quasi-geodesic from
\gamma^{-}
to
\gamma^{+}
, with
L>12\bar{\delta}
.
The path
\bar{\sigma}
is contained in
\bar{Z}
, see for instance
[
15
, Lemma 2.9]
.
Compare also with
[
12
, Proposition 2.28]
.
According to
Lemma 2.4
, it suffices to prove that
\bar{Z}
lies in the
(\bar{\beta}-6\bar{\delta})
-neighborhood of
\bar{\sigma}
.

Being loxodromic
\bar{\gamma}
does not fix any apex in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
It follows from the triangle inequality that
d(\bar{c},\bar{Z})\geqslant\rho-50\bar{\delta}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
According to
Lemma A.6
, there exists a subset
Z
of
\dot{X}
such that the map
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
.
Moreover the projection
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{Z}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}
.
We denote by
\gamma\in\Gamma
the pre-image of
\bar{\gamma}
in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
.
Since
\bar{\gamma}
is loxodromic, so is
\gamma
.
It follows from the construction that
Z
is contained in
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,100\dot{\delta})
.

Let
\bar{z}\in\bar{Z}
and
z
its pre-image in
Z
.
Since
\bar{Z}
stays away from the cone points, the radial projection
y
of
z
is such that
\mathchoice{\left|y-z\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-z|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 50\dot{\delta}
.
It follows that

\mu\left(\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}\right)\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma y-y|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\gamma z-z\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}{|\gamma z-z|_{\dot{X}}}+100\dot{\delta}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}-\bar{z}\right|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}-\bar{z}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}-\bar{z}|}{|\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}-\bar{z}|}+100\bar{\delta}\leqslant 200\bar{\delta}<2\rho.

Thus
\mathchoice{\left|\gamma y-y\right|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}{|\gamma y-y|_{X}}\leqslant\pi\operatorname{sh}(100\bar{\delta})
.
Since
\gamma
is
\beta
-thin we get

\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\rangle_{y}}\leqslant\beta+D/2,\quad\text{where}\quad D=\pi\operatorname{sh}(100\bar{\delta}).

(the Gromov product is computed in
X
).
In particular, there exists
m_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every integer
m\geqslant m_{0}
, we have

\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}\leqslant\beta+D/2+3\delta,

Geodesic triangles in a hyperbolic space are thin.
We do not assumed here that
\dot{X}
is geodesic though.
Nevertheless it is a length space.
Hence there is a point
x\in\dot{X}
such that

\max\left\{\mathchoice{\left\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{x}},\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{x}},\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{x}}\right\}\leqslant 2\dot{\delta}.

where all Gromov products are computed in
\dot{X}
.

We claim that there exists a point
w\in X
such that
\mathchoice{\left|w-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|w-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|w-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|w-x|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 151\dot{\delta}
.
Combining the inequality above with the triangle inequality, we have indeed
\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{m}z,z\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}z,z\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}z,z\rangle_{x}}{\langle\gamma^{m}z,z\rangle_{x}}\leqslant 102\dot{\delta}
.
The set
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma,100\dot{\delta})
which contains
z
and
\gamma^{m}z
is
8\dot{\delta}
-quasi-convex.
Hence there exists
s\in\operatorname{Fix}(\dot{\gamma},100\dot{\delta})
such that
\mathchoice{\left|x-s\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-s|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-s|_{\dot{X}}}{|x-s|_{\dot{X}}}\leqslant 111\dot{\delta}
.
Note that
\gamma
cannot fix a cone point from
\mathcal{C}\subset\dot{X}
.
Consequently
s
necessarily lies in the
50\dot{\delta}
-neighborhood of
X
.
The radial projection
w
of
s
, satisfies our claim.
Applying the triangle inequality, we get

\max\left\{\mathchoice{\left\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\right\rangle_{w}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{w}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{w}}{\langle y,\gamma^{-m}y\rangle_{w}},\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{w}},\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\right\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{w}}{\langle\gamma^{m}y,y\rangle_{w}}\right\}\leqslant 153\dot{\delta}

(the Gromov products are still computed in
\dot{X}
).
It follows that the same Gromov products, computed in
X
this time, are bounded above by

d=\frac{\operatorname{sh}\rho}{\operatorname{sh}(\rho-157\dot{\delta})}\ \pi\operatorname{sh}(83\dot{\delta})

see
[
14
, Proposition 2.16]
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\left|y-w\right|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}+2d
where the Gromov product in computed in
X
.
Hence
\mathchoice{\left|y-w\right|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}\leqslant\beta+D/2+2d+3\delta
.

Using the triangle inequality, we get an upper bound for the following Gromov product computed in
\dot{X}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left|y-x\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-x|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-x|_{\dot{X}}}+2\dot{\delta}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left|y-w\right|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-w|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-w|_{\dot{X}}}{|y-w|_{\dot{X}}}+153\dot{\delta}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left|y-w\right|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}{|y-w|_{X}}+153\dot{\delta}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\beta+D/2+2d+154\dot{\delta}.

Applying one more time the triangle inequality we get (in
\dot{X}
)

\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}z,\gamma^{m}z\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}z,\gamma^{m}z\rangle_{z}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}z,\gamma^{m}z\rangle_{z}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}z,\gamma^{m}z\rangle_{z}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}{\langle\gamma^{-m}y,\gamma^{m}y\rangle_{y}}+150\dot{\delta}\leqslant\beta+D/2+2d+304\dot{\delta}.

Since
f\colon\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
, we get that for every
m\geqslant m_{0}
,

\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-m}\bar{z},\bar{\gamma}^{m}\bar{z}\right\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-m}\bar{z},\bar{\gamma}^{m}\bar{z}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-m}\bar{z},\bar{\gamma}^{m}\bar{z}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-m}\bar{z},\bar{\gamma}^{m}\bar{z}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}\leqslant\beta+D/2+2d+304\bar{\delta}.

Consequently

\mathchoice{\left\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-},\bar{\gamma}^{+}\right\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-},\bar{\gamma}^{+}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-},\bar{\gamma}^{+}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}{\langle\bar{\gamma}^{-},\bar{\gamma}^{+}\rangle_{\bar{z}}}\leqslant\beta+D/2+2d+304\bar{\delta}.

Hence
\bar{z}
lies in the
M
-neighborhood of
\bar{\sigma}
where

M=\beta+D/2+2d+311\bar{\delta}

Recall that we chose
\rho\geqslant\rho_{0}
with
\operatorname{th}(\rho_{0})\geqslant 1/2
.
It follows that
d\leqslant 4\pi\operatorname{sh}(83\dot{\delta})
.
Hence
\bar{z}
belongs to the
(\bar{\beta}-6\bar{\delta})
-neighborhood of
\bar{\sigma}
.
This holds for every
\bar{z}\in\bar{Z}
, which completes the proof.
∎

A.1.5
The action of
\bar{\Gamma}

In the previous section we investigated the isometries of
\bar{X}
.
We now briefly review some properties of the action of
\bar{\Gamma}
on
\bar{X}
.

Proposition A.19

(
[
13
, Proposition 5.15]
)
.

The action of
\bar{\Gamma}
on
\bar{X}
is non-elementary.

A careful analysis of the geometry of
\bar{X}
allows us to control the invariants
A(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X})
,
\nu(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X})
, and
\operatorname{inj}\left(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X}\right)
.

Proposition A.20

(
[
13
, Corollary 5.32]
)
.

Let
\ell
be the infimum over the stable translation lengths (measured in
X
) of loxodromic elements of
\Gamma
which do not belong to
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
for some
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Then
\operatorname{inj}\left(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X}\right)\geqslant\min\{\kappa\ell,\bar{\delta}\}
where
\kappa=\bar{\delta}/\pi\operatorname{sh}(26\bar{\delta})
.

Proposition A.21

(
[
13
, Proposition 5.28]
)
.

The
\nu
-invariant is
\nu(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X})=1
.

Proposition A.22
.

The invariant
A(\bar{\Gamma},\bar{X})
is at most

A(\Gamma,X)+5\pi\operatorname{sh}(1000\bar{\delta})
.

Proof.

Although the definition of
A(\Gamma,X)
is slightly different, the proof works verbatim as in
[
12
, Proposition 5.30]
.
See also
[
15
, Proposition 4.47]
.
∎

Recall that a group
G
is
commutative transitive
if it satisfies the following property: for every
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in G\setminus\{1\}
, if
[g_{1},g_{2}]=1
and
[g_{2},g_{3}]=1
, then
[g_{1},g_{3}]=1
.

Proposition A.23
.

If
\Gamma
is commutative transitive, then so is
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\gamma}_{1},\bar{\gamma}_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{3}\in\bar{\Gamma}\setminus\{1\}
such that
[\bar{\gamma}_{1},\bar{\gamma}_{2}]=[\bar{\gamma}_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{3}]=1
.
Suppose first that
\bar{\gamma}_{2}
is loxodromic.
Hence
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{3}
are contained in the maximal elementary subgroup containing
\bar{\gamma}_{2}
, which is cyclic by
Lemma A.17
. Hence
[\bar{\gamma}_{1},\bar{\gamma}_{3}]=1
.

Assume now that
\bar{\gamma}_{2}
fixes some cone point
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
It follows from
Corollary A.14
that
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{3}
fix
\bar{c}
as well.
As we observed earlier,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
is cyclic, hence
[\bar{\gamma}_{1},\bar{\gamma}_{3}]=1
.

Assume now that
\bar{\gamma}_{2}
is elliptic and does not fix any cone point.
We let
\bar{Z}=\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}_{2},6\bar{\delta})
.
Since
\bar{\gamma}_{2}
does not fix a cone point, it follows from the triangle inequality that
d(\bar{c},\bar{Z})\geqslant\rho-3\bar{\delta}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
By
Lemma A.6
, there is a subset
Z\subset\dot{X}
such that the map
\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
.
Note that
\bar{\gamma}_{i}
preserves
\bar{Z}
, for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
We denote by
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
their pre-images in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
.
It follows from
Lemma A.6
that
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]=[\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}]=1
.
Since
\Gamma
is commutative transitive, we have
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}]=1
and thus
[\bar{\gamma}_{1},\bar{\gamma}_{3}]=1
.
Recall that
\bar{\Gamma}
has no parabolic isometries (
Lemma A.16
), hence the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition A.24
.

If
\Gamma
is CSA, then so is
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

According to
Proposition A.23
,
\bar{\Gamma}
is commutative transitive.
Hence it suffices to prove that for every
\bar{\gamma},\bar{\gamma}_{0}\in\bar{\Gamma}\setminus\{1\}
if
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
and
\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}
commute, then so do
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
and
\bar{\gamma}
.
For simplicity we let
\bar{\gamma}_{1}=\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}
.
If
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
is loxodromic, then
\bar{\gamma}
belongs to the maximal elementary subgroup containing
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
, which is cyclic by
Lemma A.17
. Hence
[\bar{\gamma},\bar{\gamma}_{0}]=1
.

Suppose that
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
fixes some cone point
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Note that
\bar{\gamma}_{1}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{-1}=\bar{\gamma}_{0}
fixes
\bar{c}
.
Hence by
Corollary A.14
,
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
fixes
\bar{c}
.
Applying again
Corollary A.14
, we see that
\bar{\gamma}
fixes
\bar{c}
.
As we already observed earlier,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{c}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{c})}
is cyclic, hence
[\bar{\gamma},\bar{\gamma}_{0}]=1
.

We are left to handle the case where
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
is elliptic, but does not fix any cone point.
The group
\bar{\Gamma}
has no parabolic subgroup (
Lemma A.16
), while its loxodromic subgroups are cyclic (
Lemma A.17
).
Hence
\bar{U}=\{\bar{\gamma}_{0},\bar{\gamma}_{1}\}
generates an elliptic subgroup.
Thus we can find a point
\bar{z}\in\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{U},6\bar{\delta})
.
We let
\bar{Z}=\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\gamma}_{1},6\bar{\delta})
.
Note that
\bar{z}
and
\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}
belong to
\bar{Z}
.
Since
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
(and thus
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
) does not fix a cone point, it follows from the triangle inequality that
d(\bar{c},\bar{Z})\geqslant\rho-3\bar{\delta}
, for every
\bar{c}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
z\in\dot{X}
be a pre-image of
\bar{z}
.
By
Lemma A.6
, there is a subset
Z\subset\dot{X}
containing
z
such that the map
\dot{X}\to\bar{X}
induces an isometry from
Z
onto
\bar{Z}
.
Note that both
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{1}
preserves
\bar{Z}
.
We denote by
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
their respective pre-images in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
.
By
Lemma A.6

(C2)(T2)B
the projection
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\Gamma}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Z\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Z)}
onto
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(\bar{Z}\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}{\operatorname{Stab}(\bar{Z})}
, hence
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
commutes.
In addition, we fix
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
such that
\gamma z
is the pre-image in
Z
of
\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}
.
Note that
\bar{\gamma}_{1}\bar{\gamma}\bar{z}=\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\bar{z}
belongs to
\bar{Z}
.
By construction
\gamma_{1}\gamma z
is its pre-image in
Z
.
It follows from
Lemma A.6

(C2)(T2)A
applied with
\gamma
that
\gamma\gamma_{0}z=\gamma_{1}\gamma z
.
Recall that
\bar{\gamma}_{1}\bar{\gamma}=\bar{\gamma}\bar{\gamma}_{0}
.
Hence by
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)E
, we obtain
\gamma_{1}\gamma=\gamma\gamma_{0}
, that is
\gamma_{1}=\gamma\gamma_{0}\gamma^{-1}
.
Since
\Gamma
is CSA, it implies that
\gamma
commutes with
\gamma_{0}
.
Consequently
\bar{\gamma}
commutes with
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
.
∎

A.2
Approximation sequence of periodic group

Given a group
\Gamma
acting on a suitable hyperbolic space
X
we are going to use small cancellation theory to approximate the periodic quotients of
\Gamma
by a sequence of negatively curved groups.
For our applications the group
\Gamma
or the space
X
may vary.
In order to make all the dependencies clear we first define some auxiliary parameters.

A.2.1
The small cancellation parameters.

We start by defining the parameters involved in
Theorem 3.11
.
We write
\delta_{0}
,
\delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{0}
and
\rho_{0}
for the constants given by
Theorem A.3
.
For simplicity we let

A=10\pi\operatorname{sh}\left(10^{3}\delta_{1}\right),\quad\kappa=\frac{\delta_{1}}{\pi\operatorname{sh}(26\delta_{1})},\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha=20\pi\operatorname{sh}(100\delta_{1}).

We choose once for all a non-decreasing map
\rho\colon\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
which is bounded below by
\rho_{0}
, diverges to infinity, and satisfies

\rho(n)=o\left(\ln n\right).

We define a rescaling parameter by

\varepsilon(n)=\sqrt{\frac{10\pi\operatorname{sh}\rho(n)}{n\kappa\delta_{1}}}.

Observe that

\ln\varepsilon(n)=-\frac{1}{2}\ln n+\frac{1}{2}\rho(n)+O(1).

Thus,
\varepsilon(n)
and
\varepsilon(n)\rho(n)
converge to zero.
Let
\tau\in(0,1)
.
We set

\tau^{\prime}=\tau\min\{A,\delta_{1},\alpha\}=\tau\delta_{1}.

There exists an critical exponent
N_{\tau}\geqslant 100
, such that for every integer
n\geqslant N_{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n)

\displaystyle<1,

(32)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n)\delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{0},

(33)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n)(A+500\delta_{1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\min\left\{\Delta_{0},5\pi\operatorname{sh}\left(10^{3}\delta_{1}\right)\right\},

(34)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n)\kappa\delta_{1}

\displaystyle<\tau\delta_{1}\leqslant\min\{\delta_{1},\tau^{\prime}\}

(35)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n)(\rho(n)+\alpha)

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{1}

(36)

We now fix an odd integer
n\geqslant N_{\tau}
.
For simplicity we write
\rho=\rho(n)
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)
.
The parameter
\varepsilon
will serve as a rescaling factor for certain metric spaces.
Note that it has no connection with one used in
Section 7
though.

A.2.2
Approximation groups.

We fix a
\tau
-bootstrap
(\Gamma,X)
for the exponent
n
(see
Definition 3.9
).
We are going to define by induction a sequence of pairs

(\Gamma_{0},X_{0})\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{1},X_{1})\twoheadrightarrow\dots\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})\twoheadrightarrow\dots

(37)

where
\Gamma_{j}
is a group acting by isometries on a metric length space
X_{j}
.
Recall that the notation

(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})\twoheadrightarrow(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})

means that the pair
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
comes with a projection
\pi_{j}\colon\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}
together with a (non necessarily onto)
\pi_{j}
-equivariant map
f_{j}\colon X_{j}\to X_{j+1}
.
In addition this sequence will satisfy, among others, the following properties.

(R1)

X_{j}
is
\delta_{1}
-hyperbolic and the action of
\Gamma_{j}
is a non-elementary.

(R2)

Every elliptic element of
\Gamma_{j}
has finite order dividing
n
.
Every elementary subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
, which is not elliptic is loxodromic and cyclic.

(R3)

\nu(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})=1
,
\displaystyle A(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})\leqslant A
, and
\displaystyle\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma_{j},X_{j}\right)>\varepsilon\kappa\delta_{1}
.

(R4)

The map
f_{j}\colon X_{j}\to X_{j+1}
is
\varepsilon
-Lipschitz.

(R5)

The kernel of the map
\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}
is generated (as a normal subgroup) by all the elements of the form
\gamma^{n}
where
\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}
is loxodromic primitive and satisfies
\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}\leqslant 10\delta_{1}
.

The base of the induction.

We assumed that
(\Gamma,X)
is
\tau
-bootstrap.
Hence, we can rescale
X
to get a new metric space
X_{0}
which is
\delta_{1}
-hyperbolic and such that
A(\Gamma,X_{0})\leqslant A
while
\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma,X_{0}\right)\geqslant\tau^{\prime}
.
Moreover we can require that every element of
\Gamma
is
\alpha
-thin for its action on
X_{0}
.
Note that this rescaling does not depend on
n
.
We start by letting
(\Gamma_{0},X_{0})=(\Gamma,X)
.
It follows from the very definition of bootstrap and our choice of
N_{\tau}
that
(C2)(T2)(R1)
–
(C2)(T2)(R3)
holds.

The induction step.

Let
j\in\mathbf{N}
and assume that the pair
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})
satisfying
(C2)(T2)(R1)
,
(C2)(T2)(R2)
and
(C2)(T2)(R3)
has been previously defined.
The construction of
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
goes as follows.
Let
R_{j}
be the set of all loxodromic primitive elements
\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}
such that
\mathchoice{\left\|\gamma\right\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}{\|\gamma\|_{X_{j}}}\leqslant 10\delta_{1}
.
Let
K_{j}
be the (normal) subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
generated by
\mathchoice{\left\{\gamma^{n}\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\right\}}{\{\gamma^{n}\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}{\{\gamma^{n}\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}{\{\gamma^{n}\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}
.
The group
\Gamma_{j+1}
is defined as the quotient
\Gamma_{j+1}=\Gamma_{j}/K_{j}
.
In particular, the canonical projection
\pi_{j}\colon\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}
satisfies
(C2)(T2)(R5)
.

If
R_{j}
is empty, then
K_{j}
is trivial and
\Gamma_{j+1}=\Gamma_{j}
.
In this situation we choose for
X_{j+1}
the rescaled space
X_{j+1}=\varepsilon X_{j}
.
It satisfies
(C2)(T2)(R1)
-
(C2)(T2)(R4)
.

If
R_{j}
is not empty, then the space
X_{j+1}
is obtained by mean of small cancellation theory.
More precisely, we focus on the action of
\Gamma_{j}
on the rescaled space
\varepsilon X_{j}
.
According to (
33
) the space
\varepsilon X_{j}
is
\delta_{0}
-hyperbolic.
Let
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
be the family defined by

\mathcal{Q}_{j}=\mathchoice{\left\{\left(\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle},Y_{\gamma}\right)\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\right\}}{\{\left(\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle},Y_{\gamma}\right)\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}{\{\left(\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle},Y_{\gamma}\right)\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}{\{\left(\mathchoice{\left\langle\gamma^{n}\right\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle}{\langle\gamma^{n}\rangle},Y_{\gamma}\right)\;:\ \gamma\in R_{j}\}}

Combining (
34
) with our control of
A(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})
and
\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma_{j},X_{j}\right)
one can estimate the small cancellation parameters of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
.
More precisely we have the following statement.

Lemma A.25

(Compare with
[
13
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

The family
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
satisfies the following:
\Delta(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\varepsilon X_{j})\leqslant\Delta_{0}
and
T(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\varepsilon X_{j})\geqslant 10\pi\operatorname{sh}\rho
.

Let
\dot{X}_{j}
be the cone-off over the
rescaled
space
\varepsilon X_{j}
relative to
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
.
The space
X_{j+1}
is the quotient of
\dot{X}_{j}
by the normal subgroup
K_{j}
.
Recall that
\rho\geqslant\rho_{0}
.
It follows from the small cancellation theorem (
Theorem A.3
) that
X_{j+1}
is a
\delta_{1}
-hyperbolic length space.
The action of
\Gamma_{j+1}
is non-elementary (
Proposition A.19
).
Hence
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
satisfies
(C2)(T2)(R1)
.

The group
\Gamma_{j+1}
has no parabolic subgroup (
Lemma A.16
).
Hence its elementary subgroups which are not elliptic are loxodromic and cyclic (
Lemma A.17
).
Every elliptic subgroup of
\Gamma_{j+1}
is either the isomorphic image of an elliptic subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
or contained in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}/H
for some
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}
(
Lemma A.10
).
On the one hand, every elliptic element of
\Gamma_{j}
has finite order dividing
n
.
On the other hand,
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}/H
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
, for every
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}
.
Thus every elliptic element of
\Gamma_{j+1}
has finite order dividing
n
.
Consequently
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
satisfies
(C2)(T2)(R2)
.

Combining Proposition
A.22
and
A.20
combined with (
34
) and (
36
) we get the following statement corresponding to
(C2)(T2)(R3)
.

Lemma A.26

(Compare with
[
13
, Lemmas 6.3 and 6.4]
)
.

The pair
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
satisfies

A(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})\leqslant A\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{inj}\left(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1}\right)>\varepsilon\kappa\delta_{1}

Moreover
\nu(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})=1
.

Recall that the natural projection
\dot{X}_{j}\to X_{j+1}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
Consequently
f_{j}\colon X_{j}\to X_{j+1}
is
\varepsilon
-Lipschitz, which proves
(C2)(T2)(R4)
.
Hence the pair
(\Gamma_{j+1},X_{j+1})
satisfies all the requirements listed above.
This completes the induction step.

Cone points.

We extend the structure of the pairs
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})
built above by attaching to them a set of cone points.
Let
j\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Recall that
\dot{X}_{j-1}
stands for the cone-off over the rescaled space
\varepsilon X_{j-1}
.
In particular, it comes with a set of cone points corresponding to the apices of the attached cones.
We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{j}
its image in
X_{j}
.
By convention
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is empty.

A.2.3
Properties of the approximation sequence

In this section we prove that the approximation sequence
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
built above satisfies the conclusion of
Theorem 3.11
.
As we observed before, the space
X_{0}
is obtaining by rescaling
X
, where the rescaling factor does not depend on
n
.
Thus
Theorem 3.11

1
holds.
The next statement corresponds to the first half of
Theorem 3.11

2
.

Proposition A.27

(Compare with
[
13
, Theorem 6.9]
)
.

The direct limit of the sequence
\Gamma_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\dots
is isomorphic to
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.

Lemma A.28
.

If every finite subgroup of
\Gamma
is cyclic, then the same holds for
\Gamma_{j}
, for every
j\in\mathbf{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
j
.
It follows from our assumption if
j=0
.
Fix
j\in\mathbf{N}
and assume that every finite subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
is cyclic.
Let
E
be a finite subgroup of
\Gamma_{j+1}
.
Assume first that
E\subset\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j+1}
.
Since
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(c\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}{\operatorname{Stab}(c)}
is cyclic, so is
E
.
Assume now that some element of
E
does not fix a cone point.
It follows from
Lemma A.10
that
E
is isomorphic to a finite subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
.
Hence it is cyclic according to our induction hypotheses.
∎

Using the previous statement, we observe that if every finite subgroup of
\Gamma
is cyclic, then the same holds for
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
.
This proves
Theorem 3.11

2
.

We now focus on
Theorem 3.11

3
.
The goal is to prove that every triple
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
belongs to the class
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho(n))
given by
Definition 3.2
for a suitable value of
\delta
(which is independent of
n
and
j
).

Let
j\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
The cones attached to
\varepsilon X_{j-1}
to build the cone-off
\dot{X}_{j-1}
have radius
\rho
.
Consequently the set
\mathcal{C}_{j}
is
2\rho
-separated.
Recall the terminology from
Section 3
.
An element of
\Gamma_{j}
is
conical
, if it fixes a unique cone point.
It is
visible
, if it is loxodromic or conical, and
elusive
otherwise.
A subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
is
elusive
, if all its elements are elusive.

Note that
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
is CSA by the very definition of bootstrap (see
Definition 3.9
).
A proof by induction using
Proposition A.24
shows that
\Gamma_{j}
is CSA, for every
j\in\mathbf{N}
.

Lemma A.29

(Elementary subgroups)
.

For every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, every elementary subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
which is not elusive is cyclic.

Proof.

Let
j\in\mathbf{N}
and
E
be a non-elusive elementary subgroup of
\Gamma_{j}
.
In view of
(C2)(T2)(R2)
we can assume that
E
is elliptic.
Since
E
is not elusive, it fixes a unique cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
.
In particular,
j\geqslant 1
.
Moreover
c
is the image in
X_{j}
of the apex of the cone
Z(Y)
for some
(H,Y)\in\mathcal{Q}_{j-1}
.
It follows from
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)C
that
E
embeds in
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}/H
.
Nevertheless by
(C2)(T2)(R2)
every loxodromic subgroup of
\Gamma_{j-1}
is cyclic.
In particular, so is
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(Y\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}{\operatorname{Stab}(Y)}
and thus
E
.
∎

Recall that
\alpha
has been fixed with the other small cancellation parameters at the beginning of
Section A.2.1
.

Lemma A.30

(Loxodromic isometries)
.

For every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, every loxodromic element of
\Gamma_{j}
is
\alpha
-thin.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
j\in\mathbf{N}
.
According to our initial rescaling, every loxodromic element of
\Gamma_{0}
is
\alpha
-thin.
Assume now that the result holds for some
j\in\mathbf{N}
.
By (
36
) every loxodromic element of
\Gamma_{j}
is
\delta_{1}
-thin, for its action on the rescaled space
\varepsilon X_{j}
.
It follows from
Lemma A.18
, that every loxodromic element of
\Gamma_{j+1}
is
\alpha
-thin.
Hence the statement holds for
j+1
.
∎

Lemma A.31

(Elliptic isometries)
.

For every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, the following holds.

1

Every non-trivial elusive element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}
, is
\alpha
-thin.

2

Every non-trivial element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}
fixing a cone point
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
is
(\rho+\alpha)
-thin at
c
.

Proof.

The proof is again by induction on
j\in\mathbf{N}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is empty.
According to our initial rescaling, every elliptic element of
\Gamma_{0}
is
\alpha
-thin, whence the result.

Assume now that the result holds for some
j\in\mathbf{N}
.
According to
Lemma A.30
combined with (
36
) every loxodromic element of
\Gamma_{j}
is
\delta_{1}
-thin for its action on the rescaled space
\varepsilon X_{j}
.
On the other hand it follows from our induction hypothesis that every non-trivial elliptic element of
\Gamma_{j}
is
(\rho+\alpha)
-for its action on
X_{j}
.
Thus by (
36
) they are
\delta_{1}
-thin when acting on the rescaled space
\varepsilon X_{j}
.
Let
\gamma
be a non-trivial elliptic element of
\Gamma_{j+1}
.
If
\gamma
fixes a cone point in
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j+1}
, it follows from
Proposition A.15
that
\gamma
is that
(\rho+\alpha)
-thin at
c
.
Assume now that
\gamma
is elusive.
It follows from
Corollary A.13
that
\gamma
does not fix a cone point in
\mathcal{C}_{j+1}
.
Consequently,
\gamma
is
\alpha
-thin (
Lemma A.11
).
In particular, the statement holds for
j+1
.
∎

Proposition A.32
.

There is
\delta\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
which does not depend on
n
such that for every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, the approximation triple
(\Gamma_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})
of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{n}
belongs to
\mathfrak{H}_{\delta}(\rho(n))
.

Proof.

We set
\delta=\max\{A,10\delta_{1},\alpha\}
.
It follows from our induction that

\nu(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})=1\quad\text{and}\quad A(\Gamma_{j},X_{j})\leqslant A\leqslant\delta.

Hence Axiom
(H1)
holds.
Axiom
(H2)
follows from
Lemma A.29
.
Recall that
\alpha\leqslant\delta
.
Hence Lemmas
A.31
and
A.30
prove
(H3)
,
(H4)
, and
(H6)
.
Axiom
(H5)
is a reformulation of
Proposition A.12
.
We are left to prove Axiom
(H7)
.
Since
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is empty, we can assume that
j\geqslant 1
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
x\in X_{j}
such that
d(x,\mathcal{C}_{j})<\rho
.
Recall that the radial projection is a map
p_{j}\colon X_{j}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j}\to X_{j}^{+}
.
If
x\neq c
, one checks that
y=p_{j}(x)
satisfies
\mathchoice{\left|x-y\right|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}{|x-y|}=\rho-\mathchoice{\left|x-c\right|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}{|x-c|}
.
If
x=c
, then the radial projection
y
of any point in the punctured ball
\mathring{B}(c,\rho)
works.
∎

The previous statement corresponds to
Theorem 3.11

3
.
As we already observed for every
j\in\mathbf{N}
, the map
X_{j}\to X_{j+1}
is
\varepsilon(n)
-Lipschitz with
\varepsilon(n)<1
.
It follows from
Theorem A.3

(C2)(T2)E
that for every
x\in X_{j}
, the projection
\Gamma_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{j+1}
is one-to-one when restricted to set

\mathchoice{\left\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \varepsilon(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\right\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \varepsilon(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \varepsilon(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}}{\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{j}\;:\ \varepsilon(n)\mathchoice{\left|\gamma x-x\right|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}{|\gamma x-x|}\leqslant\frac{\rho(n)}{100}\}},

which proves
Theorem 3.11

4
.
We are left to prove
Theorem 3.11

5
.
This is a direct application of
Proposition A.5
.
Hence the proof of
Theorem 3.11
is complete.
